S_{\Lambda^{\mathrm{sc}},\Sigma^{\mathrm{sc}}_{0}}^{[\ell]}=(S_{\Lambda^{\mathrm{sc}},\Sigma^{\mathrm{sc}}_{0}}\cup C_{\Lambda^{\mathrm{sc}}})^{(\ell)}=\{\gamma\in\bar{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{sc}}:\|\bar{a}_{\gamma}\|_{\Sigma^{\mathrm{sc}}_{0}}=\ell\},\text{ etc.}

Example 3.4.7
.

In the even, quasisplit, ramified case of Example
3.3.7
(and the coordinates there),
X_{1}(A_{G^{\mathrm{sc}}})
is the
(m_{1},\dotsc,m_{n/2})\in 1/2\mathbb{Z}^{n}
such that
m_{1}
is an integer. Then
\Sigma^{\mathrm{sc}}
is
(1,0,\dotsc,0)
together with all elements of
i
-many
1/2
’s followed by
(n/2-i)
-many
0
’s for
1\leq i\leq n/2
. Then

\|(m_{1},\dotsc,m_{n})\|_{\Sigma^{\mathrm{sc}}}=(m_{1}-m_{2})+\left(\sum_{i=2}^{n/2}2(m_{i}-m_{i+1})\right)+2m_{n/2}=m_{1}+m_{2}

so
\Sigma^{\mathrm{sc}}
is navigable.

Fix
n=4
, and also consider
\Sigma^{\mathrm{sc}}_{0}=(1/2,1/2)
. Then
w_{\Sigma^{\mathrm{sc}}_{0}}((1,0))=2
, giving non-standard modified Cartan norm

\|(m_{1},m_{2})\|_{\Sigma^{\mathrm{sc}}_{0}}=2m_{1},

In particular,
\Sigma_{0}^{\mathrm{sc}}
is navigable.

4
Golden Adelic Subgroups and Gate Sets

In this section we introduce the notions of golden and super-golden adelic groups, use them to construct gate sets of
U(n)
, and prove that these gate sets satisfy the first three properties of Definition
1.2.1
: growth, navigation and approximation (Subsection
4.4
).

We end this section by giving examples of golden and super-golden groups for
n=4
(Subsection
4.5
), noting that all previous constructions comes from such golden and super-golden groups (
[
81
,
70
]
for
n=2
and
[
28
]
for
n=3
), and discussing practical considerations for their use in quantum computing (Subsection
4.6
).

4.1
Definition and First Properties

Let
G=U_{n}^{E,H}
,
SU_{n}^{E,H}
or
PU_{n}^{E,H}
.
Let
K^{\prime}=\prod_{\ell}K^{\prime}_{\ell}\leq G(\widehat{\mathcal{O}})
be a finite index subgroup.

Definition 4.1.1
.

The adelic subgroup,
K^{\prime}\leq G(\widehat{\mathcal{O}})
, is said to be
golden
if

(1)

G\left(\mathbb{A}\right)=G\left(F\right)\cdot G_{\infty}K^{\prime}
.

(2)

G\left(F\right)\cap K^{\prime}=\left\{1\right\}

We say it is
golden at
\mathfrak{p}
if
K^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}
is the stabilizer of a special vertex in the (enlarged) building
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
(in, this case,
K^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}=K_{\mathfrak{p}}
necessarily. By lemma
3.2.2
,
K^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}
is also often special parahoric). We say it is
\tau
-super-golden
at
\mathfrak{p}
if
K^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}
is the stabilizer of a facet
\tau\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
of dimension
>0
.

Finally, if only the weaker condition
G(F)\cap K^{\prime}\subseteq Z_{G}(F)
holds instead of (2), we call
K^{\prime}

essentially golden/
\tau
-super-golden
. If (2) doesn’t hold, we call
K^{\prime}

almost golden/
\tau
-super-golden
.

Beware that the first equation is difficult to satisfy—in particular, it requires
G
to have class number one. Remark
2.3.4
therefore makes golden adelic subgroups quite rare.

Given
K^{\prime}\leq G(\widehat{\mathcal{O}})
and prime
\mathfrak{p}
, define

\Gamma:=\Gamma^{K^{\prime}}:=G(F)\cap K^{\prime}\qquad\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}:=\Lambda^{K^{\prime}}_{\mathfrak{p}}:=G(F)\cap(K^{\prime})^{\mathfrak{p}}.

(4.1)

Lemma 4.1.2
.

If
K^{\prime}\leq G(\widehat{\mathcal{O}})
is an almost golden adelic
group, then:

(1)

We have an isomorphism of
G_{\infty}
-sets

\Gamma\backslash G_{\infty}\rightarrow G\left(F\right)\backslash G\left(\mathbb{A}\right)/K^{\prime},\qquad g\mapsto G\left(F\right)\left(g,1,1,\ldots\right)K^{\prime}

inducing the following isomorphism of
G_{\infty}
-representations

L^{2}\left(\Gamma\backslash G_{\infty}\right)\cong L^{2}\left(G\left(F\right)\backslash G\left(\mathbb{A}\right)\right)^{K^{\prime}}.

(2)

For primes
\mathfrak{p}
, we have an isomorphism

\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}\backslash G_{\infty}\times G_{\mathfrak{p}}/K^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}\rightarrow G\left(F\right)\backslash G\left(\mathbb{A}\right)/K^{\prime},\qquad(g_{\infty},g_{\mathfrak{p}})\mapsto G\left(F\right)\left(g_{\infty},g_{\mathfrak{p}},1,\dotsc,\right)K^{\prime}

inducing the following isomorphism preserving the
G_{\infty}
and
G_{\mathfrak{p}}/K^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}
Hecke algebra-actions:

L^{2}\left(\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}\backslash G_{\infty}\times G_{\mathfrak{p}}\right)^{K^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\cong L^{2}\left(G\left(F\right)\backslash G\left(\mathbb{A}\right)\right)^{K^{\prime}}.

Proof.

For both claims, surjectivity follows since
G(\mathbb{A})=G(F)G_{\infty}K^{\prime}
and injectivity since
G(F)\cap K^{\prime}=\Gamma
and
G(F)\cap(K^{\prime})^{\mathfrak{p}}=\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}
.
∎

Lemma 4.1.3
.

Let
K^{\prime}\leq G(\widehat{\mathcal{O}})
be a almost golden adelic subgroup and let
\mathfrak{p}
be a prime. If
K^{\prime}
is golden at
\mathfrak{p}
(resp.
\tau
-super-golden), then:

(1)

\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}
acts transitively on
G_{\mathfrak{p}}/K^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}
with stabilizer
\Gamma

(2)

\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}/(Z_{G_{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\mathrm{spl}}\cap\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}})
acts transitively on
G_{\mathfrak{p}}v_{0}
(resp.
G_{\mathfrak{p}}\tau
) with stabilizer
\Gamma/(Z_{G_{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\mathrm{spl}}\cap\Gamma)
.

(3)

\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}\cap Z^{\mathrm{spl}}_{G_{\mathfrak{p}}}
acts simply transitively on the fibers of
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}\to\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}/(Z_{G_{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\mathrm{spl}}\cap\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}})
.

In particular, when
K^{\prime}
is golden, the above actions are all simple transitive.

Proof.

Since
K^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}
is a stabilizer of
v_{0}
or
\tau
, respectively, (1) follows from the set equalities

G_{\mathfrak{p}}=\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}\cdot K^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}\cap K^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}=\Gamma,

which in turn follow from the assumption on the group
K^{\prime}
(similarly to lemma
2.2.4
). (2) follows since
\tau
has stabilizer exactly
K^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}Z^{\mathrm{spl}}_{G_{\mathfrak{p}}}
and (3) is automatic. ∎

4.2
Golden Gate Sets

We now discuss how to construct gate sets from golden and super-golden adelic subgroups.

4.2.1
Golden Case

Definition 4.2.1
.

Let
K^{\prime}
be an almost golden arithmetic subgroup of
U_{n}^{E,H}
that is golden at
\mathfrak{p}
. Then define the gate set and lifted gate set

S_{\mathfrak{p}}:=S^{K^{\prime}}_{\mathfrak{p}}:=S_{\Lambda^{K^{\prime}}_{\mathfrak{p}}},\qquad\widetilde{S}_{\mathfrak{p}}:=\widetilde{S}^{K^{\prime}}_{\mathfrak{p}}:=\widetilde{S}_{\Lambda^{K^{\prime}}_{\mathfrak{p}}}

and analogous sets coset representatives
{}^{0}\!S_{\mathfrak{p}},{}^{0}\!\widetilde{S}_{\mathfrak{p}}
as in Definitions (
4.1
),
3.4.1
. Note that when
K
is golden, we have
{}^{0}\!S_{\Lambda}=S_{\Lambda}
and
{}^{0}\!\widetilde{S}_{\Lambda}=\widetilde{S}_{\Lambda}
.

We may also define non-standard and decimated versions following Remark
3.4.4
or Definition
3.4.5
respectively.

Proposition
3.4.2
immediately gives that

•

{}^{0}\!S_{\mathfrak{p}}\cup\Gamma
generates
\bar{\Lambda}_{\mathfrak{p}}
,

•

S_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}=(S_{\mathfrak{p}}\cup\Gamma)^{(\ell)}=\{\gamma\in\bar{\Lambda}_{\mathfrak{p}}\;:\;\|a_{\gamma}\|_{G_{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\prime}=\ell\}
.

Example 4.2.2
.

If
\mathfrak{p}_{\mathrm{spl}}
is split, then by Example
3.3.4
,
\|\cdot\|_{G_{\mathfrak{p}_{\mathrm{spl}}}}
is the graph distance on the
1
-skeleton of
\mathcal{B}
so

S_{\mathfrak{p}_{\mathrm{spl}}}=\{\gamma\in\bar{\Lambda}_{\mathfrak{p}_{\mathrm{spl}}}\;:\;\dist(\gamma.v_{0},v_{0})=1\}.

If
\mathfrak{p}_{\mathrm{ns}}
in non-split unramified and
H_{\mathfrak{p}_{\mathrm{ns}}}
is also unramified, then by Example
3.3.5
,
\|\cdot\|_{G_{\mathfrak{p}_{\mathrm{ns}}}}
is half the graph distance on the
1
-skeleton of
\mathcal{B}
. In addition,
Z^{\mathrm{spl}}_{G_{\mathfrak{p}_{\mathrm{ns}}}}=1
so we can ignore center technicalities. Therefore,

S_{\mathfrak{p}_{\mathrm{ns}}}=\widetilde{S}_{\mathfrak{p}_{\mathrm{ns}}}=\{\gamma\in\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}_{\mathrm{ns}}}\;:\;\dist(\gamma.v_{0},v_{0})=2\}.

4.2.2
Super-golden Case

In the
\tau
-super-golden case, we need to first restrict our choice of
\tau
:

Definition 4.2.3
.

Let
\tau\subseteq\mathcal{B}
contain a special vertex
x_{0}
. For each
x\in Gx_{0}
, define
(G\tau)_{x}\subseteq G\tau
to be the cosets containing
g
such that
gx_{0}=x
.

Let
\Sigma_{\tau}\subseteq\Sigma
be
\lambda
such that for each (equiv. any)
x\in Gx_{0}
such that
\|x-x_{0}\|=\lambda
, there is
\tau_{0,x}\in(G\tau)_{x_{0}}
,
\tau_{1,x}\in(G\tau)_{x}
, and
r_{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
such that any automorphism
\varphi
of
\mathcal{B}
such that
\varphi(\tau_{0,x})=\tau_{1,x}
satisfies
\varphi^{r_{x}}(\tau_{0,x})=\tau_{0,x}
.

We say that
\tau
is
traversable
if
\Sigma_{\tau}
is Weyl-complete (as in
3.3.10
) and navigable (as in
3.3.12
). If
\Sigma_{\tau}=\Sigma
, we say that
\tau
is
standard traversable
. Otherwise, it is
non-standard traversable
. If all the
\tau_{0,x}
are
C_{\mathfrak{p}}
translates and each
\tau_{1,x}
is an automorphism of the corresponding
\tau_{0,x}
, we say that
\tau
is
simply traversable
.

Finally if
\Sigma^{\mathrm{sc}}\neq\Sigma
, we define analogous notions of
decimated traversable
with respect to the analogous
\Sigma_{\tau}\subseteq\Sigma^{\mathrm{sc}}
.

In this case, there is a particularly nice structure we can give
C_{\mathfrak{p}}
and
{}^{0}\!S_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

Definition 4.2.4
.

Let
K^{\prime}
be a golden arithmetic subgroup of
U_{n}^{E,H}
that is
\tau
-super-golden at
\mathfrak{p}
for
\tau
traversable. Then, following Definitions (
4.1
),
3.4.1
and Remark
3.4.4
, set

C_{\mathfrak{p}}:=C^{K^{\prime}}_{\mathfrak{p}}:=C_{\Lambda^{K^{\prime}}_{\mathfrak{p}}}=\overline{\Gamma},\qquad\widetilde{C}_{\mathfrak{p}}:=\widetilde{C}^{K^{\prime}}_{\mathfrak{p}}:=\widetilde{C}_{\Lambda^{K^{\prime}}_{\mathfrak{p}}}=\Gamma.

and

S_{\mathfrak{p}}:=S^{K^{\prime}}_{\mathfrak{p}}:=S_{\Lambda^{K^{\prime}}_{\mathfrak{p}},\Sigma_{\tau}},\qquad\widetilde{S}_{\mathfrak{p}}:=\widetilde{S}^{K^{\prime}}_{\mathfrak{p}}:=\widetilde{S}_{\Lambda^{K^{\prime}}_{\mathfrak{p}},\Sigma_{\tau}}.

In the decimated traversable case, make analogous definitions following
3.4.5
.

Fix a set of representatives
v_{i}
of the orbits
C_{\mathfrak{p}}\backslash\{v\in Gv_{0}\;:\;\|v-v_{0}\|_{G_{\mathfrak{p}}}=1\}
and for each
i
, fix choices of
\tau_{0,v_{i}}
and
\tau_{1,v_{i}}
as in Definition
4.2.3
. Since
C_{\mathfrak{p}}
acts transitively on
(G\tau)_{v_{0}}
, we may without loss of generality choose the
v_{i}
so that we may choose all
\tau_{0,v_{i}}=\tau
. Then define:

\displaystyle T_{\mathfrak{p}}:=T^{K^{\prime}}_{\mathfrak{p}}:=\{\gamma\in S_{\mathfrak{p}}\;:\;\gamma\tau=\tau_{1,v_{i}}\text{ for some }i\}

\displaystyle\widetilde{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}:=\widetilde{T}^{K^{\prime}}_{\mathfrak{p}}:=\{\gamma\in\widetilde{S}_{\mathfrak{p}}\;:\;\gamma\tau=\tau_{1,v_{i}}\text{ for some }i\}

Choose representatives
{}^{0}\!T_{\mathfrak{p}}
for the cosets
T_{\mathfrak{p}}/C_{\mathfrak{p}}
and
{}^{0}\!\widetilde{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
for
\widetilde{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}/\widetilde{C}_{\mathfrak{p}}
such that each
\widetilde{t}_{i}\in{}^{0}\!\widetilde{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
lifts a
t_{i}\in{}^{0}\!T_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

In the golden case,
{}^{0}\!T_{\mathfrak{p}}=T_{\mathfrak{p}}
and
{}^{0}\!\widetilde{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}=\widetilde{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
. As some quick properties:

Lemma 4.2.5
.

The following hold

(1)

C_{\mathfrak{p}}
is a finite group.

(2)

For each
v_{i}
, there is exactly one
t_{i}\in{}^{0}\!T_{\mathfrak{p}}
with
t_{i}\tau=\tau_{1,v_{i}}
by lemma
4.1.3
.

(3)

For each
t_{i}\in T_{\mathfrak{p}}
,
t_{i}^{r_{v_{i}}}\in\Gamma
. In particular,
t_{i}
has finite order that divides
r_{v_{i}}
when
K^{\prime}
is golden.

(4)

S_{\mathfrak{p}}=\{c_{2}tc_{2}^{-1}c_{1}\;:\;c_{1},c_{2}\in C_{\mathfrak{p}},\;t\in{}^{0}\!T_{\mathfrak{p}}\}
(note here that
c_{2}tc_{2}^{-1}c_{1}=c_{1}^{\prime}c_{2}tc_{2}^{-1}
for
c^{\prime}_{1}
in the stabilizer of
c_{2}tc_{2}^{-1}v_{0}
). In particular,
S_{\mathfrak{p}}\cup C_{\mathfrak{p}}
is generated by finite-order elements.

(5)

\widetilde{S}_{\mathfrak{p}}=\{c_{2}\widetilde{t}c_{2}^{-1}c_{1}\;:\;c_{1},c_{2}\in\widetilde{C}_{\mathfrak{p}},\;\widetilde{t}\in{}^{0}\!\widetilde{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}\}
.

We can therefore make choices of coset representatives:

{}^{0}\!S_{\mathfrak{p}}\subseteq\{ctc^{-1}\;:\;c\in C_{\mathfrak{p}},t\in{}^{0}\!T_{\mathfrak{p}}\},\qquad{}^{0}\!\widetilde{S}_{\mathfrak{p}}\subseteq\{c\widetilde{t}c^{-1}\;:\;c\in C_{\mathfrak{p}},t\in{}^{0}\!T_{\mathfrak{p}}\}.

Note that these are both exact equalities in the golden case. Either way, Proposition
3.4.2
and Remark
3.4.4
then give that

•

{}^{0}\!S_{\mathfrak{p}}\cup C_{\mathfrak{p}}
generates
\bar{\Lambda}_{\mathfrak{p}}
,

•

S_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}:={}^{0}\!S_{\mathfrak{p}}^{(\ell)}C_{\mathfrak{p}}=(S_{\mathfrak{p}}\cup\overline{\Gamma})^{(\ell)}=\{\gamma\in\bar{\Lambda}_{\mathfrak{p}}\;:\;\|a_{\gamma}\|_{\Sigma_{0}}^{\prime}=\ell\}
.

Example 4.2.6
.

Here are some traversable
\tau
and the corresponding
C_{\mathfrak{p}}
and
T_{\mathfrak{p}}
. All examples are simply traversable except (2).

(1)

G=\mathrm{GL}_{n}
and
\tau\ni x_{0}
a chamber in
\mathcal{B}_{G}
:
\Sigma_{\tau}=\Sigma
, the
\tau_{0,x}
range over
\Omega_{x_{0}}
-translates of
\tau
, and the
\tau_{1,x}
are rotations of
\tau_{0,x}
.

Then
C_{\mathfrak{p}}
acts transitively on the chambers containing
x_{0}
. The
t_{i}
are all of the form
r^{i}
, where
r
has order
n
and acts on
\tau
as an
n
-cycle on its vertices.

Note that the
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
-case is what was used in
[
70
]
where
\mathcal{B}
is a tree and there is a single
t_{i}
of order two that flips an edge.

(2)

G
a quasisplit, unramified unitary group and
\tau\ni x_{0}
a chamber in
\mathcal{B}_{G}
:
\Sigma_{\tau}=\Sigma
, the
\tau_{0,x}
range over Weyl translates of
\tau
, and the
\tau_{1,x}
range over
\Omega_{y}
-translates of
\tau_{0,x}
where
y
is another vertex of
\tau_{0,x}
.

Then
C_{\mathfrak{p}}
acts transitively on the chambers containing
x_{0}
. There are many different
t_{i}
which are complicated to describe.

(3)

G
a quasisplit, unramified
U_{4}
and
\tau\ni x_{0}
a two-edge path connecting
x_{0}
to a hyperspecial vertex through a non-special vertex:
\Sigma_{\tau}
is the
\Sigma_{0}
from Example
3.3.15
, the
\tau_{0,x}
range over
\Omega_{x_{0}}
-translates of
\tau
and the
\tau_{1,x}
are reflections of
\tau_{0,x}
across the middle non-special vertex.

Then
C_{\mathfrak{p}}
acts transitively on the hyperspecial vertices at graph-distance two from
x_{0}
through a non-special vertex. There is one
t_{i}
whose square is in
C_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

(4)

G
a quasisplit, ramified
U_{4}
and
\tau\ni x_{0}
an edge connecting
x_{0}
to a special vertex:
\Sigma_{\tau}
is the
\Sigma_{0}
from Example
3.3.16
, the
\tau_{0,x}
range over
\Omega_{x_{0}}
-translates of
\tau
and the
\tau_{1,x}
are reflections of
\tau_{0,x}
across the middle.

Then
C_{\mathfrak{p}}
acts transitively on special vertices at graph-distance one from
x_{0}
. There is one
t_{i}
whose square is in
C_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

(5)

G
a quasisplit, ramified
U_{4}
and
\tau\ni x_{0}
a two-edge path connecting
x_{0}
to a special vertex through a non-special vertex:
\Sigma^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\tau}
is {(1,0)} as in Example
3.4.7
, and the corresponding super-golden gate set behaves similarly to (3) except for being decimated.

(6)

G
a non-quasisplit
U_{4}
and
\tau
the union of two edges connected at a vertex not in
Gx_{0}
:
\Sigma_{\tau}=\Sigma
, the
\tau_{0,x}
range over
(G\tau)_{x_{0}}
, and the
\tau_{1,x}
are reflections of the
\tau_{0,x}
across their middles.

Then
C_{\mathfrak{p}}
acts transitively on the distance-
2
vertices from
x_{0}
and there is only one
t_{i}
whose square is in
C_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

4.3
Some Useful Computations

4.3.1
Finding Gates

Fix
K^{\prime}
an almost golden arithmetic subgroup of
U_{n}^{E,H}
. We explain some cases of how to find the gate sets
S_{\mathfrak{p}}
at each place
\mathfrak{p}
where
K^{\prime}
is golden or
\tau
-super-golden for
\tau
traversable. The various sub-pieces
{}^{0}\!S_{\mathfrak{p}}
,
C_{\mathfrak{p}}
,
T_{\mathfrak{p}}
, etc. can be easily constructed from the full set
S_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

Example 4.3.1
.

Assume
\mathfrak{p}|\mathfrak{q}
is unramified and
H
is unramified. The
G_{\mathfrak{p}}=U^{E_{\mathfrak{q}},H_{\mathfrak{p}}}(F_{\mathfrak{p}})
and the gates
S_{\mathfrak{p}}
are exactly the
g\in K^{\prime}\cap U^{E,H}(F)
such that the modified Cartan norm
\|a_{g_{\mathfrak{p}}}\|=1
. The
a_{g_{\mathfrak{p}}}
can be computed by the
U^{E,H}(F_{\mathfrak{p}})
- or equivalently
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(E_{\mathfrak{q}})
-Cartan decomposition and
\|a_{g_{\mathfrak{p}}}\|=1
is equivalent to largest entry of
a_{g_{\mathfrak{p}}}
being
1
as in Example
3.3.5
.

By the standard algorithm for computing Cartan decompositions for
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
,
\|a_{g_{\mathfrak{p}}}\|=1
is therefore equivalent to

g\in\pi_{\mathfrak{q}}^{-1}\Mat_{n\times n}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}})\setminus\Mat_{n\times n}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}),

where
\pi_{\mathfrak{q}}
is a uniformizer for
E_{\mathfrak{q}}
(equiv.
F_{\mathfrak{p}}
). Assume further that the scalar matrix
\pi_{\mathfrak{q}}\in(K^{\prime})^{v}
(e.g, without loss of generality if
E
has class number one and
K^{\prime}
is a principal congruence subgroup). Then scaling by
\pi_{\mathfrak{q}}
gives:

S_{\mathfrak{p}}=\{\pi_{\mathfrak{q}}^{-1}g\subseteq\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}\;:\;g^{*}Hg=\pi_{\mathfrak{p}}^{2}H,\,g\in\Mat_{n\times n}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}})\times(K^{\prime})^{\mathfrak{p}},\,g\notin\pi_{\mathfrak{q}}K^{\prime}\},

where without loss of generality
\pi_{\mathfrak{p}}=\pi_{\mathfrak{q}}
is a uniformizer for
F_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

Example 4.3.2
.

Assume
\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{p}_{1}\mathfrak{p}_{2}
is split and
K^{\prime}=U^{E,H}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}})
is golden at
v
. Then
G_{\mathfrak{p}}=\mathrm{GL}_{n}(E_{\mathfrak{p}_{1}})
. Fix lifts
\widetilde{\Sigma}
to have smallest coordinate
0
. Then the gates
S_{\mathfrak{p}}
are exactly the
g\in K^{\prime}\cap U^{E,H}(F)
such that the modified Cartan norm
\|a_{g_{\mathfrak{p}_{1}}}\|=1
. By Example
3.3.4
, these are also exactly the
g
with
a_{g_{\mathfrak{p}_{1}}}
having largest coordinate
1
and smallest
0
.

Again using the standard algorithm for Cartan decompositions for
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
,
\|a_{g_{\mathfrak{p}_{1}}}\|=1
is then equivalent to

g\in\pi_{\mathfrak{p}_{1}}^{-1}\Mat_{n\times n}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}_{1}})\setminus\Mat_{n\times n}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}_{1}})\cup\pi_{\mathfrak{p}_{1}}^{-1}\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}_{1}}),

where
\pi_{\mathfrak{p}_{1}}
is a uniformizer for
E_{\mathfrak{p}_{1}}
. If we similarly assume further that
\pi_{\mathfrak{p}_{1}}\in(K^{\prime})^{\mathfrak{p}}
, then we get

S_{\mathfrak{p}}=\{\pi_{\mathfrak{p}_{1}}^{-1}g\subseteq\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}\;:\;g^{*}Hg=\pi_{\mathfrak{p}}H,\,g\in\Mat_{n\times n}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}})\times(K^{\prime})^{\mathfrak{p}},\,g\notin\pi_{\mathfrak{p}_{1}}K^{\prime}\cup\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}_{1}})\},

where
\pi_{\mathfrak{p}}=\pi_{\mathfrak{p}_{1}}\pi_{\mathfrak{p}_{1}}^{*}
is a uniformizer for
F_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

Example 4.3.3
.

The other cases when
\mathfrak{p}|\mathfrak{q}
is non-split work similarly to Example
4.3.1
except inputting the computations of modified Cartan norms from Examples
3.3.7
,
3.3.8
, and
3.3.9
. We get

S_{\mathfrak{p}}=\{\pi_{\mathfrak{q}}^{-1}g\subseteq\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}\;:\;g^{*}Hg=\pi_{\mathfrak{p}}^{e}H,\,g\in\Mat_{n\times n}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}})\times(K^{\prime})^{\mathfrak{p}},\,g\notin\pi_{\mathfrak{q}}K^{\prime}\},

where

e=\begin{cases}2&E_{\mathfrak{q}}/F_{\mathfrak{p}}\text{ unramified}\\
1&E_{\mathfrak{q}}/F_{\mathfrak{p}}\text{ ramified}\end{cases}.

Example 4.3.4
.

The non-standard gates of Examples
3.3.15
and
3.3.16
can be selected from the corresponding standard gates of Example
4.3.3
as those where the second coordinate of their Cartan invariant is
0
.

Example 4.3.5
.

When
n
is even and
\mathfrak{p}|\mathfrak{q}
is ramified, the gates in the standard decimated case of Example
3.4.7
are the (proper) subset:

S_{\mathfrak{p}}\subsetneq\{\pi_{\mathfrak{q}}^{-2}g\subseteq\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}\;:\;g^{*}Hg=\pi^{2}_{\mathfrak{p}}H,\,g\in\Mat_{n\times n}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}})\times(K^{\prime})^{\mathfrak{p}},\,g\notin\pi_{\mathfrak{q}}K^{\prime}\}

of the
s
with Cartan invariants having first two coordinates
(1/2,1/2,\dotsc)
or
(1,0,\dotsc)
.

If
n=4
, in the non-standard decimated case we instead get (proper) subset

S_{\mathfrak{p}}\subsetneq\{\pi_{\mathfrak{q}}^{-1}g\subseteq\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}\;:\;g^{*}Hg=\pi^{2}_{\mathfrak{p}}H,\,g\in\Mat_{4\times 4}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}})\times(K^{\prime})^{\mathfrak{p}},\,g\notin\pi_{\mathfrak{q}}K^{\prime}\}

of the
s
with Cartan invariant
(1/2,1/2)
.

4.3.2
Sizes and Growth Rates

To understand the sizes of gate sets
{}^{0}\!S_{\mathfrak{p}}
and
S^{[\ell]}_{\mathfrak{p}}
, we recall the following well-known fact about buildings:

Proposition 4.3.6
.

The degree
\deg(v_{0})
of a hyperspecial vertex in the
1
-skeleton of the building
\mathcal{B}
for group
H/\mathbb{F}
is the number of maximal proper parabolics in
H(k_{\mathbb{F}})
for the corresponding integral model.

Proposition 4.3.7

(
[
19
]
*Prop. 1.5.2)
.

Let
v_{0}
be a special vertex of
\mathcal{B}
and
\lambda\in X_{1+}(\bar{A}_{G})
. Let
B_{\lambda}
be the number of points
v\in\mathcal{B}
such that
v-v_{0}=\lambda\in X_{+}(\bar{A}_{G})
. Then

C_{1}q_{\mathbb{F}}^{\langle\lambda,2\rho_{G}\rangle}\leq B_{\lambda}\leq C_{2}q_{\mathbb{F}}^{\langle\lambda,2\rho_{G}\rangle}

for some constants
C_{1},C_{2}
depending on
H
and where
\rho_{G}
is the half-sum of positive roots.

Proof.

Let
M
be the centralizer of
A_{G}
and
m\in M
such that
a_{m}=\lambda
. Let
\widetilde{K}
be the stabilizer of
v_{0}
. Then by the Cartan decomposition,
B_{\lambda}=|\widetilde{K}m\widetilde{K}/\widetilde{K}|
.

Let
P
be a parabolic for
M
and
N
the corresponding unipotent with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{b}
. We now apply
[
19
]
*Prop. 1.5.2:
|\det\Ad_{\mathfrak{n}}m|=\langle\lambda,2\rho_{G}\rangle
so the result follows.
∎

Corollary 4.3.8
.

Let
K^{\prime}<G^{\infty}
be almost golden at
\mathfrak{p}
(resp. almost
\tau
-super-golden for
\tau
standard traversable) and
S_{\mathfrak{p}}
the corresponding gate set. Let
M=\max_{\lambda\in\Sigma}\langle\lambda,2\rho_{G_{\mathfrak{p}}}\rangle
and
k
the number of
\lambda\in\Sigma
realizing this maximum. Then
|S_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}|\asymp\ell^{k-1}q_{\mathfrak{p}}^{M\ell}
.

Proof.

By lemma
4.1.3
and the versions of Proposition
3.4.2
as used above,

|S_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}|=|C_{\mathfrak{p}}||\{v\in G_{\mathfrak{p}}v_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{B}\;:\;\|v-v_{0}\|=\ell\}|

By Proposition
4.3.7
, if we let
\lambda_{0}
realize the maximum value of
\langle\lambda,2\rho_{G}\rangle
, this gives

|S_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}|\asymp\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in X_{+}(\bar{A}_{G_{\mathfrak{p}}})\\
\|\lambda\|=\ell\end{subarray}}q_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\langle\lambda,2\rho_{G}\rangle}\asymp\binom{\ell+k-1}{k-1}q_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\langle\ell\lambda_{0},2\rho_{G}\rangle},

where the last line is by counting how many terms in the sum are equal to
q_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\langle\ell\lambda_{0},2\rho_{G}\rangle}
and noting that the number of terms equal to
q_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\langle\ell\lambda_{0},2\rho_{G}\rangle-c}
is larger by a factor that is most polynomial in
c
and that
\sum_{c=0}^{\infty}c^{n}q_{\mathfrak{p}}^{-c}
converges for all
n
.
∎

Corollary 4.3.9
.

Let
K^{\prime}<G^{\infty}
be almost
\tau
-super-golden at
\mathfrak{p}
for
\tau
non-standard traversable. Let
M=\max_{\lambda\in\Sigma}w_{\Sigma_{\tau}}(\lambda)^{-1}\langle\alpha,2\rho_{G_{\mathfrak{p}}}\rangle
recalling the definition of
w_{\Sigma_{\tau}}
from
3.3.11
. Let
k
be the number of
\lambda\in\Sigma
realizing this maximum. Then
|S_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}|\asymp\ell^{k-1}q_{\mathfrak{p}}^{M\ell}
.

Proof.

This is the same argument as lemma
4.3.8
.
∎

Example 4.3.10
.

If
\mathfrak{p}
is split then
G_{\mathfrak{p}}=GL_{n}/F_{\mathfrak{p}}
. If
K^{\prime}
is almost golden at
\mathfrak{p}
, then
|S_{\mathfrak{p}}|=\deg(v_{0})
. This is the sum of the sizes of the Grassmanians
G(n,k)
for
1\leq k\leq n-1
over the residue field of
F_{\mathfrak{p}}
giving

|{}^{0}\!S_{\mathfrak{p}}|=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}{\binom{n}{k}}_{q_{\mathfrak{p}}}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\frac{(1-q_{\mathfrak{p}}^{n})\cdots(1-q_{\mathfrak{p}}^{n-k+1})}{(1-q_{\mathfrak{p}})\cdots(1-q_{\mathfrak{p}}^{k})}=(1+\mathbf{1}_{n\text{ odd}})q_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\lfloor n^{2}/4\rfloor}+O(q_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\lfloor n^{2}/4\rfloor-1}).

In comparison,
2\rho_{G}=(n-1,n-3,\dotsc,-n+1)
, which has maximized pairing with the middle fundamental weight in
\Sigma
(there are
2
if
n
is odd). This gives

|S^{[\ell]}_{\mathfrak{p}}|\asymp\ell^{\mathbf{1}_{n\text{ odd}}}q_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\lfloor n^{2}/4\rfloor\ell}

in both the golden and super-golden cases.

Example 4.3.11
.

If
\mathfrak{p}
is non-split, then
G_{\mathfrak{p}}
is a unitary group. Coordinatize

X_{*}(A_{G})=\{(m_{1},\dotsc,m_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}\;:\;m_{i}=-m_{n+1-i}\}

with
m_{n/2}=m_{n/2+1}=0
if
G_{\mathfrak{p}}
is non-quasisplit. We still have
2\rho_{G}=(n-1,n-3,\dotsc,-n+1)
. Then, inspecting Examples
3.3.5
-
3.3.9
, the maximized pairing is with the
(m_{i})\in\Sigma
with
m_{i}=(1+\mathbf{1}_{\mathfrak{p}\text{ ramified}})^{-1}
for
1\leq i\leq\lfloor n/2\rfloor-\mathbf{1}_{G_{\mathfrak{p}}\text{ n.qs.}}
. This gives

|S_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}|\asymp q_{\mathfrak{p}}^{A\ell},\qquad A=\begin{cases}2\lfloor n^{2}/4\rfloor&\mathfrak{p}\text{ unramified, }G_{\mathfrak{p}}\text{ quasisplit}\\
\lfloor n^{2}/4\rfloor&\mathfrak{p}\text{ ramified, }G_{\mathfrak{p}}\text{ quasisplit}\\
n^{2}/2-2&\mathfrak{p}\text{ unramified, }G_{\mathfrak{p}}\text{ non-quasisplit}\\
n^{2}/4-1&\mathfrak{p}\text{ ramified, }G_{\mathfrak{p}}\text{ non-quasisplit}\end{cases}.

Example 4.3.12
.

In the non-standard
\tau
for
U_{4}
case of Example
3.3.15
/
4.2.6
(3),
2\rho_{G}=(3,1,-1,3)
and the pairing in
4.3.9
is maximized for
\lambda=(1,0,0,-1)
. This gives

|S_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}|\asymp q_{\mathfrak{p}}^{6\ell}.

In the similar non-standard case of Example
3.3.16
/
4.2.6
(4), we similarly get

|S_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}|\asymp q_{\mathfrak{p}}^{3\ell}.

Finally, in the decimated non-standard case of Example
3.4.7
/
4.2.6
(5), the maximizing
\lambda
is
(1/2,1/2,-1/2,-1/2)
, giving

|S_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}|\asymp q_{\mathfrak{p}}^{4\ell}.

4.3.3
Another Criterion for Class Number One

In this subsection we introduce an alternative “elementary” criterion to lemma
2.3.3
to prove that some
G=U_{n}^{E,H}
is of class number one (equivalently, that
G(\widehat{\mathcal{O}})
is an almost golden group).

First we record the following general Lemma.

Lemma 4.3.13
.

Let
X
be a connected
k
-regular graph and
v_{0}
a vertex in the graph.
For
\Lambda
acting on
G
, denote
C=\mathrm{Stab}_{\Gamma}(v_{0})
and
S=\{g\in\Lambda\,|\,\mathrm{dist}(g.v_{0},v_{0})=1\}
.
If
|S/C|=k
, then
\Lambda
acts transitively on the vertices of
X
.

Proof.

First denote
N(v_{0})
the set of neighboring vertices of
v_{0}
in the graph, and note that
k=|N(v_{0})|
, and that
|S/C|
denotes the number of neighbors of
v_{0}
in the orbit of
v_{0}
by
\Lambda
.
In particular, by
|S/C|=k
we get that
N(v_{0})\subset\Lambda.v_{0}
.

Now let
v
be a general vertex in the graph, denote
n=\mathrm{dist}(v,v_{0})
, and we shall prove that
v\in\Lambda.v_{0}
by induction on
n
.
The base case
n=1
follows from
N(v_{0})\subset\Lambda.v_{0}
, and assume the validity of the claim for
n-1
.
Since the graph is connected there is path from
v_{0}
to
v
and let
u
be the neighbor of
v
satisfying
n-1=\mathrm{dist}(u,v_{0})
.
By the induction assumption there is
g\in\Lambda
such that
g.v_{0}=u
.
From this we get

1=\mathrm{dist}(v,u)=\mathrm{dist}(v,g.v_{0})=\mathrm{dist}(g^{-1}.v,v_{0}),

namely
g^{-1}.v\in N(v_{0})
, and since
N(v_{0})\subset\Lambda.v_{0}
we get
v\in\Lambda.v_{0}
, as needed.
∎

The above Lemma specialize to the following Corollary when we take
X
to be the Bruhat-Tits building of
GL_{d}(F_{\mathfrak{p}})
(whose underlying graph is indeed regular).

Corollary 4.3.14
.

Let
G=GL_{d}(F_{\mathfrak{p}})
,
K=GL_{d}({\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{p}})
,
\mathcal{B}
the Bruhat-Tits building of
G
,
v_{0}
the vertex in
\mathcal{B}
whose stabilizer is
K
, and
\deg(v_{0})=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}{n\choose i}_{q_{\mathfrak{p}}}
, the degree of
v_{0}
in
\mathcal{B}
.
For a discrete subgroup
\Lambda\leq G
, denote
C=\Lambda\cap K
and
S=\{g\in\Lambda\,|\,\mathrm{dist}(g.v_{0},v_{0})=1\}
.
If
|S|/|C|=\deg(v_{0})
then
G=\Lambda\cdot K\cdot Z_{G}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.3.13
we get that
\Lambda
acts transitively on the vertices of the building
\mathcal{B}
.
Since
G/Z_{G}\leq\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{B})
and
K=\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(v_{0})
, we get that
G/Z_{G}\leq\Lambda\cdot K
.
∎

Combining the above Corollary together with the Lemma
2.2.4
, which states that the class number is equal the
\mathfrak{p}
-class number for any prime
\mathfrak{p}
, we get the our second criterion for proving class number one (which avoids calculating the mass formula).

Proposition 4.3.15
.

Let
G=U_{n}^{E,H}
with
E
of class number one,
\mathfrak{p}
a split prime such that
G({\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{p}})
is hyperspecial, and
v_{0}
the vertex in the Bruhat-Tits building of
G(F_{\mathfrak{p}})
whose stabilizer is
G({\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{p}})
.
If

|\{g\in M_{d}(\mathcal{O}_{E})\;|\;g^{*}Hg=q_{\mathfrak{p}}H\}|/|G({\mathcal{O}})|=\sum_{i=0}^{n}{n\choose i}_{q_{\mathfrak{p}}},

then
G
is of class number one.

Proof.

Denote
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}=G({\mathcal{O}}[1/\mathfrak{p}])\leq G(F_{\mathfrak{p}})
,
C_{\mathfrak{p}}=G({\mathcal{O}})=\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}\cap G({\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{p}})
,
S_{\mathfrak{p}}=\{g\in\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}\,|\,\mathrm{dist}(g.v_{0},v_{0})=1\}
, and
\widehat{S}_{\mathfrak{p}}=\{g\in\Mat_{n\times n}(\mathcal{O}_{E})\,|\,g^{*}Hg=q_{\mathfrak{p}}H\}

As in Example
4.3.2
, if we factor
p=\pi_{1}\pi_{2}
in
\mathcal{O}_{E}
, then

S_{\mathfrak{p}}=\pi_{1}^{-1}\widehat{S}_{\mathfrak{p}}\setminus(\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}\cap(C_{\mathfrak{p}}\cup\pi_{1}^{-1}C_{\mathfrak{p}}))

so

|\widehat{S}_{\mathfrak{p}}|/|C_{\mathfrak{p}}|=|\pi_{1}^{-1}\widehat{S}_{\mathfrak{p}}/C_{\mathfrak{p}}|=|S_{\mathfrak{p}}|/|C_{\mathfrak{p}}|+1+\mathbf{1}_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}\cap\pi_{1}^{-1}C_{\mathfrak{p}}\neq\emptyset}.

The argument of lemma
4.3.13
also gives that
|S_{\mathfrak{p}}|/|C_{\mathfrak{p}}|\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}{n\choose i}_{q_{\mathfrak{p}}}
so our assumptions force

|S_{\mathfrak{p}}|/|C_{\mathfrak{p}}|=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}{n\choose i}_{q_{\mathfrak{p}}},\qquad\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}\cap\pi_{1}^{-1}C_{\mathfrak{p}}\neq\emptyset.

Therefore, Corollary
4.3.14
gives
G(F_{\mathfrak{p}})=\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}\cdot G({\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{p}})\cdot Z_{G}(F_{\mathfrak{p}})=\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}\cdot G({\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{p}})
, and by Lemma
2.2.4
, we get the class number one assertion.
∎

4.4
Growth, Navigation, and Approximation

Next we summarize how the gate sets that correspond to golden adelic groups satisfy the last three properties in the Definition
1.2.1
of golden gate sets; namely, growth, navigation and approximation.

Theorem 4.4.1
.

Let
G=U_{n}^{E,H}
, let
K^{\prime}\leq G(\widehat{\mathcal{O}})
be an almost golden adelic subgroup, let
\mathfrak{p}
be a prime at which
K^{\prime}
is golden (resp.
\tau
-super-golden for
\tau
traversable), and let
S_{\mathfrak{p}}
(resp.
{}^{0}\!S_{\mathfrak{p}}\cup C_{\mathfrak{p}}
which is generated by elements of finite order by
4.2.5
(4)) be the gate set corresponding to
K^{\prime}
as in definition
4.2.1
(resp.
4.2.4
). Then
S_{\mathfrak{p}}
satisfies the following properties:

(1)

Growth
:

|S_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}|
grows exponentially in
\ell
.

(2)

Navigation
: The group generated by
S_{\mathfrak{p}}
is
\bar{\Lambda}_{p}:=\bar{\Lambda}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{K^{\prime}}

and it has the following efficient solution for its word problem: Given
1\neq g\in\bar{\Lambda}_{\mathfrak{p}}
, find an element
s\in{}^{0}\!S_{\mathfrak{p}}
such that
a_{sg}\prec a_{g}
, and proceed by induction on
a_{g}
. The algorithm will terminate in
O(|{}^{0}\!S_{\mathfrak{p}}|\cdot\|a_{g}\|))
time which (for a fixed
\mathfrak{p}
) is polynomial in the input
g
.

(3)

Approximation
: If
\mathcal{O}_{F}
is Euclidean, there exists a heuristic polynomial algorithm such that given
g\in PU(n)
,
\varepsilon>0
, and
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
if we write
g=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\prod_{k=1}^{3}g^{i,j}_{k}
,
g^{i,j}_{k}\in U(n)
, as in the decomposition given in Lemma
4.4.5
below and if
B\left(g^{i,j}_{k},\varepsilon\right)\cap S_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}\neq\emptyset
for any
i,j,k
, then the algorithm outputs an element in
B\left(g,\varepsilon N\right)\cap S_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell\cdot A]}
, where
A=3\cdot{n\choose 2}
.

Remark 4.4.2
.

If
S_{\mathfrak{p}}
posses the covering property (as well as the growth property), then by
[
70
, Corollary 3.2]
, for any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\ell=O(\log(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
, such that for any
g\in U(n)
, then
B\left(g,\varepsilon\right)\cap S_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}\neq\emptyset
.
Note that a simple union bound gives
\ell=\Omega(\log(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
, for generic elements
g\in U(n)
.
Therefore, in claim (3) of Theorem
4.4.1
, assuming we have the covering property, in the generic case we can replace condition
B\left(g^{i,j}_{k},\varepsilon\right)\cap S_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}\neq\emptyset
for any
i,j,k
, with the condition
B\left(g,\varepsilon\right)\cap S_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}\neq\emptyset
, where the constant
A
now is potentially bigger.

Proof of Theorem
4.4.1
.

In order:

Growth
:
This follows from Corollary
4.3.8
.

Navigation
:
Both termination and run time follow by the proof of
3.4.2
(1). By uniqueness of the Cartan decomposition
3.1
, we can compute
a_{g}
at each step by the integer normal form algorithm on
G_{\mathfrak{p}}=GL_{n}(F_{\mathfrak{p}})
when
v
is split or on the bigger group
GL_{n}(E_{\mathfrak{q}})\supset G_{\mathfrak{p}}
when
w
lies over
v
and
v
is non-split.

Approximation
:
This follows as a consequence (Corollary
4.4.6
) of the algorithm of Ross and Selinger
[
74
,
70
]
for approximating elements in
U(2)
by matrices with integer coefficients. We sketch this in the subsequent part of this section.

To apply those results, Proposition
3.4.2
(2) and the work of §
4.3.1
find
i
so that
S_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[k]}\subseteq U_{n}^{E/H}(F)\cap\varpi_{\mathfrak{p}}^{-i}M_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{E}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}})
if and only if
k\leq\ell
. Furthermore, by Gram-Schmidt, we can find a diagonal
H^{\prime}
such that
U_{n}^{E,H}/F\cong U_{n}^{E,H^{\prime}}/F
so we can find
m\in\mathbb{N}
with only ramified factors such that
U_{n}^{E/H}(\mathcal{O}_{F})\subseteq m^{-1/2}M_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{E})\cap U^{H^{\prime}}(n)
. These two together provide the inputs
m,H^{\prime}
in Corollary
4.4.6
below.

Since
S_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}
is then a finite fraction of
\varpi_{\mathfrak{p}}^{-i}m^{-1/2}M_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{E})\cap U^{H^{\prime}}(n)
, sampling enough outputs will heuristically generate one in
S_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}
with high probability.
∎

In the following Theorem we state a slight generalization of the Ross-Selinger heuristic algorithm (see
[
70
]
for a discussion on the heuristic nature of the algorithm).
We introduce the following notion of
(\varepsilon,m)
-approximation w.r.t. some ring of integers.

Definition 4.4.3
.

Let
K
be a totally real number field such that its ring of integers
\mathcal{O}_{K}
is Euclidean, let
d\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
H^{\prime}\in M_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{K}[\sqrt{-d}])
be a diagonal definite Hermitian matrix.
For
\varepsilon>0
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
, and a unitary matrix
g\in\in U^{H^{\prime}}(2)
, we say that
h\in M_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{K}[\sqrt{-d}])
is an
(\varepsilon,m)
-approximation of
g
(w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}_{K}[\sqrt{-d}]
) if

\tilde{h}=m^{\frac{-1}{2}}\cdot h\in U^{H^{\prime}}(2)\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad 1-\frac{|\mbox{Trace}(g^{*}\cdot\tilde{h})|}{2}<\varepsilon^{2}.

“Theorem” 4.4.4

(
[
74
]
,
[
70
, Thm 2.6, §2.3]
)
.

Let
K
,
d
, and
H^{\prime}
be as above.
Then, there is a randomized, heuristic efficient algorithm, such that:

(1)

Given
\varepsilon>0
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
, and a diagonal unitary matrix
g\in\in U^{H^{\prime}}(2)
, it finds
h\in M_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{K}[\sqrt{-d}])
which is an
(\varepsilon,m)
-approximation of
g
, if such a matrix exists.

(2)

Given
\varepsilon>0
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
, and a unitary matrix
g\in\in U^{H^{\prime}}(2)
, written as a product of three diagonal unitary matrices
g=g_{1}g_{2}g_{3}
, and assume each
g_{i}
has an
(\varepsilon,m)
-approximation in
M_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{K}[\sqrt{-d}])
, then the algorithm finds
h\in M_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{K}[\sqrt{-d}])
which is an
(3\varepsilon,m^{3})
-approximation of
g
.

Proof.

For (1) the original statement of the Theorem 2.6 in
[
70
]
is with
d=1
and
H=I
, but their arguments works just as well for any fixed
d
by modifying the lattices studied and any fixed diagonal
H^{\prime}
by the discussion after (3.13) therein.
(2) clearly follows from (1).
∎

Theorem
4.4.4
(2), can be generalized to higher dimensional unitary groups.
To do so we recall the following decomposition of any unitary matrix as a bounded product of level
2
unitary matrices (by
[
69
, Section 4.5.1]
), each of which can be written as a product of three one-parameter diagonal unitary matrices (by
[
74
]
).

Lemma 4.4.5
.

[
69
, Section 4.5.1]

Any unitary matrix
g\in U(n)
, can be written as a product of level
2
unitary matrices
g=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}g^{i,j}
, namely
(g^{i,j})_{k,\ell}=0
if
k,\ell\not\in\{i,j\}
.
Note that any level
2
unitary matrix can be identify as an element of
U(2)
, and write
g^{i,j}=g^{i,j}_{1}g^{i,j}_{2}g^{i,j}_{3}
, a product of three diagonal unitary matrices
g^{i,j}_{k}
, as in
[
74
]
.

We now state the straightforward higher dimensional generalization of Theorem
4.4.4
(2), which we use in the proof of Claim (3) in Theorem
4.4.1
(where
m
is running over powers of a fixed prime).

Corollary 4.4.6
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, denote
N=\frac{3n(n-1)}{2}
, and let
K
,
d
, and
H^{\prime}
be as above.
Then, there is a heuristic efficient algorithm such that given
\varepsilon>0
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
, and a unitary matrix,
g\in U(n)
, and assume each
g^{i,j}_{k}
has an
(\varepsilon,m)
-approximation w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}_{K}[\sqrt{-d}]
, then the algorithm finds
h\in M_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{K}[\sqrt{-d}])
which is an
(\varepsilon N,m^{N})
-approximation of
g
.

Proof.

By Theorem
4.4.4
, for each
1\leq i<j\leq n
and
k=1,2,3
, there exists
h^{i,j}_{k}\in M_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{K}[\sqrt{-d}])
such that
\widetilde{h^{i,j}_{k}}=m^{-1/2}h^{i,j}_{k}\in U^{H^{\prime}}(n)
and
1-|\mbox{Trace}((g^{i,j}_{k})^{*}\cdot\widetilde{h^{i,j}_{k}})|/n<\varepsilon^{2}
.
Denote
h=\prod_{i,j,k}h^{i,j}_{k}\in M_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{K}[\sqrt{-d}])
, then
\tilde{h}=m^{-N/2}h\in U^{H^{\prime}}(n)
and by the triangle inequality for the bi-invariant metric
d\,:\,U(n)\times U(n)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, defined by
d(g,h)=\sqrt{1-|\mbox{Trace}(g^{*}\cdot h)|/n}
, we get that
d(g,\tilde{h})\leq\sum_{i,j,k}d(g^{i,j}_{k},\widetilde{h^{i,j}_{k}})\leq N\varepsilon
, hence
h
is an
(N\varepsilon,m^{N})
-approximation of
g
.
∎

4.5
Explicit Constructions of Golden Adelic Groups

Let us present several examples of golden and super-golden adelic groups. Table 8.1 in
[
51
]
gives a very comprehensive list of unitary groups over
\mathbb{Q}
with class number one though in a form that takes some work to translate into what we need.

4.5.1
Previous Constructions

Example 4.5.1
.

In dimension
n=2
, several constructions were given in
[
70
,
81
]
.
Let us present just one example of a golden adelic group which is super-golden at
p=3
:

G:=U_{2}^{\mathbb{Q}\left[\sqrt{-1}\right],I}/\mathbb{Q},\quad K^{\prime}:=\left\{g\in G\left(\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\right)\mid g\equiv I\mod 2\right\}.

Example 4.5.2
.

In dimension
n=3
, several constructions were given in
[
28
,
16
]
.
Here are two such examples of golden adelic groups:

G:=U_{3}^{\mathbb{Q}\left[\sqrt{-1}\right],I}/\mathbb{Q},\quad K^{\prime}:=\left\{g\in G\left(\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\right)\mid\forall i,\;g_{i,i}\equiv 1\mod 2+2i\right\},

G:=U_{3}^{\mathbb{Q}\left[\sqrt{-3}\right],I}/\mathbb{Q},\quad K^{\prime}:=\left\{g\in G\left(\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\right)\mid\forall i,\;g_{i,i}\equiv 1\mod 3\right\}.

Furthermore, as a consequence of the work of Mumford
[
65
]
(see
[
16
]
for the details), one get that for the
3\times 3
Hermitian positive definite matrix

H:=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}3&\lambda&\lambda\\
\bar{\lambda}&3&\lambda\\
\bar{\lambda}&\bar{\lambda}&3\end{array}\right)\quad\mbox{where}\quad\lambda:=\frac{1+\sqrt{-7}}{2},

the following is a golden adelic group which is super-golden at
p=2
:

G:=U_{3}^{\mathbb{Q}\left[\sqrt{-7}\right],H}/\mathbb{Q},\quad K^{\prime}:=\left\{g\in G\left(\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\right)\mid\forall i>j,\;g_{i,j}\equiv 0\mod 2\right\}.

4.5.2
New Constructions:
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-3})

Below we present our constructions of golden and super-golden groups.
We start with the case of
E/F=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-3})/\mathbb{Q}
.

Remark 4.5.3
.

The motivation for the following construction come from reading
[
88
]
and
[
7
]
, where the authors presents elegant constructions of the
E_{8}
lattice in
4
-dimensional spaces (field extensions in
[
7
]
) over the Eisenstein quadratic imaginary field
E=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-3})
.

In fact, our examples over other quadratic extensions also appear to come from realizing the integers in the extension as the
E_{8}
lattice, suggesting a deeper, abstract reason why this is a fruitful place to look.

Proposition 4.5.4
.

For the
4\times 4
Hermitian positive definite matrix,

H_{3}:=2\cdot\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}I&A\\
-A&I\end{array}\right)\quad\mbox{where}\quad A:=\frac{\sqrt{-3}}{3}\cdot\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&1\\
1&-1\end{array}\right),

define

G:=U_{4}^{\mathbb{Q}\left[\sqrt{-3}\right],H_{3}}/\mathbb{Q}.

Then the following are essentially golden adelic groups:

K_{1}^{(2)}:=\{g\in G(\widehat{\mathbb{Z}})\mid g\equiv I\mod 2\},\qquad K_{1}^{(\sqrt{3})}:=\{g\in G(\widehat{\mathbb{Z}})\mid g\equiv I\mod{\sqrt{3}}\}

Proof.

We note that
H_{3}
was chosen so that
U^{E,H_{3}}
is quasisplit at all places and
U^{E,H_{3}}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
is special at
p=3
and hyperspecial at all other
p
, e.g, as in Remark
3.2.3
. In particular
\Ram(H)=\emptyset
so all the
\lambda_{\ell}
in
2.3.2
are
1
. Plugging in the
L
-values:

R(G)=155520^{-1}.

We may also compute that
|G\left(\mathbb{Z}\right)|=155520
. One method that runs in a few minutes on a personal laptop is to first minimally scale
H
to have integral entries, note that the diagonal entries are then all
3
, find all
2
2

2

there are 240 of these corresponding to the roots of an interpretation of
\mathcal{O}_{E}^{4}
as the
E_{8}
lattice.

v\in\mathcal{O}_{E}^{4}
of norm
3
, and finally build up all possible
\mathcal{O}_{E}
-matrices preserving
H
through choosing the rows one-by-one from this set of
v
. Therefore, by lemma
2.3.3
,
G
has class number one.

Since
K_{1}^{(2)}
is the kernel of the reduction map
r_{2}:G(\widehat{\mathbb{Z}})\to G(\mathbb{Z}/2)
, it therefore suffices to show that
r_{2}|_{G(\mathbb{Z})}
is surjective with kernel contained in
Z_{G}(\widehat{\mathbb{Z}})
. The prime
2
is unramified in
G
, hence
G\left(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}\right)\cong U_{4}\left(\mathbb{F}_{2}\right)
and by a standard formula,
\left|U_{4}\left(\mathbb{F}_{2}\right)\right|=77760=|G(\mathbb{Z})|/2
. Another computer check finally shows that
G\left(\mathbb{Z}\right)\cap\ker r_{2}=\{\pm 1\}
.

For
K_{1}^{(\sqrt{3})}
, since
G(\widehat{\mathbb{Z}})
is special at
3
,
G(\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(\mathbb{F}_{3}[x]/x^{2})
with reductive quotient
\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(\mathbb{F}_{3})
. Furthermore, this composite map
G(\widehat{\mathbb{Z}})\to\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(\mathbb{F}_{3})
can be described exactly by reducing matrix entries mod
\sqrt{3}
. Therefore, by a standard formula,
[K:K_{1}^{(\sqrt{3})}]=|\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(\mathbb{F}_{3})|=51840=|G(\widehat{Z})|/3
. A computer check then gives that
G(\mathbb{Z})\cap K_{1}^{(\sqrt{3})}=\{1,\zeta_{3},\zeta_{3}^{2}\}
, which has
3
elements.
∎

This example gives gate sets
S_{p}
for all
p
with growth rates:

|S_{p}^{[\ell]}|\asymp\begin{cases}p^{4\ell}&p\equiv 1\pmod{3}\\
p^{8\ell}&p\equiv 2\pmod{3},\\
81^{\ell}&p=3\end{cases}.

It also can produce super-golden examples:

Proposition 4.5.5
.

Consider
G=U_{4}^{\mathbb{Q}\left[\sqrt{-3}\right],H_{3}}/\mathbb{Q}
as in Proposition
4.5.4
. Choose
X_{2}\in\mathrm{GL}_{4}(\mathbb{Z}_{2}[\sqrt{-3}])
such that

X_{2}^{*}H_{3}X_{2}\equiv\begin{pmatrix}&&&1\\
&&1&\\
&1&&\\
1&&&\end{pmatrix}\pmod{4},

for example,

X_{2}:=\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{i\sqrt{3}}{2}&\frac{1}{2}+\frac{i}{2\sqrt{3}}&\frac{1}{2}-\frac{i}{2\sqrt{3}}&\frac{2i}{\sqrt{3}}\\
-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{i}{2\sqrt{3}}&-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{i\sqrt{3}}{2}&\frac{2i}{\sqrt{3}}&-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{i}{2\sqrt{3}}\\
0&\frac{1}{2}-\frac{i\sqrt{3}}{2}&-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{i\sqrt{3}}{2}&0\\
\frac{1}{2}-\frac{i\sqrt{3}}{2}&0&0&-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{i\sqrt{3}}{2}\end{pmatrix}.

Define

K^{(2)}_{s}:=\left\{g\in G(\widehat{\mathbb{Z}})\,\middle|\,X_{2}^{-1}gX_{2}\in\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{O}_{E_{2}}&\mathcal{O}_{E_{2}}&\mathcal{O}_{E_{2}}&\mathcal{O}_{E_{2}}\\
2\mathcal{O}_{E_{2}}&\mathcal{O}_{E_{2}}&\mathcal{O}_{E_{2}}&\mathcal{O}_{E_{2}}\\
2\mathcal{O}_{E_{2}}&\mathcal{O}_{E_{2}}&\mathcal{O}_{E_{2}}&\mathcal{O}_{E_{2}}\\
4\mathcal{O}_{E_{2}}&2\mathcal{O}_{E_{2}}&2\mathcal{O}_{E_{2}}&\mathcal{O}_{E_{2}}\end{pmatrix}\right\}.

Then
K^{(2)}_{s}
is almost
\tau
-super golden for (non-standard traversable)
\tau
as in Example
4.2.6
(3).

Proof.

Note that
X_{2}^{-1}G(\mathbb{Q}_{2})X_{2}
then has a chamber corresponding to the lattice
\mathcal{O}_{E_{2}}^{4-i}\oplus 2\mathcal{O}_{E_{2}}^{i}
for
0\leq i\leq 3
. The non-special vertex corresponds to
i=1,3
which are dual to each other under
X_{2}^{*}H_{3}X_{2}
. Therefore lattices
\mathcal{O}_{E_{2}}^{4}
and
\mathcal{O}_{E_{2}}\oplus 2\mathcal{O}_{E}^{2}\oplus 4\mathcal{O}_{2}
are hyperspecial connected through a two-step path through a non-special vertex and
K^{(2),s}
exactly stabilizes both.

We can also compute that
[K:K^{(2)}_{s}]=[K_{2}:K^{(2)}_{s}]=90=G(\mathbb{Z})/1728
(this is counting the choices of
\tau
starting at the hyperspecial vertex
K
: there are
45
choices for the first edge and
2
per each of these for the second). Therefore, since
K
is almost golden,
K^{(2)}_{s}
is almost golden if and only if
|G(\mathbb{Z})\cap K^{(2)}_{s}|=1728
, which holds.
∎

Proposition 4.5.6
.

Consider
G=U_{4}^{\mathbb{Q}\left[\sqrt{-3}\right],H_{3}}/\mathbb{Q}
as in Proposition
4.5.4
. Choose
X_{3}\in\mathrm{GL}_{4}(\mathbb{Z}_{3}[\sqrt{-3}])
such that for some
C\in(\mathbb{Z}/3)[\sqrt{-3}]
,

X_{3}^{*}H_{3}X_{3}\equiv C\begin{pmatrix}&&&\sqrt{-3}\\
&&\sqrt{-3}&\\
&-\sqrt{-3}&&\\
-\sqrt{-3}&&&\end{pmatrix}\pmod{3},

for example,

X_{3}:=\begin{pmatrix}4&-3&i\sqrt{3}&-2i\sqrt{3}\\
3&4&-2i\sqrt{3}&-i\sqrt{3}\\
-4i\sqrt{3}&-i\sqrt{3}&-1&-4\\
-i\sqrt{3}&4i\sqrt{3}&4&-1\end{pmatrix}.

Define

K^{(3)}_{s}:=\left\{g\in G(\widehat{\mathbb{Z}})\,\middle|\,X_{3}^{-1}gX_{3}\in\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{O}_{E_{\sqrt{-3}}}&\mathcal{O}_{E_{\sqrt{-3}}}&\mathcal{O}_{E_{\sqrt{-3}}}&\mathcal{O}_{E_{\sqrt{-3}}}\\
\mathcal{O}_{E_{\sqrt{-3}}}&\mathcal{O}_{E_{\sqrt{-3}}}&\mathcal{O}_{E_{\sqrt{-3}}}&\mathcal{O}_{E_{\sqrt{-3}}}\\
3\mathcal{O}_{E_{\sqrt{-3}}}&3\mathcal{O}_{E_{\sqrt{-3}}}&\mathcal{O}_{E_{\sqrt{-3}}}&\mathcal{O}_{E_{\sqrt{-3}}}\\
3\mathcal{O}_{E_{\sqrt{-3}}}&3\mathcal{O}_{E_{\sqrt{-3}}}&\mathcal{O}_{E_{\sqrt{-3}}}&\mathcal{O}_{E_{\sqrt{-3}}}\end{pmatrix}\right\}.

Then
K^{(3)}_{s}
is almost
\tau
-super golden for (decimated, non-standard traversable)
\tau
as in Example
4.2.6
(5).

Proof.

Note that
X_{3}^{-1}G(\mathbb{Q}_{3})X_{3}
then has a chamber corresponding to the lattices
\mathcal{O}_{E_{\sqrt{-3}}}^{4-i}\oplus\sqrt{3}\mathcal{O}_{E_{\sqrt{-3}}}^{i}
for
0\leq i\leq 3
. The non-special vertex corresponds to
i=2
. Therefore lattices
\mathcal{O}_{E_{\sqrt{-3}}}^{4}
and
\mathcal{O}_{E_{\sqrt{-3}}}^{2}\oplus 3\mathcal{O}_{E_{\sqrt{-3}}}^{2}
are special connected through a two-step path through a non-special vertex and
K^{(3)}_{s}
exactly stabilizes both.

We then compute as in Proposition
4.5.5
that
[K:K^{(3),s}]=[K_{3}:K^{(3),s}_{3}]=120=|G(\mathbb{Z})|/1296
(there are
40
choices for the first edge and
3
per each of these for the second). It therefore suffices to check by computer that
|G(\mathbb{Z})\cap K^{(3),s}|=1296
which holds.
∎

K^{(3)}_{s}
and
K^{(2)}_{s}
therefore both produce super-golden gate sets for
PU(4)
with finite group

C_{p}=G(\mathbb{Z})/\langle\zeta_{6}\rangle\cong{}^{2}\!A_{2}(2)\cong C_{2}(3)

and a single extra element that we call
T_{E,2}
and
T_{E,3}
respectively. They have growth rates

|S_{p}^{[\ell]}|\asymp\begin{cases}64^{\ell}&K^{(2),s}\\
81^{\ell}&K^{(3)}_{s}\end{cases}.

Let
K^{(\sqrt{-3})}_{s}
be the same as
K^{(3)}_{s}
except that the congruence condition is only mod
\sqrt{-3}
. If we let

X_{M}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0&1\\
0&0&-1&0\\
0&\frac{i}{\sqrt{3}}&-\frac{i}{\sqrt{3}}&\frac{i}{\sqrt{3}}\\
-\frac{i}{\sqrt{3}}&0&\frac{i}{\sqrt{3}}&\frac{i}{\sqrt{3}}\end{pmatrix},

then
X_{M}^{-1}K^{(\sqrt{-3})}_{s}X_{M}
is
\{\pm 1\}
times the group of monomial matrices with entries that are
3
rd roots of unity.

Using the isomorphism
(K\cap G(\mathbb{Q}))/\langle\zeta_{3}\rangle\cong\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(\mathbb{F}_{3})
from the proof of Proposition
4.5.4
and noting that it maps
(K^{(\sqrt{-3})}_{s}\cap G(\mathbb{Q}))/\langle\zeta_{3}\rangle
to a parabolic subgroup,
K\cap G(\mathbb{Q})
is generated by
K^{(\sqrt{-3})}_{s}\cap G(\mathbb{Q})
and the preimage
C_{W}
of a long Weyl element. We may choose
C_{W}
so that

X_{M}^{-1}C_{W}X_{M}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{-3}}\begin{pmatrix}1&0&-1&-1\\
0&1&-1&1\\
-1&-1&-1&0\\
-1&1&0&-1\end{pmatrix}.

Using the techniques of
4.3.3
, we may choose (possibly without loss of generality conjugating
K^{(3)}_{s}
within
K^{(\sqrt{-3})}_{s}
):

X_{M}^{-1}T_{E,3}X_{M}=\begin{pmatrix}&&&1\\
&&1&\\
&-1&&\\
-1&&&\end{pmatrix}.

This gives Theorem
1.2.4
.

Finally we note that while the hermitian positive definite matrix
H_{3}
gives golden and super golden gate sets with large finite group
C_{p}\cong\,^{2}A_{2}(2)
, one can instead take the identity matrix
I
and also get a golden gate sets with a smaller finite group,
C_{p}\cong\langle\zeta_{6}\rangle^{4-1}\rtimes S_{4}
. At this time it is not clear whether super golden gate sets can be obtained from this form.

Proposition 4.5.7
.

Define

G:=U_{4}^{\mathbb{Q}\left[\sqrt{-3}\right],I}/\mathbb{Q}.

Then
G(\widehat{\mathbb{Z}})
is an almost golden adelic group.

Proof.

We note that
G
is quasisplit at all places and
G(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
is hyperspecial at all
p\neq 3
.
At
p=3
, we have that
G(\mathbb{Z}_{3})
is a maximal parahoic subgroup (see Lemma 5.8 in
[
16
]
, it is stated for
n=3
but the proof works verbatim for all
n\geq 2
).
By
[
33
]
we get that
\lambda_{3}=\frac{3^{N(Sp_{4}/\mathbb{F}_{3}))}\cdot Sp_{4}(\mathbb{F}_{3})}{3^{N(G/\mathbb{F}_{3})}\cdot G(\mathbb{F}_{3})}=\frac{1}{5}
, and for any
p\neq 3
we have
\lambda_{p}=1
, hence
\lambda(G)=\frac{1}{5}
.
Since
G
is an inner form of
U_{4}^{\mathbb{Q}\left[\sqrt{-3}\right],H_{3}}
, and since
L(G)
is invariant under taking inner forms, we get that
L(G)=155520^{-1}
, hence
R(G)=31104^{-1}
.
On the other hand,
G(\mathbb{Z})
is comprised of exactly the monomial matrices with non-zero coefficients in the unit group of the ring of integers, hence
G(\mathbb{Z})\cong\langle\zeta_{6}\rangle^{4}\rtimes S_{4}
, and therefore
|G(\mathbb{Z})|=6^{4}\cdot 4!=31104
.
Therefore, by lemma
2.3.3
,
G
has class number one.
∎

4.5.3
New Constructions: Rank-
8

Next, we present the rank-
8
golden group of
[
62
]
.

Proposition 4.5.8
.

For the
8\times 8
Hermitian positive definite matrix

{}^{8}\!H_{3}:=\begin{pmatrix}H_{3}&0\\
0&H_{3}\end{pmatrix}.

Then the following is an almost golden adelic group

G:=U_{8}^{\mathbb{Q}[\sqrt{-3}],{}^{8}\!H_{3}}/\mathbb{Q},\qquad K:=G(\widehat{\mathbb{Z}})

Proof.

Then
U_{8}^{\mathbb{Q}[\sqrt{-3}],{}^{8}\!H_{3}}
is quasisplit at all places and
U_{8}^{\mathbb{Q}[\sqrt{-3}],{}^{8}\!H_{3}}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
is hyperspecial at all
p\neq 3
and special at
p=3
. The result then follows from the main result of
[
62
]
. We can also compute that
R(G)=48372940800^{-1}
and that
U_{8}^{\mathbb{Q}[\sqrt{-3}],{}^{8}\!H_{3}}(\mathbb{Z})
is the
U_{4}^{\mathbb{Q}[\sqrt{-3}],\!H_{3}}(\mathbb{Z})
from
4.5.4
squared semidirect
\mathbb{Z}/2
which has size
2\cdot 155520^{2}=48372940800
.
∎

This gives golden gate sets for
3
qubits.

4.5.4
New Constructions:
\mathbb{Q}(i)

Moving on to
E/F=\mathbb{Q}(i)/\mathbb{Q}
:

Proposition 4.5.9
.

For the
4\times 4
Hermitian positive definite matrix

H_{4}:=\begin{pmatrix}4&0&2+i&1+i\\
0&4&-1+i&2-i\\
2-i&-1-i&2&0\\
1-i&2+i&0&2\end{pmatrix},

define

G:=U^{\mathbb{Q}(i),H_{4}}_{4}/\mathbb{Q}.

Then
G(\widehat{\mathbb{Z}})
is an almost golden adelic subgroup of
G
.

Proof.

H_{4}
is selected so that
G(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
is hyperspecial at all
p\neq 2
and special at
p=2
following Remark
3.2.3
. Note that the different ideal of
\mathbb{Q}_{2}(i)/\mathbb{Q}_{2}
is
(2)
so we can choose
H_{4}
to be equivalent to the antidiagonal matrix of all ones over
\mathbb{Z}_{2}[i]
.

Then the argument follows applying lemma
2.3.3
as in Proposition
4.5.4
: here, both
R(G)^{-1}
and
|G(\mathbb{Z})|
are
46080
.
∎

H_{4}
also produces some super-golden gate sets:

Proposition 4.5.10
.

Consider
G=U_{4}^{\mathbb{Q}\left[i\right],H_{4}}/\mathbb{Q}
as in Proposition
4.5.9
. Choose
X_{2}\in\mathrm{GL}_{4}(\mathbb{Z}_{2}[i])
such that

X_{2}^{*}H_{4}X_{2}\equiv\begin{pmatrix}&&&1\\
&&1&\\
&1&&\\
1&&&\end{pmatrix}\pmod{4},

for example,

X_{2}:=\begin{pmatrix}-1&-2&-2&-1\\
1&1&-2&1\\
2&2&1-2i&2-i\\
-1-2i&-i&3&-i\end{pmatrix}.

Define

K^{\prime}:=\left\{g\in G(\widehat{\mathbb{Z}})\,\middle|\,X_{2}^{-1}gX_{2}\in\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{O}_{E_{1+i}}&\mathcal{O}_{E_{1+i}}&\mathcal{O}_{E_{1+i}}&\mathcal{O}_{E_{1+i}}\\
\mathcal{O}_{E_{1+i}}&\mathcal{O}_{E_{1+i}}&\mathcal{O}_{E_{1+i}}&\mathcal{O}_{E_{1+i}}\\
2\mathcal{O}_{E_{1+i}}&2\mathcal{O}_{E_{1+i}}&\mathcal{O}_{E_{1+i}}&\mathcal{O}_{E_{1+i}}\\
2\mathcal{O}_{E_{1+i}}&2\mathcal{O}_{E_{1+i}}&\mathcal{O}_{E_{1+i}}&\mathcal{O}_{E_{1+i}}\end{pmatrix}\right\}.

Then
K^{\prime}
is almost
\tau
-super golden for (decimated non-standard traversable)
\tau
as in Example
4.2.6
(5).

Proof.

As in Proposition
4.5.6
,
X_{2}^{-1}G(\mathbb{Q}_{2})X_{2}
exactly stabilizes a particular two-step path through a non-special vertex. In the wildly ramified setting, we additionally need to ensure that this two-step path is actually in
\mathcal{B}(G_{\mathbb{Q}_{2}})
instead of intersecting barbs—this is guaranteed by the congruence condition on
X_{2}
being mod
4
instead of mod
2
.

Then, arguing as in Proposition
4.5.5
,
[K:K^{\prime}]=[K_{2}:K^{\prime}_{2}]=30=|G(\mathbb{Z})|/1536
(there are
15
choices for the first edge and
2
per each of these for the second by standard formulas) and by computer check,
|G(\mathbb{Z})\cap K^{\prime}|=1536
.
∎

The example in Proposition
4.5.10
is particularly interesting since here,
C=G(\mathbb{Z})/\langle i\rangle
is the
2
-qubit Clifford group
3
3

3

Beware that
G(\mathbb{Z})
on the other hand is a different lift of the projective Clifford group to
U(4)
than is standard in the literature.
(this is guaranteed by its size and a classification of maximal finite subgroups of
PU(4)
, e.g,
[
54
]
*Appx D). Therefore, this provides a single element that we call
T_{G}
that together with the
2
-qubit Clifford group gives a super-golden gate set on
PU(4)
with growth rate

|S_{2}^{[\ell]}|\asymp 16^{\ell}.

Proposition 4.5.11
.

In the notation of proposition
4.5.10
, Let

K^{\prime\prime}:=\left\{g\in G(\widehat{\mathbb{Z}})\,\middle|\,X_{2}^{-1}gX_{2}\in\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{O}_{E_{1+i}}&\mathcal{O}_{E_{1+i}}&\mathcal{O}_{E_{1+i}}&\mathcal{O}_{E_{1+i}}\\
(1+i)\mathcal{O}_{E_{1+i}}&\mathcal{O}_{E_{1+i}}&\mathcal{O}_{E_{1+i}}&\mathcal{O}_{E_{1+i}}\\
(1+i)\mathcal{O}_{E_{1+i}}&\mathcal{O}_{E_{1+i}}&\mathcal{O}_{E_{1+i}}&\mathcal{O}_{E_{1+i}}\\
(1+i)\mathcal{O}_{E_{1+i}}&(1+i)\mathcal{O}_{E_{1+i}}&(1+i)\mathcal{O}_{E_{1+i}}&\mathcal{O}_{E_{1+i}}\end{pmatrix}\right\}

Then
K^{\prime\prime}\subseteq G
is almost
\tau
-super golden for (non-standard traversable)
\tau
as in Example
4.2.6
(4).

Proof.

Let
K_{1}\subseteq K
be the subgroup of elements that are trivial mod
(1+i)
. Then,
K/K_{1}
is the reductive quotient in the special fiber of the parahoric model corresponding to
K
and isomorphic to
\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
. Inside this,
K^{\prime\prime}/K_{1}
is a parabolic subgroup, so
K^{\prime\prime}
is the stabilizer of an edge emanating from
x_{0}
. Since
K^{\prime\prime}
doesn’t contain
K^{\prime}
, this edge must connect to a special vertex.

Then, arguing as in Proposition
4.5.5
, we may compute
[K:K^{\prime\prime}]=[K_{2}:K^{\prime\prime}_{2}]=15=|G(\mathbb{Z})|/3072
and that
|G(\mathbb{Z})\cap K^{\prime\prime}|=3072
.
∎

The super-golden gate set
S_{2^{\prime}}
from
4.5.11
is again the 2-qubit Clifford group with an added element we now call
T^{\prime}_{G}
. It has growth rate

|S_{2^{\prime}}^{[\ell]}|\asymp 8^{\ell}.

While this growth rate is much worse than the other examples, the extra gate
T^{\prime}_{G}
has much better properties for fault-tolerant implementation:

Lemma 4.5.12
.

In the notation above,
T^{\prime}_{G}
is in the
3
rd level of the Clifford hierarchy.

Proof.

As in the proof of Proposition
4.5.11
, let
K_{1}
be the elements of
K
that are trivial mod
(1+i)
so that
K/K_{1}\cong\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
. We can also compute:

[G(\mathbb{Z}):G(\mathbb{Z})\cap K_{1}]=[K:K_{1}]\implies G(\mathbb{Z})/(G(\mathbb{Z})\cap K_{1})=K/K_{1}\cong\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(\mathbb{F}_{2}).

Identifying
G(\mathbb{Z})/\langle i\rangle
with the
2
-qubit Clifford group, this characterizes
(G(\mathbb{Z})\cap K_{1})/\langle i\rangle
as the
2
-qubit Pauli group.

Next, since
T^{\prime}_{G}
acts as an involution of the edge containing
x_{0}
stabilized by
K^{\prime\prime}
,
T^{\prime}_{G}K^{\prime\prime}(T^{\prime}_{G})^{-1}
stabilizes
x_{0}
and is therefore contained in
K
. In total

T^{\prime}_{G}(K^{\prime\prime}\cap G(\mathbb{Q}))(T^{\prime}_{G})^{-1}\subseteq G(\mathbb{Z}).

Since
K_{1}\subseteq K^{\prime\prime}
, this shows that
T^{\prime}_{G}
conjugates the Pauli group
(G(\mathbb{Z})\cap K_{1})/\langle i\rangle
into the Clifford group
G(\mathbb{Z})/\langle i\rangle
.
∎

The
2
-qubit Pauli group has a standard presentation generated by tensors of two matrices chosen from

I=\begin{pmatrix}1&\\
&1\end{pmatrix},\quad X=\begin{pmatrix}&1\\
1&\end{pmatrix},\quad Y=\begin{pmatrix}&-i\\
i&\end{pmatrix},\quad Z=\begin{pmatrix}1&\\
&-1\end{pmatrix}

and the scalars
\pm 1,\pm i
. If we set:

X_{P}:=\begin{pmatrix}1&-1&1&-1\\
1&-i&-1&i\\
i&2-i&-1&1\\
-1&i&i&1-2i\end{pmatrix},

then
X_{P}^{-1}(K_{1}\cap G(\mathbb{Q}))X_{P}
is the Pauli group in this standard presentation.

As in Example
4.3.4
, we can make a choice (possibly without loss of generality conjugating
K^{\prime\prime}
inside
K
; note that conjugation doesn’t change the normal
K_{1}
):

T^{\prime}_{G}:=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{3}{2}-\frac{i}{2}&-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{i}{2}&\frac{1}{2}-\frac{i}{2}&\frac{1}{2}+\frac{i}{2}\\
-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{i}{2}&\frac{3}{2}+\frac{i}{2}&-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{i}{2}&\frac{1}{2}-\frac{i}{2}\\
-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{i}{2}&\frac{1}{2}-\frac{i}{2}&\frac{1}{2}+\frac{i}{2}&-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{i}{2}\\
\frac{1}{2}+\frac{3i}{2}&-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{3i}{2}&\frac{1}{2}+\frac{3i}{2}&-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{i}{2}\end{pmatrix}.

Then,
X_{P}^{-1}T^{\prime}_{G}X_{P}
is the CS gate
4
4

4

In particular, the gate set of
4.5.11
is equivalent to the gate set studied in
[
35
]
. In fact, the exact synthesis algorithm of
[
35
]
can be interpreted as using the exceptional Lie group isomorphism therein to realize the Clifford group as the points of an integral model of
\mathrm{SO}_{6}
and then applying the general Bruhat-Tits theoretic algorithm of Theorem
4.4.1
(2). Here, we instead get the realization as integral points through conjugation by
X_{P}
, which also gives us far stronger covering bounds by automorphic techniques.
, giving Theorem
1.2.5
. We may in fact take the
2
-qubit Clifford group together with any additional matrix
T
such that
T^{\prime}_{G}=X_{P}TX_{P}^{-1}
is in
G(\mathbb{Z}[1/2])
and has Cartan invariant
(1/2,0)
—for example, many other controlled Clifford gates or monomial matrices work.

4.5.5
New Constructions:
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-7})

For
E/F=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-7})/\mathbb{Q}
, Proposition
4.5.13
produces the example in Theorem
1.2.4
.

Proposition 4.5.13
.

For the
4\times 4
Hermitian positive definite matrix

H_{7}:=\begin{pmatrix}7&0&-3i\sqrt{7}&-2i\sqrt{7}\\
0&7&2i\sqrt{7}&-3i\sqrt{7}\\
3i\sqrt{7}&-2i\sqrt{7}&14&0\\
2i\sqrt{7}&3i\sqrt{7}&0&14\\
\end{pmatrix},

the following is an almost golden adelic group:

G:=U_{4}^{\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-7}),H_{7}}/\mathbb{Q},\qquad K^{\prime}:=\{g\in G(\widehat{\mathbb{Z}})\,|\,g\text{ upper triangular}\pmod{(1+\sqrt{-7})/2}\}

It is also almost
\tau
-super-golden at
p=2
for
\tau
a complete chamber.

Proof.

Here,
H_{7}
was chosen so that
U^{E,H_{7}}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
is special at
p=7
and hyperspecial at all other
p
as in Remark
3.2.3
. Plugging in
L
-values

R(G)=5040^{-1}.

As in Proposition
4.5.4
, we can also compute that
|G(\mathbb{Z})|=5040
, though the computation takes significantly longer to run
5
5

5

the norm-
7
vectors again correspond to the
240
roots of the
E_{8}
lattice while the norm-
14
vectors are the
2160
of second-smallest length
. Therefore by lemma
2.3.3
,
G
has class number one.

Next, in the embedding
G_{2}\subseteq\mathrm{GL}_{4}(E_{(1+\sqrt{-7})/2})\times\mathrm{GL}_{4}(E_{(1-\sqrt{-7})/2})
, projection onto the first coordinate is an isomorphism,
K^{\prime}_{2}
is an Iwahori subgroup. This is the stabilizer of a chamber.

Finally, we need to show that
[G(\mathbb{Z}):G(\mathbb{Z})\cap K^{\prime}]=[G(\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}):K^{\prime}]
. The Iwahori in
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
has index
315
by standard formulas for general linear groups over finite fields. Therefore, it suffices to check that
|G(\mathbb{Z})\cap K^{\prime}|=5040/315=16
, which holds.
∎

The chamber
\tau
is standard traversable as in Example
4.2.6
(1). This gives a super-golden gate set with growth rate

|S_{2}^{[\ell]}|\asymp 16^{\ell}.

The group
C_{2}
is the alternating group
A_{7}
(resp.
\widetilde{C}_{2}
is the double cover usually denoted
2.A_{7}
). Inside this
\Gamma
(resp.
\widetilde{\Gamma}
) is the Sylow-
2
subgroup. Finally, we can write down explicit generators

\displaystyle T_{2}:=\left\{T_{K}^{i}:=\begin{pmatrix}-o&2o+2&-o+2&o-2\\
-2&o-2&0&-o\\
1&4o-4&o+2&-o-2\\
-o+2&o-4&o&-o\end{pmatrix}^{i},1\leq i<4\right\}

\displaystyle C_{2}:=\left\langle\begin{pmatrix}0&-2o+1&1&-2\\
-o+1&-o&0&-1\\
o+1&-2&-o+1&o\\
-1&-4&-o+1&2o-1\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}2&-o+1&-o+1&-o\\
1&2o&-1&-o+2\\
-o+1&-3&-o-1&o-1\\
-2o+2&-o+2&-1&-o-2\end{pmatrix}\right\rangle

where
o=(1+\sqrt{-7})/2
. The element
T_{K}
acts as an order-4 rotation cyclically permuting the vertices of a chamber.

4.5.6
New Constructions:
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-2})

For
E/F=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-2})/\mathbb{Q}
, we have:

Proposition 4.5.14
.

For the
4\times 4
Hermitian positive definite matrix

H_{8}:=\begin{pmatrix}4&0&-\sqrt{-2}&-2+2\sqrt{-2}\\
0&4&-2-2\sqrt{-2}&-\sqrt{-2}\\
\sqrt{-2}&-2+2\sqrt{-2}&4&0\\
-2-2\sqrt{-2}&\sqrt{-2}&0&4\\
\end{pmatrix}

the following is an almost golden adelic group:

G:=U_{4}^{\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-2}),H_{8}}/\mathbb{Q},\qquad K^{\prime}:=\left\{g\in G(\widehat{\mathbb{Z}})\,\middle|\,g\equiv\begin{pmatrix}*&*&*&*\\
*&*&*&*\\
*&*&*&*\\
0&0&0&*\end{pmatrix}\pmod{1+\sqrt{-2}}\right\}

It also acts transitively on the set of edges of type
(01)
—i.e. connecting vertices corresponding to adjacent points in the affine Dynkin diagram for
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
.

Proof.

As in the other examples,
H_{8}
was chosen so that
U^{E,H_{8}}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
is special at
p=2
and hyperspecial at all other
p
as in Remark
3.2.3
. We also check
6
6

6

we again have that the norm-
4
vectors with respect to
H_{8}
correspond to roots of the
E_{8}
lattice
that
R(G)^{-1}=3840=|G(\mathbb{Z})|
so
G(\mathbb{Z})
has class number one by lemma
2.3.3
.

Next, in the embedding
G_{3}\subseteq\mathrm{GL}_{4}(E_{1+\sqrt{-2}})\times\mathrm{GL}_{4}(E_{1-\sqrt{-2}})
, projection onto the first coordinate is an isomorphism. Therefore,
K^{\prime}
is a parahoric subgroup stabilizing an edge of type
(01)
.

Finally, we check that
[G(\mathbb{Z}):G(\mathbb{Z})\cap K^{\prime}]=[G(\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}):K^{\prime}]=[G_{3}:K^{\prime}_{3}]
. For this,
G_{3}/K^{\prime}_{3}
is projective
3
-space over
\mathbb{F}_{3}
which has
40
points and we can compute that
|G(\mathbb{Z})\cap K^{\prime}|=96=3840/40
.
∎

Note that an edge of type
(01)
isn’t traversable as in
4.2.3
, so Proposition
4.5.14
doesn’t a priori give a set of super-golden gates. However, in a stroke of good luck,
\bar{\Lambda}_{3}^{K^{\prime}}
does contain an element

T_{8}:=\begin{pmatrix}-2&\sqrt{-2}-1&-\sqrt{-2}-2&-1\\
-\sqrt{-2}+3&0&0&-2\sqrt{-2}\\
2\sqrt{-2}&0&0&\sqrt{-2}+3\\
2\sqrt{-2}+2&0&\sqrt{-2}-1&2\end{pmatrix}

acting as a cyclic rotation on the vertices of a chamber containing the point
v_{0}
stabilized by
G(\mathbb{Z}_{3})
. Therefore, this example can actually produce a super-golden gate set, even though it does not fit into our general framework.

We also have an example involving more complicated subgroups at primes over
2
:

Proposition 4.5.15
.

For the
4\times 4
Hermitian positive definite matrix

H_{2}:=\begin{pmatrix}2&0&\sqrt{-2}&0\\
0&2&0&\sqrt{-2}\\
-\sqrt{-2}&0&2&0\\
0&-\sqrt{-2}&0&2\\
\end{pmatrix},

the following is an almost golden adelic group:

G:=U_{4}^{\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-2}),H_{2}}/\mathbb{Q}.

Proof.

Here we use Proposition
4.3.15
on
G=U_{4}^{\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-2}),H_{2}}
.
Note that
E=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-2})
is of class number one.
We consider
\mathfrak{p}=3
, which is a split prime in
E
,
3=(1+\sqrt{-2})(1-\sqrt{-2})
, and since
\det H=4
we get that
G({\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{p}})
is hyperspecial.
We compute

\sum_{i=0}^{4}{4\choose i}_{3}=1+40+130+40+1=212.

A direct calculation via computer yields
|G(\mathbb{Z})|=512
and

|\{g\in M_{4}(\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{-2}]\,|\,g^{*}H_{2}g=3H_{2}\}|=108544=512\cdot 212.

Hence, by Proposition
4.3.15
, we get the claim.
∎

4.5.7
New Constructions: Non-Quasisplit

Finally, we present examples where
G
is non-quasisplit at some places:

Proposition 4.5.16
.

For the
4\times 4
Hermitian positive definite matrix

H_{3R}:=\diag(1,1,1,2),

Define

G:=U_{4}^{\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-3}),H_{3R}}/\mathbb{Q}.

Then
G
is non-quasisplit at
p=2,3
and
G(\widehat{\mathbb{Z}})
is almost golden.

Proof.

Non-quasisplitness can be tested by computing
\mathrm{disc}(H_{3R})
. In addition
G(\widehat{\mathbb{Z}})
was chosen as in Remark
3.2.3
so that it is extraspecial at
3
, special at
2
, and hyperspecial at all other places.

Next,
G(\mathbb{Z})
is easily computable as
\mu_{6}^{4}\rtimes S^{3}
where
\mu_{6}
is the group of
6
th roots of unity and the
S_{3}
acts on the first three coordinates. This has size
6^{5}=7776
and we may also compute
R(G)=7776^{-1}
so the result follows by lemma
2.3.3
.
∎

The gate sets
S_{2}
and
S_{3}
from
4.5.16
are particularly interesting:
\langle S_{2}\rangle
acts transitively on every other vertex of an infinite
9
-regular tree while
\langle S_{3}\rangle
acts transitively on the degree-
10
vertices of an infinite
(10,4)
-biregular tree.

Proposition 4.5.17
.

For the
4\times 4
Hermitian positive definite matrix

H_{4R}:=\diag(1,1,1,3),

define

G:=U_{4}^{\mathbb{Q}(i),H_{4R}}/\mathbb{Q}.

Then
G
is non-quasisplit at
p=2,3
and
G(\widehat{\mathbb{Z}})
is almost golden.

Proof.

This is a similar argument to
4.5.16
with
R(G)^{-1}=|G(\mathbb{Z})|=1536
.
∎

In the case of
4.5.17
,
\langle S_{2}\rangle
acts transitively on the degree-
5
vertices of an infinite
(5,3)
-biregular tree while
\langle S_{3}\rangle
acts transitively on every other vertex of an infinite
28
-regular tree.

Remark 4.5.18
.

The example in
4.5.16
can be modified to give a
K^{\prime}
that is
\tau
-super-golden at
2
for
\tau
a
single
edge in
\mathcal{B}(G_{2})
. However, this
\tau
is far less convenient than Example
4.2.6
(6) since we do not have an element in
G_{2}
that flips an edge—it requires
8
additional elements
t_{i}
corresponding to the other
8
edges neighboring at a vertex.

Moving to the setting of
GU_{4}^{\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-3}),H_{3R}}
may provide the desired single-edge flip. However, we cannot prove our automorphic bound
8.3.3
for
GU
since we do not then have access to the endoscopic classification. Therefore we postpone analyzing this until the requisite automorphic results are known to be true.

4.5.8
Clifford Gates

We end this section by discussing the well-studied and standard Clifford+T gates
and their generalizations from the point of view of arithmetic unitary
groups and golden adelic groups (see
[
81
]
and
the references there).

The classical Clifford+T gates are a finite set of unitary
2\times 2

matrices with coefficients in the ring
\mathbb{Z}\left[\sqrt{-1},\sqrt{2}^{\pm 1}\right]
.
By
[
52
]
, the set of elements in
U\left(2\right)

that are synthesisable (i.e. generated by matrix multiplication and
tensoring) by the Clifford+T gates is precisely the full
2
-arithmetic
group
\Lambda
of unitary
2\times 2
matrices with coefficients
in the ring
\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{-1},\sqrt{2}^{\pm 1}]
; i.e.

\Lambda:=G(\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{2}^{\pm 1}])
where

G:=U_{2}^{\mathbb{Q}[\sqrt{2},\sqrt{-1}],I}/\mathbb{Q}[\sqrt{2}].

The group
G
has class number one and moreover gives rise to super
golden gate sets (see Section 4.1.3 in
[
70
]
).

The multiqubit Clifford+T gates are a finite set of unitary
2^{n}\times 2^{n}

matrices with coefficients in the ring
\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{-1},\sqrt{2}^{\pm 1}]
.
By
[
34
]
, the group of elements in
U\left(2^{n}\right)

that are synthesisable by the multiqubit Clifford+T gates is
\Lambda:=G(\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{2}^{\pm 1}])
,
where

G:=U_{2^{n}}^{\mathbb{Q}[\sqrt{2},\sqrt{-1}],I}/\mathbb{Q}[\sqrt{2}].

However, for
n\geq 2
, the group
G
is not of class number one—in particular
\Lambda
does not act transitively on the special
vertices of the corresponding Bruhat-Tits building of
G(\mathbb{Q}[\sqrt{2}]_{\sqrt{2}})
.

The Clifford+cyclotomic gates are a finite set of unitary
2\times 2

matrices with coefficients in the ring
R_{m}:=\mathbb{Z}\left[\zeta_{m}\right]\left[\frac{1}{2}\right]
,
where
\zeta_{m}=e^{\frac{2\pi i}{m}}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
. These
matrices sit inside the full
2
-arithmetic subgroup of

G:=U_{2}^{\mathbb{Q}[\zeta_{m}],I}/\mathbb{Q}[\zeta_{m}+\zeta_{m}^{-1}].

By
[
31
,
43
]
, the group of elements
in
U\left(2\right)
that are synthesizable by the Clifford+cyclotomic
gates is a
2
-arithmetic subgroup of
G
if and only if
m=4,8,12,16

or
24
. Note that being a
2
-arithmetic subgroup only implies that
the class number is finite, not necessarily that the class number
is one (it is one for
m=4,8
).

4.6
Super-Golden Gate Set Comparisons

The case of super-golden gates on
PU(4)
is particularly interesting for applications. We compare the covering rate produced by our
2
-qubit super-golden gates versus using
1
-qubit super-golden gates together with specific other
2
-qubit gates.

Assume first we have a set of super-golden gates
S_{\mathfrak{p}}
for
PU(4)
such that
|S_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}|\asymp R^{\ell}
. We want to find the minimum
\ell
such that there is an element of
S_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}
within distance
\epsilon
of some
A\in PU(4)
; in other words, such that
B(A,\epsilon^{15})\cap S_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}\neq\emptyset
. Up to decreasing
C
by an arbitrarily small factor, the covering property gives

\ell\leq\log_{R}(\epsilon^{-15})+A=15(\log_{2}R)^{-1}\log_{2}(1/\epsilon)+A

for some constant
A
.

On the other hand, assume we have a set of super-golden gates
S_{\mathfrak{p}}
for
PU(2)
such that
|S_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}|\asymp C^{\ell}
. There is an embedding
L=U(2)\times U(2)/U(1)\hookrightarrow PU(4)
that restricts to two embeddings
L_{1},L_{2}:U(2)\hookrightarrow PU(4)
. The paper
[
92
]
gives a way to write elements of
PU(4)
as a product of a minimal number of elements in
L_{1}\cup L_{2}
together with copies of a fixed additional matrix (called
B
). This requires
6
elements of
L_{1}\cup L_{2}
.

To approximate
A\in PU(4)
within distance
\epsilon
, each of the factors
A_{i}
in the
L_{i}
needs to be approximated within distance
\approx\epsilon/6
; in other words we need to find an element of
S_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}
in
B(A_{i},1/\epsilon^{3})
(together with a phase shift). This implies that we need
\ell
gates in total with

\ell\leq 6\log_{R}(\epsilon^{-3})+A\approx 18(\log_{2}R)^{-1}\log_{2}(1/\epsilon)+A

for some constant
A
.

The CNOT gate is sometimes preferred instead of
B
since it is contained in the
2
-qubit Clifford group. Three copies of CNOT together with
8
elements of
L_{1}\cup L_{2}
suffice to give any element of
PU(4)
(see
[
87
]
). This approximation strategy therefore instead requires
\ell
gates with:

\ell\leq 24(\log_{2}R)^{-1}\log_{2}(1/\epsilon)+A.

In Table
4.1
, we summarize details of selected gate sets constructed from either
1
-qubit super-golden sets from
[
70
]
or the
2
-qubit sets here. Each can be thought of as a finite group together with some extra finite-order elements—which ones are relevant for how well the gates can be (or could be with future work) realized in a fault-tolerant way with respect to some quantum error correction scheme.

More specifically, for the added gates to be implementable with the teleportation procedure of
[
37
]
, we need the added
T
to satisfy that
TQT^{-1}\subseteq K\cap G(\mathbb{Q})
for some group
Q
that linearly spans all
4\times 4
complex matrices (equivalently,
Q
is an irreducible representation of itself). In the simply traversable case (recall Definition
4.2.3
—this is satisfied for all examples considered here), it suffices to check that
K^{\prime}\cap G(\mathbb{Q})
spans. For compatibility with the current stabilizer-code paradigm for fault-tolerant implementation, we need the much stronger property that the finite group is Clifford and that the added gates are in the 3rd level of the Clifford hierarchy. We mark these cases in Table
4.1
.

We also present, as in the calculations above, the growth rate for the gate set
L
and the constant determining how efficiently elements of
PU(4)
can be approximated. We emphasize that this “covering efficiency” is the theoretical optimal. The best known practically computable approximations are a factor of
7/3
larger in the
1
-qubit cases as in
[
10
]
. The
2
-qubit cases here are worse by a much larger factor since the algorithm in Theorem
4.4.1
(3) is very far from optimized.

Remark 4.6.1
.

Expanding the notion of super-golden gates may be worthwhile. As one example, we can consider the case of
K^{\infty}_{2}\subseteq K^{\infty}_{1}\subseteq G^{\infty}
and place
\mathfrak{p}
such that

•

K^{\infty}_{1}
has class number 1,

•

K^{\infty}_{2}
has class number
n
,

•

K_{1,\mathfrak{p}}
is special,

•

K_{2,\mathfrak{p}}
is a stabilizer of
\tau
as in Example
4.2.6
(1,3-6).

Then, we get a super-golden gate set requiring
n
different added gates
T_{i}
. The weakening of the class-number-
1
condition on
K^{\infty}_{2}
allows for examples with dramatically better growth rates, though it is unclear whether this is worth the cost of extra
T_{i}
. The best growth rates should come from Example
4.2.6
(3) giving
R=q_{\mathfrak{p}}^{6}
as in Example
4.3.11
.

The best general framework for constructing super-golden gate sets is currently unclear.

5
Automorphic Representations Background

We now enter the second half of the paper: proving the covering property of our golden gate sets using the theory of automorphic representations. In this section we recall some basic facts and notations concerning automorphic representations.

5.1
Automorphic representations

Throughout this section,
F
is a number field with ring of integers

\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{O}_{F}
and adele ring
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{A}_{F}
. Let
v
denote a place
of
F
, let
F_{v}
be the
v
-completion of
F
, and, when
v

is finite, let
\mathcal{O}_{v}
be the ring of integers of
F_{v}
with
uniformizer
\varpi_{v}
and order of residue field
q_{v}:=|\mathcal{O}_{v}/\mathfrak{p}_{v}\mathcal{O}_{v}|
.

Let
G
be a connected reductive group over
F
. For simplicity, assume that the maximal split torus in the center of the real group
G_{\infty}
is trivial so that
G(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A})
has finite volume. Fix a
k
-embedding
G\hookrightarrow GL_{n}(k)
. For any place
v
, denote
G_{v}:=G(k_{v})
. When
v
is finite, denote
K_{v}=G(k_{v})\cap GL_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{v})
and, for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
, denote
K_{n}(\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{m}):=\ker\left(K_{v}\rightarrow GL_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{v}/\mathfrak{p}_{v}^{m}\mathcal{O}_{v})\right)
.

Consider the right regular
G(\mathbb{A})
-representation on

L^{2}(G(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A}))
. An
F
-automorphic representation
of
G
is an irreducible
G(\mathbb{A})
-representation
\pi

which is weakly contained in
L^{2}(G(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A}))

and whose central character is unitary. Denote by
\mathcal{AR}(G)
the
set of
F
-automorphic representations of
G
. Consider the decomposition
of
\mathcal{AR}(G)
into its cuspidal
\mathcal{AR}_{cusp}(G)
, residual
\mathcal{AR}_{res}(G)
, discrete
\mathcal{AR}_{\mathrm{disc}}(G)
, and continuous
\mathcal{AR}_{cont}(G)
parts:

\displaystyle\mathcal{AR}(G)=\mathcal{AR}_{cusp}(G)\oplus\mathcal{AR}_{res}(G)\oplus\mathcal{AR}_{cont}(G),

\displaystyle\mathcal{AR}_{\mathrm{disc}}(G)=\mathcal{AR}_{cusp}(G)\oplus\mathcal{AR}_{res}(G).

Any
\pi\in\mathcal{AR}(G)
decomposes as a restricted tensor product
\pi=\otimes_{v}^{{}^{\prime}}\pi_{v}
,
where
\pi_{v}
, called the
local-factor
of
\pi
at
v
, is an irreducible
admissible
G_{v}
-representation (cf.
[
30
]
).
Let
\sigma(\pi_{v})
be the infimum over
\sigma\geq 2
, such that
each
K_{v}
-finite matrix coefficient of
\pi_{v}
is in
L^{\sigma+\epsilon}(G_{v})

for any
\epsilon>0
. Say that
\pi
is tempered at
v
if
\sigma(\pi_{v})=2
.
The Generalized Ramanujan Conjecture for
G=GL_{n}
states the following
(see
[
79
]
):

Conjecture 5.1.1
.

(GRC) Let
F
be a number field,
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
and let
\pi\in\mathcal{AR}_{cusp}(GL_{n})
. Then the local component
\pi_{v}
is tempered at
every place
v
of
F
.

The conjecture is open for any
N\geq 2
and any number field
F
.
However, there are special cases of cuspidal automorphic representations
for which it is a theorem:

Definition 5.1.2
.

Let
\pi\in\mathcal{AR}(n)
. Say that
\pi

is cohomological if, for an Archimdean place
v
of
F
, the
v
-factor
of
\pi
has an infinitesimal character of a finite dimensional representation.
When
F
is totally real (resp. CM), say that
\pi
is
F

self dual (resp.
F
-conjugate self-dual) if it is isomorphic to its (resp.
F
-conjugate of its) contragredient representation
\tilde{\pi}(g):=\pi((g^{t})^{-1})
.

Theorem 5.1.3
.

[
40
,
83
,
20
,
17
]

Let
F
be a CM field,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
\pi\in\mathcal{AR}_{cusp}(GL_{n})
be both cohomological and
F
-conjugate self-dual (see Definition
5.1.2
). Then
\pi_{v}
is tempered at every place
v
of
F
.

Remark 5.1.4
.

For
F=\mathbb{Q}
and
n=2
, this Theorem was first proved by Eichler

[
27
]
, for weight
k=2
, and by Deligne
[
25
]
. For general weights,
[
17
]
gives the result in all cases we need, extending the results of
[
40
]
,
[
20
]
, and
[
83
]
under progressively weaker technical assumptions.

According to the Langland functoriality conjecture, for any
G
