with dual
\widehat{G}\leq GL_{n}
,
the set
\mathcal{AR}(G)
should be encoded in
\mathcal{AR}(GL_{n}/F)
. We therefore begin
by describing the classification of automorphic representations of

G=GL_{n}
over a number field
F
. Fix a global field
F
and,
for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
, denote
\mathcal{AR}(n):=\mathcal{AR}(GL_{n}/F)
and
\mathcal{AR}_{\star}(n):=\mathcal{AR}_{\star}(GL_{n}/F)

for
\star=cusp,\;res\;\mbox{or}\;cont
.

Definition 5.1.5
.

Define an (unrefined) shape of
n
to be a sequence
of pairs of positive integers,
\Box=\left((T_{1},d_{1}),\ldots,(T_{k},d_{k})\right)
,
such that
\sum_{i}T_{i}\cdot d_{i}=n
. Let
M_{\Box}:=\prod_{i=1}^{k}GL_{T_{i}}^{d_{i}}\leq P_{\Box}\leq GL_{n}

be the corresponding Levi (block diagonal) and parabolic (block upper
triangular) subgroups of shape
\Box
.

Theorem 5.1.6
.

[
55
,
66
]

For any shape of
n
,
\Box=\left((T_{i},d_{i})\right)_{i=1}^{k}
,
there is a map,
I_{\Box}\,:\,\prod_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{AR}_{cusp}(T_{i})\rightarrow\mathcal{AR}(n)
,
called the automorphic parabolic induction of shape
\Box
and satisfying:

(1)

For any
\pi\in\mathcal{AR}(n)
, there exist a unique shape
\Box=\left((T_{i},d_{i})\right)_{i=1}^{k}
and a unique (up to order) sequence of cuspidal representations
(\pi_{i})_{i=1}^{k}\in\prod_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{AR}_{cusp}(T_{i})
such that
\pi=I_{\Box}((\pi_{i})_{i=1}^{k})
, in which case
\pi
is said to be of shape
\Box
. Moreover,
\pi
lies in the discrete (resp. cuspidal) part of
\mathcal{AR}(n)
if and only if
k=1
(resp.
k=1
and
d_{1}=1
).

(2)

Let
\pi=I_{\Box}((\pi_{i})_{i=1}^{k})
, where
\Box=\left((T_{i},d_{i})\right)_{i=1}^{k}

and
(\pi_{i})_{i=1}^{k}\in\prod_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{AR}_{cusp}(T_{i})
. Then,
for any place
v
of
k
, the local component
\pi_{v}
is a subqoutient
of the (unitary) parabolic induction

\mbox{Ind}_{P_{\Box}(\mathbb{Q}_{v})}^{GL_{n}(\mathbb{Q}_{v})}\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{k}\left(|\cdot|_{v}^{\frac{d_{i}-1}{2}}\pi_{i,v}\otimes|\cdot|_{v}^{\frac{d_{i}-3}{2}}\pi_{i,v}\otimes\ldots\otimes|\cdot|_{v}^{\frac{1-d_{i}}{2}}\pi_{i,v}\right)\right).

Definition 5.1.7
.

Let
\Box=((T_{i},d_{i}))_{i=1}^{k}
. Then we shorthand
I_{\Box}(\tau_{1},\dotsc,\tau_{k})
by the formal expression

I_{\Box}(\tau_{1},\dotsc,\tau_{k})=:\bigoplus_{i=1}^{k}\tau_{i}[d_{i}].

5.2
Endoscopic Classification and Shapes

The unitary endoscopic classification of
[
63
]
extended to non-quasisplit unitary groups in
[
45
]
lets us decompose
\mathcal{AR}_{\mathrm{disc}}(G)
for our unitary groups
U^{E/F,H}_{n}
into pieces corresponding to shapes
\Box
for
GL_{n}
. Fix CM quadratic extension
E/F
.

Definition 5.2.1
.

An automorphic representation
\bigoplus_{i}\tau_{i}[d_{i}]
of
\Res^{E}_{F}GL_{n}
(this is the same as one of
GL_{n}/E
) is said to be
elliptic
if each
\tau_{i}
is conjugate self-dual and the individual
\tau_{i}[d_{i}]
are all distinct.

Let
\widetilde{\Psi}_{\mathrm{ell}}(n)
be the set of elliptic automorphic representations
\psi
of
\Res^{E}_{F}GL_{n}
. These are usually referred to as elliptic (global) Arthur parameters.

Attached to
\Res^{E}_{F}GL_{n}
are a set of elliptic twisted endoscopic groups
G^{*}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}^{E}_{\mathrm{ell}}(n)
described in
[
63
]
*§2.1. These each come with
L
-embeddings

{}^{L}\!{G}^{*}\hookrightarrow{}^{L}\!{\Res}^{E}_{F}GL_{n}.

We will only care about a specific “simple” element:
U^{+}_{n}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}^{E}_{\mathrm{ell}}(n)
. The main result of
[
63
]
is a decomposition

\widetilde{\Psi}_{\mathrm{ell}}(n)=\bigsqcup_{G^{*}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}^{E}_{\mathrm{ell}}(n)}\Psi_{\mathrm{ell}}(G^{*})

and a description of discrete automorphic representations of each
G^{*}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}^{E}_{\mathrm{ell}}(n)
in terms of
\Psi_{\mathrm{ell}}(G^{*})
.

The paper
[
45
]
generalizes Mok’s classification to “extended pure inner” forms
G
of each
G^{*}
. These are enumerated in
[
45
]
*§0.3.3. In particular, the extended pure inner forms of
U^{+}_{n}
include all the definite unitary groups
U^{E,H}_{n}
we consider here. We recall that if
G
is an inner form of
G^{*}
, then
{}^{L}\!{G}={}^{L}\!{G}^{*}
.

We recall all parts of
[
45
]
’s classification that are needed to explain our results and point readers to
[
23
, §2]
for a full summary geared towards trace formula applications.

Theorem 5.2.2

(
[
45
]
partial summary of main result)
.

Let
G^{*}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}^{E}_{\mathrm{ell}}(n)
and
G
an extended pure inner form of
G^{*}
. Then

(1)

To each
\psi\in\Psi_{\mathrm{ell}}(G^{*})
there is subset
\Pi^{G}_{\psi}
called the (global) Arthur packet such that

\mathcal{AR}_{\mathrm{disc}}(G)=\bigsqcup_{\psi\in\Psi_{\mathrm{ell}}(G^{*})}\Pi^{G}_{\psi}.

This
\Pi^{G}_{\psi}
is empty unless
\psi
satisfies a condition of being relevant as in
[
45
, §0.4,1.2]
.

(2)

Let
\psi=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{k}\tau_{i}[d_{i}]
and fix a place
V
. Through the local Langlands correspondence, each
\tau_{i}
is associated to a (local)
L
-parameter

\tau_{i}:WD_{F_{v}}\to{}^{L}\!{(}\Res^{E}_{F}GL_{T_{i}})_{v}

from the Weil-Deligne group of
F_{v}
. Define the local
A
-parameter

\psi_{v}=\bigoplus_{i}\tau_{i}\boxtimes[d_{i}]:WD_{F_{v}}\times\mathrm{SL}_{2}\to{}^{L}\!{(}\Res^{E}_{F}GL_{n})_{v}

where
[d_{i}]
is the
d_{i}
-dimensional representation of
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
. Then
\psi_{v}
factors through
{}^{L}\!{G}
.

(3)

There is a finite set of representations
\Pi^{G}_{\psi_{v}}
, depending only on
\psi_{v}
, called the local
A
-packet such that for all
\pi\in\Pi^{G}_{\psi}
, we have
\pi_{v}\in\Pi^{G}_{\psi_{v}}
.

(4)

If
\psi_{v}
is generic (i.e. all
d_{i}=1
), then the assignment
\psi_{v}\to\Pi_{\psi_{v}}
satisfies all the desired properties of a local Langlands correspondence. In particular:

(a)

If
v|\infty
, then the infinitesimal character of
\pi_{v}\in\Pi_{\psi_{v}}
is the same as that of
\psi_{v}
through the embedding
\widehat{G}\hookrightarrow GL_{n}\mathbb{C}\times GL_{n}\mathbb{C}
.

(b)

\pi_{v}\in\Pi_{\psi_{v}}
is tempered if and only if
\psi_{v}
is (for
v\nmid\infty
, this means that the
\tau_{i}
are bounded/correspond to unitary supercuspidals).

We can now make our key definition, following
[
24
, §5]
:

Definition 5.2.3
.

Let
\Box
be a shape for
GL_{n}/E
. If
\pi\in\mathcal{AR}_{\mathrm{disc}}(G)
with parameter
\psi\in\Psi_{\mathrm{ell}}(n)
such that
\psi\in\Box
, then we say
\pi
has shape
\Box
or
\pi\in\Box
.

Remark 5.2.4
.

The definition of shape in
[
24
, §5]
is actually a list of triples
\Box=((T_{i},d_{i},\eta_{i}))_{i=1}^{k}
for some signs
\eta_{i}
. In general, the
\eta_{i}
are needed to determine the
G
such that
\psi\in\Psi_{\mathrm{ell}}(G^{*})
for all
\psi\in\Box
. However, when
G^{*}
is a simple twisted endoscopic group (e.g.
U^{+}_{n}
), there is always a unique choice of
\eta_{i}
that determines
\psi\in\Psi_{\mathrm{ell}}(G^{*})
.

In our case, we require a priori that
\psi\in\Psi_{\mathrm{ell}}(U^{+}_{n})
and can therefore ignore the data of the
\eta_{i}
. Nevertheless, the induction in the black-boxed proof of Theorem
7.1.1
requires keeping track of them.

As in
[
24
]
, we also associate to shape
\Box=((T_{i},d_{i}))_{i=1}^{k}
the group

G_{F}(\Box):=\prod_{i=1}^{k}U^{+}_{T_{i}}.

(5.1)

This is not in general an element of
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{ell}}(n)
and can be thought of as the smallest group through which
\psi\in\Box
functorially factor through. It will appear in bounds on sizes of automorphic families intersected with
\Box
.

Finally,

Definition 5.2.5
.

Let

L^{2}_{\Box}:=\bigoplus_{\pi\in\Box}m_{\pi}\pi,

where
m_{\pi}
is the multiplicity of
\pi
in
L^{2}(G(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A}))
, and

\Proj_{\Box}:L^{2}(G(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A}))\to L^{2}_{\Box}

be the orthogonal projection operator.

If
K
is an open compact subgroup of
G(\mathbb{A})
, we will also use
\Proj_{\Box}
to denote the restriction of this projection operator to the subspace
L^{2}(G(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A}))^{K}
.

5.3
Infinitesimal Characters

5.3.1
Definitions and Relation to Shapes

Formulas later on will involve infinitesimal characters. Consider again
G
that is an extended pure inner form of
G^{*}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{ell}}(n)
.

Any finite dimensional representation
\pi_{\infty}
of
G_{\infty}
has an associated infinitesimal character
\lambda
that is a semisimple conjugacy class in
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}_{\infty}
.
Since
G^{*}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{ell}}(n)
, there is a map
\widehat{G}\hookrightarrow GL_{n}(\mathbb{C})\times GL_{n}(\mathbb{C})
restricted from
{}^{L}\!{G}\hookrightarrow{}^{L}\!{\Res}^{E}_{F}GL_{n}
. It is in particular determined by its first coordinate so the infinitesimal character can also be represented by a semisimple conjugacy class in
\Mat_{n\times n}(F_{\infty}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C})
. This can then be represented as an unordered sequence of eigenvalues,

\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dotsc,\lambda_{N}),

with each
\lambda_{i}=(\lambda_{i,v})_{v|\infty}\in F_{\infty}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}
.

It is also sometimes useful to package the tuple
\lambda_{v}=(\lambda_{1,v},...,\lambda_{n,v})
as the generating function
\sum_{j}X^{\lambda_{j,v}}
, which, by abuse of notation, we will also denote by
\lambda_{v}
. In this way, if
\bigoplus_{i}\tau_{i,v}[d_{i}]
is a local Arthur parameter such that each
\tau_{i,v}
has infinitesimal character
\lambda_{v}^{(i)}=\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i}}X^{\lambda_{j,v}^{(i)}}
, then we have infinitesimal character assignment

\left(\bigoplus_{i}\tau_{i,v}[d_{i}]\right)_{\infty}\mapsto\Box((\lambda_{v}^{(i)})_{i}):=\sum_{i}\lambda_{v}^{(i)}\sum_{l=1}^{d_{i}}X^{\frac{d_{i}+1}{2}-l}.

(5.2)

It can be seen from this that the character of
\tau[d]
determines that of
\tau
.

Definition 5.3.1
.

If
\lambda
matches the infinitesimal character of a finite-dimensional representation, we say that it is
regular integral
.

Regular integral is equivalent to two conditions:

•

(Regular) For each
v
, the
\lambda_{i,v}
are distinct.

•

(Integral) If
N
is even, the
\lambda_{i,v}\in\mathbb{Z}
and if
N
is odd, the
\lambda_{i,v}\in\mathbb{Z}+1/2
.

We without loss of generality order regular integral
\lambda
:

\lambda_{1,v}>\cdots>\lambda_{N,v}.

We also make some convenient defintions:

Definition 5.3.2
.

Let
\lambda_{\Box}
be the set of possible regular, integral infinitesimal characters of
\psi_{\infty}
with
\psi\in\Box
(i.e. the image of (
5.2
)). We say
\lambda\in\Box
as shorthand for
\lambda\in\lambda_{\Box}
.

Let
\Box^{-1}(\lambda)
be the set of possible assignments of infinitesimal characters
(\lambda^{(i)})_{i=1}^{k}
to each
(\tau_{i,\infty})_{i=1}^{k}
so that
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{k}\tau_{i,\infty}[d_{i}]
has infinitesimal character
\lambda
.

5.3.2
Norms of Infinitesimal Characters

Choose distinguished infinite place
v_{0}
and consider infinitesimal character
\lambda
of
G_{v_{0}}
for
G
an extended pure inner form of
G^{*}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{ell}}(n)
.

Let
\Phi_{+}(G)
be the standard set of positive coroots of
G_{v_{0}}
. We will need to compare/recall three different norms of
\lambda_{v_{0}}
:

•

The dimension of the finite dimensional representation corresponding to
\lambda
:

\dim\lambda:=C_{G,1}\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi_{+}(G)}\langle\alpha,\lambda\rangle,

•

\lambda
paired with itself with by the Killing form:

\|\lambda\|:=C_{G,2}\left(\sum_{\alpha\in\Phi_{+}(G)}\langle\alpha,\lambda\rangle^{2}\right)^{1/2},

•

A minimum

m(\lambda):=\min_{\alpha\in\Phi_{+}(G)}\langle\alpha,\lambda\rangle=\min_{v|\infty}\min_{1\leq i\leq N-1}(\lambda_{i,v}-\lambda_{i+1,v}),

(5.3)

where the constants
C_{G,i}
only depend on
G
.

Given unrefined shape
\Box
, we can also define

\dim_{\Box}\lambda:=\max_{(\lambda_{i})_{i}\in\Box^{-1}(\lambda)}\prod_{i}\dim\lambda_{i}.

Note that this can be
0
if
\Box^{-1}(\lambda)
is empty. All such definitions can be made analogously for
G_{\infty}
as a whole.

Given a group
G
, there are some key dimensions to keep track of
N_{G}=\dim G
,
r_{G}=\rank G
, and
N^{\mathrm{der}}_{G}=\dim G^{\mathrm{der}}
. From these we can compute the number of positive roots:

P_{G}:=\frac{1}{2}(N_{G}-r_{G}).

As some bounds (recalling the definition (
5.1
) of
G_{F}(\Box)
):

Lemma 5.3.3
.

\dim_{\Box}\lambda\leq(\dim\lambda)m(\lambda)^{P_{G_{F}(\Box)}-P_{G}}.

Proof.

The factors
\langle\alpha,\lambda\rangle
in the Weyl dimension formula for
\dim_{\Box}\lambda
are always a subset of those in
\dim\lambda
. However,
\dim_{\Box}\lambda
has
P_{G}-P_{G_{F}(\Box)}
fewer factors.
∎

Lemma 5.3.4
.

\dim_{\Box}\lambda\leq C\|\lambda\|^{P_{G_{F}(\Box)}}

for some constant
C
depending only on
G
and
\Box
.

Proof.

\dim_{\Box}\lambda
is a product of some subset of size
P_{G_{F}(\Box)}
of the
\langle\alpha,\lambda\rangle
for
\alpha\in\Phi_{+}(G)
. Therefore, by the RMS-AM-GM math-contest inequality,
(\dim_{\Box}\lambda)^{1/P_{G_{F}(\Box)}}
is bounded above by the root-mean-square of this subset. This is further bounded above by a constant times
\|\lambda\|
where the constant depends only on
P_{G_{F}(\Box)}
and
P_{G}
.
∎

For
\Box=\Sigma_{\eta}
, the bound
5.3.4
has the optimal exponent on
\|\lambda\|
when there is
C
such that
C\min_{\alpha\in\Phi_{+}(G)}\langle\alpha,\lambda\rangle\geq\max_{\alpha\in\Phi_{+}(G)}\langle\alpha,\lambda\rangle
. This is an asymptotically positive proportion of all
\lambda
in a
\|\cdot\|
-ball as the ball’s radius goes to infinity.

However, the
\lambda\in\Box
do not satisfy this property if
\Box
has non-trivial
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
as some of the
\langle\alpha,\lambda\rangle
are then bounded. Therefore, we will also need a slight variant of the bound:

Lemma 5.3.5
.

Choose constant
m
and subset
S\subseteq\Phi_{+}(G)
. Then for all
\lambda
such that
\langle\alpha,\lambda\rangle\leq m
for all
\alpha\in S
,

\dim\lambda\leq Cm^{|S|}\|\lambda\|^{P_{G}-|S|}

for some constant
C
depending only on
G
and
|S|
.

Proof.

This is a slight variant of the argument of lemma
5.3.4
where we apply RMS-AM-GM to the set of
\langle\alpha,\lambda\rangle
for
\alpha\in\Phi_{+}(G)-S
.
∎

Applying this to
\lambda_{\Box}
, define for
\Box=((T_{i},d_{i}))_{i}
a correction

e(\Box):=\sum_{i}\frac{1}{2}T_{i}d_{i}(d_{i}-1).

(5.4)

Then we get our tightening:

Corollary 5.3.6
.

Choose unrefined shape
\Box
. Then for all
\lambda\in\Box
and all
\delta>0
,

\dim\lambda\leq C\|\lambda\|^{P_{G}-e(\Box)}

for some constant
C
depending only on
G
and
\Box
.

Proof.

By inspecting formula (
5.2
), we see that for each
(T_{i},d_{i})
pair making up
\Box
, any
\lambda\in\Box
has
1/2T_{i}d_{i}(d_{i}-1)
different
\alpha\in\Phi_{+}
with
\langle\alpha,\lambda\rangle\leq d_{i}-1
. The result then follows from lemma
5.3.5
after noting that the
m
and
|S|
just depend on
\Box
.
∎

5.4
Automorphic Families and the Density Hypothesis

In this subsection, we introduce the notion of an automorphic family
(following
[
76
]
) and the statements of the Ramanujan
conjecture and density hypothesis for such automorphic families (following

[
77
,
78
]
).

Recall our notations
f\lesssim g
if for any
\epsilon>0
, there exists
c_{\epsilon}>0
such that
f(x)\leq c_{\epsilon}\cdot\max\{g(x)^{1+\epsilon},g(x)^{1-\epsilon}\}
for any
x\geq 0
and
f\sim g
if both
f\lesssim g
and
g\lesssim f
.

Definition 5.4.1
.

Let
G/F
be a reductive group over a number field. A (discrete)
automorphic family

\mathcal{F}
for
G
is a weighted subset of
\mathcal{AR}_{\mathrm{disc}}(G)
: i.e. a function

\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{AR}_{\mathrm{disc}}(G)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}.

Example 5.4.2
.

Let
K^{\prime}<G^{\infty}
be compact open. Then the family of automorphic forms at level
K^{\prime}
is

\mathcal{F}_{K^{\prime}}(\pi)=m_{\pi}\dim\left((\pi^{\infty})^{K^{\prime}}\right).

This models the vector space of automorphic forms on
G
of level
K^{\prime}

Definition 5.4.3
.

We say automorphic family
\mathcal{F}
satisfies the
Ramanujan conjecture
if all
\pi\in\mathcal{AR}_{\mathrm{disc}}(G)
with
\mathcal{F}(\pi)\neq 0
are tempered.

If
\mathcal{F}
is the family of all cuspidal automorphic representations, this is called the “naïve Ramanujan conjecture” and it was shown to be false even just on
\mathrm{Sp}_{4}
in
[
42
]
. The expected correction is that Ramanujan holds for the families of
generic
automorphic representations—this is the generalized Ramanujan conjecture. In the case of
GL_{n}
, cuspidal implies generic so this difference is irrelevant.

Definition 5.4.4
.

Let
G/F
be a reductive group over a number field. An asymptotic family
\mathcal{F}
for
G
is an indexed sequence of automorphic families
(\mathcal{F}_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
together with a “conductor” function
m:\Lambda\to\mathbb{R}
such that:

•

Each
\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}
has finite total weight.

•

The size of the support of
\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}
goes to infinity as
m(\lambda)\to\infty
.

Example 5.4.5
.

Let
K^{\prime}
a compact open subgroup of
G^{\infty}
. Then the
weight-aspect family of level-
K^{\prime}
automorphic forms
on
G
is

\mathcal{F}_{K^{\prime},\lambda}(\pi)=m_{\pi}\mathbf{1}_{\text{infchar}(\pi_{\infty})=\lambda}\dim\left((\pi^{\infty})^{K^{\prime}}\right)

indexed over the set of regular, integral infinitesimal characters
\lambda
of
G_{\infty}
. Its conductor function is the
m
from (
5.3
).

This models the space of automorphic forms on
G
of level
K^{\prime}
as the infinitesimal character at infinity gets larger and larger.

Example 5.4.6
.

Let
I\subseteq\infty
contain all infinite places at which
G
is non-compact. Let
K=K^{\infty}G_{\infty\setminus I}
for open compact
K^{\infty}\subseteq G^{\infty}
. Then the connected component of the identity in
G(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A})/K
is
\Gamma\backslash G_{I}
for some discrete, cofinite volume
\Gamma
.

Pick an invariant metric on
G_{I}
and let
B(\delta)
be the ball of volume
\delta
around the identity in
\Gamma\backslash G_{I}
. Then define the
\delta
-ball family in
G_{I}
by:

\mathcal{F}_{\delta}(\pi):=m_{\pi}\frac{\|\Proj_{\pi_{I}}(\mathbf{1}_{B(\delta)})\|_{2}^{2}}{\|\mathbf{1}_{B(\delta)}\|_{2}^{2}}\mathbf{1}_{\pi_{\infty\setminus I}\text{ triv.}}\dim\left((\pi^{\infty})^{K^{\infty}}\right)=m_{\pi}\frac{1}{\|\mathbf{1}_{B(\delta)}\|_{2}^{2}}\tr_{\pi}((f_{\epsilon}\star f_{\epsilon}^{*})\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{G_{\infty\setminus I}}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{K}),

with
m(\delta)=1/\delta
.

This models the decompositions of the indicators of smaller and smaller balls in the automorphic spectrum.

Definition 5.4.7
.

We say an asymptotic family
\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}
eventually satisfies the Ramanujan conjecture if there
L
such that
\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}
satisfies the Ramanujan conjecture whenever
m(\lambda)\geq L
.

Fix place
v
so that
(G_{\mathrm{sc}})_{v}
has no anisotropic factors. We say an asymptotic family
\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}
satisfies the density hypothesis at
v
if for all
\sigma\geq 2
and
\epsilon>0
,

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\mathcal{AR}_{\mathrm{disc}}(G)\\
\sigma(\pi,v)\geq\sigma\end{subarray}}\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}(\pi)\lesssim\left(\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{AC}(G)}\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}(\pi)\right)^{1-\frac{2}{\sigma}}\left(\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{AR}_{\mathrm{disc}}(G)}\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}(\pi)\right)^{\frac{2}{\sigma}},

where
\mathcal{AC}(G)
is the set of automorphic characters (
1
-dimensional automorphic representations of
G
) and
\lesssim
is interpreted asymptotically in
m(\lambda)
.

Note that
\sigma(\pi_{v})=\infty
is equivalent to
\pi_{v}
being a character which, under our conditions on
G_{v}
, is further equivalent to
\pi
being a character (see e.g.
[
49
]
*Lem 6.2). Therefore, this can be thought of as an interpolation between the case
\sigma=2
and
\sigma=\infty
.

The automorphic density hypothesis was raised as a conjecture in
[
77
,
78
]
as a possible substitute for the failure of the naïve Ramanujan conjecture. In recent years this conjecture was proven in several special instances
[
11
,
59
,
60
,
36
,
80
]
. Here, we will specifically be applying methods from
[
23
,
24
]
.

Example 5.4.8
.

Let
G
be one of the
U^{E,H}_{n}
. Then weight-aspect families on
G
can be seen to eventually satisfy the Ramanujan conjecture through the endoscopic classification
[
45
]
: if
m(\lambda)\gg 1
, then formula (
5.2
) shows that all
\pi
with infinitesimal character
\lambda
at infinity are necessarily of shape
\Box=((T_{i},d_{i}))_{i=1}^{k}
with all
d_{i}=1
(i.e. they have generic parameters). Then, Theorem
5.1.3
can be used to show that they are all tempered.

Example 5.4.9
.

The density hypothesis for a slight variant of the
\delta
-ball family will be the key input towards proving the optimal covering property for our set of gates.

6
Matrix Coefficient Decay

We next need to understand how the shape of an
A
-parameter of an automorphic representation controls the decay of the matrix coefficients of its local components at finite places. This will require the very serious black-box inputs of Theorem
5.1.3
and results from explicit constructions of
A
-packets.

Fix some finite place
v
of quadratic extension
E/F
and unitary group
G=U_{n}^{E/H}
. Assume first that
v
is non-split. A much simpler version of this argument works for
v
split (see Remark
6.4.3
)—we only present the full details of the more complicated non-split case for brevity.

6.1
Exponents

Fix a minimal parabolic
P_{0}
of
G_{v}
containing minimal Levi
M_{0}
. We will consider the set of standard Levi’s
M\supseteq M_{0}
and standard parabolics
P_{M}\subseteq P_{0}
, where
P_{M}
has Levi factor
M
. Given a representation
\pi
of such
M
, define
\mathcal{I}_{M}^{G}(\pi):=\mathcal{I}_{P_{M}}^{G}(\pi)
to be the normalized parabolic induction of
[
8
]
*§1.8(a) and
\mathcal{R}_{M}^{G}(\pi)
to be the normalized Jacquet functor of
[
8
]
*§1.8(b); see
[
47
]
*§3.2 for a modern summary.

Let
\pi
be an irreducible representation of
G_{v}
. Then by the Langlands classification (
[
47
]
*§3.3 for a modern exposition), there is a standard Levi
M
of
G_{v}
, tempered irreducible representation
\sigma
of
M
, and unramified character
\lambda
of
M
such that
\pi
is a subrepresentation of
\mathcal{I}_{P_{M}}^{G}(\sigma\otimes\lambda)
.

Since
G_{v}
is unitary splitting over
E_{w}
,
M
is of the form:

M=\Res_{F_{v}}^{E_{w}}GL_{n_{1}}\times\cdots\times\Res_{F_{v}}^{E_{w}}GL_{n_{k}}\times G^{\prime}_{v},

where
G^{\prime}_{v}
is a smaller unitary group splitting over
E_{w}
(see e.g.
[
61
, §3.2.3]
). Therefore, by the Bernstein-Zelevinsky classification (
[
91
]
,
[
56
]
*§2 for a modern exposition), we can actually choose
(M,\sigma)
so that
\sigma\otimes\lambda
is of the form

\sigma\otimes\lambda=\St(\rho_{1},a_{1})|\det|^{-x_{1}}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\St(\rho_{k},a_{k})|\det|^{-x_{k}}\boxtimes\pi_{\mathrm{temp}},

(6.1)

where
\St(\rho_{i},a_{i})
are Steinberg representations built out of supercuspidals
\rho_{i}
of
GL_{T_{i}}E_{w}
with
T_{i}a_{i}=n_{i}
,
\pi_{\mathrm{temp}}
is a tempered representation
7
7

7

we say
\pi_{\mathrm{temp}}=0
in the case when the last factor of
M
doesn’t appear
of
G^{\prime}_{v}
, and
x_{1}\geq\cdots\geq x_{k}>0
. Such
\sigma\otimes\lambda
is unique up to permuting factors
i
with equal
x_{i}
’s.

Furthermore, by the endoscopic classification
5.2.2
,
\pi_{\mathrm{temp}}
has a tempered
L
-parameter:

\varphi_{\pi_{\mathrm{temp}}}=\bigoplus_{j}\tau_{j}\boxtimes[b_{j}]

(6.2)

for
\tau_{j}
unitary supercuspidals of some
GL_{R_{j}}(E_{w})
.

Following
[
67
]
, we can now define some invariants of
\pi
:

Definition 6.1.1
.

For
\pi
an irreducible representation of
G_{v}
as above, define:

(1)

The Langlands data for
\pi
is the data
((\rho_{i},a_{i},x_{i})_{i},\pi_{\mathrm{temp}})
from (
6.1
),

(2)

The extended supercuspidal support for
\pi
is the multiset produced by taking a union of

(\rho_{i}|\det|^{l-x_{i}},\bar{\rho}_{i}^{\vee}|\det|^{l+x_{i}}:l\in\{(a_{i}-1)/2,(a_{i}-3)/2,\dotsc,(1-a_{i})/2\})

over the
i
from (
6.1
) together with

(\tau_{j}|\det|^{l}:l\in\{(b_{j}-1)/2,(b_{j}-3)/2,\dotsc,(1-b_{j})/2\})

over the
j
from (
6.2
).

To understand matrix coefficient decay, we also need a slightly different invariant:

Definition 6.1.2
.

For
\pi
an irreducible representation of
G_{v}
as above, define:

(1)

the coarse exponents
\overline{L}_{\pi}
to be the list of
x_{i}
from (
6.1
) in non-increasing order,

(2)

the exponents
L_{\pi}
to be the list of each
x_{i}
from (
6.1
) repeated
n_{i}
times in non-increasing order.

Lists of exponents can be compared:

Definition 6.1.3
.

If
L=(l_{i})_{i=1}^{k}
is a non-increasing list of numbers:

(1)

define

\sigma_{i}(L):=\sum_{j=1}^{i}x_{j},

where for indexing purposes,
x_{j}=0
when
j
is out-of-bounds.

(2)

We say that
L_{1}\succeq L_{2}
if for all
i
,
\sigma_{i}(L_{1})\geq\sigma_{i}(L_{2})
.

6.2
Exponents and Matrix Coefficient Decay

The exponents of a representation control its matrix coefficients’ decay. Pick representation
\pi
of
G_{v}
and define the corresponding
\lambda,\sigma,M
as in (
6.1
); i.e, in terms of the Langlands data:

\lambda:M=\Res_{F_{v}}^{E_{w}}GL_{n_{1}}\times\cdots\times\Res_{F_{v}}^{E_{w}}GL_{n_{k}}\times G^{\prime}_{v}\to\mathbb{C}:\\
g_{1}\times\cdots\times g_{k}\times g\mapsto|\det g_{1}|^{-x_{1}}\cdots|\det g_{k}|^{-x_{k}}.

For any standard Levis
M_{1}\supseteq M_{2}
, let
\delta^{M_{1}}_{M_{2}}
be modulus character of
P_{M_{2}}\cap M_{1}
—i.e the choice of
P_{0}
determines a set of (absolute) positive roots
\Phi^{+}_{M}
for each
M
and we take a product:

\delta^{M_{1}}_{M_{2}}:=\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}(M_{1})\setminus\Phi^{+}(M_{2})}|\alpha|.

This extends to a character of
M_{2}
. Note that for
z
in the center
Z_{M_{2}}
of
M_{2}
:

\delta^{M_{1}}_{M_{2}}(z)=\delta^{M_{1}}(z):=\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}(M_{1})}|\alpha(z)|

and for
z\in Z_{M_{1}}
,
\delta^{M_{1}}_{M_{2}}(z)=\delta^{M_{1}}(z)=1
.

Let
T
be a maximal torus of the minimal standard Levi
M_{0}
and
T^{\mathcal{O}}
be the subset on which all algebraic characters take values with norm
1
. For any
M
, define
Z_{M}^{\mathcal{O}}
similarly in the center of
M
and let
Z_{M}^{-}
be the set of all
z\in Z_{M}
such that
|\alpha(z)|\leq 1
for all
\alpha\in\Phi^{+}(G)\setminus\Phi^{+}(M)
.

The next two lemmas apply to arbitrary
p
-adic reductive groups
G
. Lemma
6.2.1
is the key technical idea that lets us input the fact of
\sigma
being tempered to tighten bounds as much as possible.

Lemma 6.2.1
.

In the notation above, let
L
be another standard Levi of
G
. Let
\chi
be the central character of an irreducible subquotient of
\mathcal{R}^{G}_{L}\mathcal{I}_{M}^{G}(\sigma\otimes\lambda)
. Then there is Weyl element
w
such that
\chi=\chi_{1}\otimes(\lambda\circ w)|_{Z_{L}}
, where
|\chi_{1}(z)|\leq 1
for all
z\in Z_{L}^{-}\setminus Z_{G}T^{\mathcal{O}}
.

Proof.

This follows from Bernstein’s geometric lemma
[
8
, pg 448]
:

Any
\chi
is, in the notation therein, a central character of a subquotient of some

F_{w}(\sigma\otimes\lambda):=\mathcal{I}^{L}_{L^{\prime}}\circ w\circ\mathcal{R}^{M}_{M^{\prime}}(\sigma\otimes\lambda),

with
w
some Weyl element satisfying
w(M^{\prime})=L^{\prime}
(among other conditions). Computing central characters of
F_{w}
step-by-step,
\mathcal{R}^{M}_{M^{\prime}}(\sigma\otimes\lambda)
has central characters of the form
\chi^{\prime}_{1}\otimes\lambda|_{Z_{M}^{\prime}}
where
\chi^{\prime}_{1}
is a central character of
\mathcal{R}^{M}_{M^{\prime}}\sigma
. Further applying
w
produces those of the form
\chi_{1}\otimes(\lambda\circ w)|_{Z_{L^{\prime}}}
where
\chi_{1}
is a character in
\mathcal{R}^{wM}_{L^{\prime}}(w\sigma)
. Applying the induction then gives characters of the form
\chi_{1}\otimes(\lambda\circ w)\otimes(\delta^{L})^{1/2}|_{Z_{L}}=\chi_{1}\otimes(\lambda\circ w)|_{Z_{L}}
.

Finally,
[
19
, Cor 4.4.6]
gives the desired property of
\chi_{1}
since
w\sigma
is tempered. Note that we cancel out the
(\delta^{wM}_{L^{\prime}})^{-1/2}
in the reference by using normalized Jacquet modules.
∎

Proposition 6.2.2
.

In the notation above, the matrix coefficients of
\mathcal{I}^{G_{v}}_{M}(\sigma\otimes\lambda)
are
L^{p+\epsilon}
mod center for all
\epsilon>0
if for all negative dominant
\nu\in X_{*}(Z_{M_{0}})\setminus X_{*}(Z_{G})
,

|\lambda(\nu(\varpi))\delta^{G}(\nu(\varpi))^{1/2-1/p}|\leq 1.

Proof.

The parameter
p
in the application of Corollary 4.4.5 in the proof of Theorem 4.4.6 in
[
19
]
can be any value instead of just
2
producing a test for
L^{p}
matrix coefficients.

Recalling that our Jacquet modules are normalized, it therefore suffices to check that for any Levi
L
, the central characters
\chi
of
\mathcal{R}^{G}_{L}\mathcal{I}_{M}^{G}(\sigma\otimes\lambda)
satisfy that

|\chi(a)\delta^{G}(a)^{1/2-1/p}|\leq 1

(6.3)

for all
a\in Z^{-}_{L}\setminus Z_{G}T^{\mathcal{O}}
. However, by lemma
6.2.1
, there is a Weyl element
w
such that

|\chi(a)\delta^{G}(a)^{1/2-1/p}|\leq|\lambda(wa)\delta^{G}(a)^{1/2-1/p}|\leq|\lambda(a)\delta^{G}(a)^{1/2-1/p}|

by the ordering of the
x_{i}
. Then, since
\lambda
is trivial on
Z^{\mathcal{O}}_{L}
, we only need to check the condition (
6.3
) for the listed coset representatives
\nu(\varpi)
of
Z^{-}_{L}/Z_{L}^{\mathcal{O}}
, ignoring those intersecting
Z_{G}
.

The result follows from noting that
Z_{L}\subseteq Z_{M_{0}}
.
∎

We rephrase this slightly in our specific situation:

Corollary 6.2.3
.

In the notation above,

\frac{2}{\sigma(\pi)}\geq 1-\max_{1\leq i\leq\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\frac{2\sigma_{i}(L_{\pi})}{i(n-i)}.

Proof.

Since
\pi\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{M}^{G_{v}}(\sigma\otimes\lambda)
, it suffices to check which
p
satisfy condition of Proposition
6.2.2
. We also without loss of generality consider all negative dominant
\nu\in X_{*}(A)\setminus X_{*}(G)
for a maximally split torus
A
.

Parameterize
A
as diagonal matrices
(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{n})
for
t_{i}\in E_{w}
with
t_{i}^{-1}=\bar{t}_{N-i}
. When
G_{v}
isn’t quasisplit,
n
is even and we further require
t_{n/2}=t_{n/2+1}=1
. Then
X_{+}(A)\setminus X_{*}(Z_{G})
is generated as a semigroup by the fundamental weights for
1\leq i\leq\lfloor n/2\rfloor
(resp.
n/2-1
when
G_{v}
isn’t quasisplit):

\xi_{i}:F_{v}^{\times}\to T:t\mapsto(t,\dotsc,t,1\dotsc,1,t^{-1},\dotsc,t^{-1})

where the breakpoints are at indices
i
and
n-i+1
.

By the inequality,

b,d>0\text{ and }\frac{a}{b}\leq\frac{c}{d}\implies\frac{a}{b}\leq\frac{a+c}{b+d}\leq\frac{c}{d},

it suffices in
6.2.2
to only check the cases where
\nu=-\xi_{i}
for some
i
. Then

\log_{q_{v}}|\lambda(-\xi_{i}(\varpi))|=2\sigma_{i}(L_{\pi}),\qquad\log_{q_{v}}|\delta^{G}(-\xi_{i}(\varpi))|=-2i(n-i),

so
\pi
has matrix coefficients in
L^{p}
if

\sigma_{i}(L_{\pi})\leq\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}\right)i(n-i)\iff\frac{2}{p}\leq 1-\frac{2\sigma_{i}(L_{\pi})}{i(n-i)}

for all
1\leq i\leq\lfloor n/2\rfloor
(without loss of generality adding in an irrelevant term when
G_{v}
isn’t quasisplit). The result follows.
∎

6.3
Exponents and Parameters

Parameters also have a notion of exponents. Let tempered local parameter
\psi_{v}
decompose as a representation of
W_{F_{v}}\times\mathrm{SL}_{2}\times\mathrm{SL}_{2}
as

\bigoplus_{i}\tau_{i}\boxtimes[a_{i}]\boxtimes[d_{i}],

where the
\tau_{i}
are unitary supercuspidals of
GL_{T_{i}}E_{w}
. Let
\tau_{i}
have dimension
T_{i}
.

Definition 6.3.1
.

In the notation above, the coarse exponents
\overline{L}_{\psi_{v}}
for
\psi_{v}
is the concatenation of the lists

\bigsqcup_{i}((d_{i}-1)/2,(d_{i}-3)/3,\cdots,(d_{i}-2\lfloor d_{i}/2\rfloor)/2),

where the result is reordered to be non-increasing.

The exponents
L_{\psi_{v}}
for
\psi_{v}
are the same except we repeat the
i
th list
T_{i}a_{i}
times:

\bigsqcup_{i}((d_{i}-1)/2,(d_{i}-3)/3,\cdots,(d_{i}-2\lfloor d_{i}/2\rfloor)/2)^{(T_{i}a_{i})},

with the result ordered to be non-increasing.

Definition 6.3.2
.

In the notation above, the extended supercuspidal support of
\psi_{v}
is the multiset produced by taking a union of

(\tau_{i}|\det|^{l}:l\in\langle a_{i}\rangle\boxplus\langle b_{i}\rangle)

over all
i
. We use shorthand

\langle r\rangle:=((r-1)/2,(r-3)/2,\dotsc,(1-r)/2)

and define
A\boxplus B
to be the multiset
(a+b:a\in A,b\in B)
.

We can now state a key input of Mœglin:

Theorem 6.3.3

(
[
67
, Thm 7.2, Prop 4.1]
)
.

Let
\psi_{v}\in\Psi_{G_{v}}
and
\pi_{v}\in\Pi_{\psi_{v}}
. Then:

(1)

\overline{L}_{\pi_{v}}\preceq\overline{L}_{\psi_{v}}
.

(2)

The extended supercuspidal support of
\pi_{v}
is the same as that of
\psi_{v}
.

Proof.

For ease of the reader, the groups to which
[
67
]
applies are specified in the first paragraph of the introduction: all inner forms of unitary, symplectic, and orthogonal groups.
∎

This is not good enough to bound matrix coefficient decay; what we actually desire is:

Conjecture 6.3.4
.

Let
\psi_{v}\in\Psi_{G_{v}}
and
\pi_{v}\in\Pi_{\psi_{v}}
. Then
L_{\pi_{v}}\preceq L_{\psi_{v}}
.

Remark 6.3.5
.

Conjecture
6.3.4
would follow from the closure-order conjecture
[
89
, Conj 2.1]
as shown in in
[
41
, Thm 4.11(2)]
.

The closure-order conjecture is known in case of symplectic and orthogonal groups by
[
41
]
. The argument depends on algorithms computing the set of
A
-packets containing a representation from
[
6
]
in the symplectic/orthogonal case—these are expected to analogize to the unitary case, though the details have not been completed as-of-this-writing.

The closure-order conjecture also holds holds for the ABV packets of
[
22
]
which are conjectured (see Conjecture 8.1 therein) to be the same as the
A
-packets we use from
[
63
]
.

However, for our applications, we only need special cases:

Corollary 6.3.6
.

Conjecture
6.3.4
holds in the following cases:

(1)

\pi_{v}
is unramified,

(2)

n=4
.

Proof.

Fix a
\pi_{v}
. If the
N_{i}=T_{i}a_{i}
in (
6.1
) are all
1
, then
L_{\pi_{v}}=\overline{L}_{\pi_{v}}\preceq\overline{L}_{\psi_{v}}\preceq L_{\psi_{v}}
by
6.3.3
(1) so
6.3.4
always holds. This covers case (1) and all
\pi_{v}
for case (2) except those with Langlands data of the form
((\chi,2,x),0)
for some character
\chi
of
E_{w}^{\times}
.

Then, such
\pi_{v}
has extended supercuspidal support

(\bar{\chi}^{\vee}|\det|^{1/2+x},\bar{\chi}^{\vee}|\det|^{-1/2+x},\chi|\det|^{1/2-x},\chi|\det|^{-1/2-x})

By
6.3.3
(2), this needs to match that of
\psi_{v}
which can only happen if
x=0,1/2,1
.

If
x=0
,
L_{\pi_{v}}
is all
0
’s so we are done. If
x=1/2
, then
\chi=\bar{\chi}^{\vee}
and
\psi_{v}=\chi[1][3]+\chi[1][1]
(using the natural shorthand). Therefore,
L_{\psi_{v}}=(1,0,0)
and
L_{\pi_{v}}=(1/2,1/2)
which satisfies the bound. Finally,
x=1
would force
\chi=\bar{\chi}^{\vee}
and
\psi_{v}=\chi[1][4]
which has
A
-packet containing just characters. This contradicts.
∎

The case
n=8
will be resolved later in Corollary
7.3.4
.

6.4
Bounding Decay by Shape

Note that for global parameter
\psi
, each
L_{\psi_{v}}
only depends on the restriction to the Arthur-
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
. Therefore, for any shape
\Box
and place
v
,
L_{\psi_{v}}
is constant over
\psi\in\Box
.

Definition 6.4.1
.

For
\Box
a shape for
G
, let
L_{\Box}
be the common value of
L_{\psi_{v}}
for non-split, unramified
v
and
\psi\in\Box
.

As a consequence of all the above work and the deep input of Theorem
5.1.3
, we get our final result:

Theorem 6.4.2
.

Let
\Box
be a shape for
G
and
\psi\in\Box
such that
\psi_{\infty}
has regular, integral infinitesimal character.

Then for all non-split places
v
and
\pi\in\Pi_{\psi_{v}}
such that conjecture
6.3.4
holds:

\frac{2}{\sigma(\pi)}\geq 1-\max_{1\leq i\leq\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\frac{2\sigma_{i}(L_{\Box})}{i(n-i)}.

Proof.

Let
\psi=\bigoplus_{i}\tau_{i}\boxtimes[d_{i}]
with
\tau_{i}
cuspidal. Then, since
\psi
has infinitesimal character at infinity matching that a finite-dimensional representation, all the
\tau_{i}
also do and are therefore cohomological (see also,
[
68
, Cor 4]
). Therefore, by Theorem
5.1.3
, the
\tau_{i,v}
are all tempered. Then,
\psi_{v}
decomposes as
\bigoplus_{j}\sigma_{j}\boxtimes[a_{j}]\boxtimes[d_{j}]
for
\sigma_{j}
unitary supercuspidal, so it is in
\Psi_{v}
(instead of the larger
\Psi^{+}_{v}
of
[
63
]
).

The result then follows from the equality
L_{\psi_{v}}=L_{\Box}
, Conjecture
6.3.4
and Corollary
6.2.3
.
∎

Remark 6.4.3
.

When
v
is split, analogous notions of exponents for representations
\pi_{v}
of
G_{v}
can be defined using the Bernstein-Zelevinsky classification. The analogue of Corollary
6.2.3
then still holds. If
\psi_{v}
is a parameter of
G_{v}
then it corresponds to a irreducible representation
\psi_{0}\boxtimes\psi_{0}^{\vee}
on
GL_{n}(F_{v}\otimes_{F}E)\cong GL_{n}(E_{w})^{2}
where
w
lies over
v
. Then
\Pi_{\psi_{v}}
is a singleton containing only the representation
\pi_{\psi}
corresponding to
\psi_{0}
(see e.g.
[
23
, lem 6.1.1]
). In particular,
L_{\psi}=L_{\pi_{\psi}}
so Conjecture
6.3.4
always holds. Therefore, Theorem
6.4.2
always holds as well.

Motivated by the above:

Definition 6.4.4
.

Let
\Box
be a shape for
G
. Then define
\sigma_{\Box}
by

\frac{2}{\sigma_{\Box}}:=1-\max_{1\leq i\leq\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\frac{2\sigma_{i}(L_{\Box})}{i(n-i)}.

In particular, Theorem
6.4.2
gives that if
\psi\in\Box
and
\pi\in\Pi_{\psi_{v}}
, then
\sigma(\pi)\leq\sigma_{\Box}
.

7
Density Hypothesis Proof

Let
G=U_{N}^{E/F,H}
be a definite arithmetic unitary group and
v_{0}
a distinguished infinite place. Choose open compact
K^{\prime}<G^{\infty}
. Let
\Gamma=G(F)\cap K
as a subgroup of
G_{v_{0}}
so that there is a map

\rho^{K^{\prime}}:G_{v_{0}}\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma\backslash G_{v_{0}}\hookrightarrow G(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A})/KG_{\infty\setminus v_{0}}.

In our eventual application when
K^{\prime}
is golden,
\Gamma=1
and the second map will be a bijection.

In this section, we prove the density hypothesis for a variant of the
\delta
-ball family on
G_{v_{0}}
: we define functions
f_{v_{0}}^{\epsilon,Z}
on
G_{v_{0}}
that are approximately indicator functions of balls of radius
\epsilon
and consider families

\mathcal{F}^{K^{\prime}}_{\epsilon,Z}(\pi):=m_{\pi}\frac{\|\Proj_{\pi_{v_{0}}}(f_{v_{0}}^{\epsilon,Z})\|_{2}^{2}}{\|f_{v_{0}}^{\epsilon,Z}\|_{2}^{2}}\mathbf{1}_{\pi_{\infty\setminus v_{0}}\text{ triv.}}\dim\left((\pi^{\infty})^{K^{\prime}}\right)\\
=m_{\pi}\frac{1}{\|f_{\epsilon}\|_{2}^{2}}\tr_{\pi}((f_{\epsilon}\star f_{\epsilon}^{*})\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{G_{\infty\setminus v_{0}}}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{K^{\prime}})

(7.1)

in conductor
m(\epsilon)=1/\epsilon
and for various choices of
Z
. We can also consider
f_{v_{0}}^{\epsilon,Z}
as an element of
L^{2}(G(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A}))^{K^{\prime}}
through summing over fibers of
\rho^{K^{\prime}}:G_{v_{0}}\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma\backslash G_{v_{0}}
to get an alternate interpretation:

\mathcal{F}^{K^{\prime}}_{\epsilon,Z}(\pi)=m_{\pi}\frac{\|\Proj_{\pi}(\rho^{K^{\prime}}_{*}f_{v_{0}}^{\epsilon,Z})\|_{2}^{2}}{\|f_{v_{0}}^{\epsilon,Z}\|_{2}^{2}}.

The proof is from comparing two bounds: first in Theorem
7.2.5
, we bound

\sum_{\pi\in\Box}\mathcal{F}^{K^{\prime}}_{\epsilon,Z}(\pi)=\frac{\|\Proj_{\Box}(\rho^{K^{\prime}}_{*}f^{\epsilon,Z}_{v_{0}})\|_{2}^{2}}{\|f^{\epsilon,Z}_{v_{0}}\|_{2}^{2}},

(7.2)

(recalling notation from Definition
5.2.5
). This requires the very serious black-boxed input of the endoscopic classification as used in Theorem
7.1.1
. We then compare this to the matrix-coefficient decay bound Theorem
6.4.2
.

7.1
Input Bound

We now state our black-box input bound.

Fix infinitesimal character
\lambda
for
G_{\infty}
and let
V_{\lambda}
be the corresponding finite dimensional representation. If
\Box
is a shape and
K^{\prime}<G^{\infty}
is an open compact, define asymptotic automorphic family

\mathcal{F}^{G,K^{\prime}}_{\lambda,\Box}(\pi):=m_{\pi}\mathbf{1}_{\pi\in\Box}\mathbf{1}_{\pi_{\infty}=V_{\lambda}}\dim\left((\pi_{\infty})^{K^{\prime}}\right).

The paper
[
23
, §7-9]
used the endoscopic classification of
[
45
]
through an inductive analysis of
[
84
]
to upper bound asymptotics of the total mass of
\mathcal{F}^{G,K^{\prime}}_{\lambda,\Box}
for certain sequences of
K^{\prime}\to 1
.

The paper
[
24
, §5-6]
used the same techniques in the much simpler case of
m(\lambda)\to\infty
. We recall the result:

Theorem 7.1.1

(Special case of
[
24
, Thm 6.5.1]
)
.

In the notation above,

\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{AR}_{\mathrm{disc}}(G)}\mathcal{F}^{G,K^{\prime}}_{\lambda,\Box}(\pi)\leq(\dim_{\Box}\lambda)(\Lambda(G,\Box,K^{\prime})+O_{G,\Box,K^{\prime}}(m(\lambda)^{-1}))

for some constant
\Lambda(G,\Box,K^{\prime})
depending only on the three arguments.

Proof.

Recall the definitions of
I^{G}_{\Box}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{K^{\prime}})
and
S^{G*}_{\Box}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{K^{\prime}})
from
[
24
, §6.1]
.

Since
V_{\lambda}
is the only representation of compact
G_{\infty}
with infinitesimal character
\lambda
,

\tr_{\pi}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{K^{\prime}})=\mathbf{1}_{\pi_{\infty}=V_{\lambda}}\dim\left((\pi_{\infty})^{K^{\prime}}\right).

Therefore,

\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{AR}_{\mathrm{disc}}(G)}\mathcal{F}^{G,K^{\prime}}_{\lambda,\Box}(\pi)=I^{G}_{\Box}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{K^{\prime}}).

The result then follows from the second bound of
[
24
, Thm 6.5.1]
.

For the reader’s convenience, we very roughly sketch the argument of
[
24
, Thm 6.5.1]
. By an implementation in
[
23
]
of an inductive strategy from
[
84
]
inputting the endoscopic classification
[
45
,
63
]
, our count of representations can be upper-bounded by a linear combination of terms on the geometric side of Arthur’s discrete-at-
\infty
trace formula from
[
3
]
.

All these
[
3
]
terms are sums of smaller terms of the form

C_{\gamma}\Phi^{H}_{\lambda^{\prime}}(\gamma),

where
H
ranges over groups that are
G_{F}(\Box)
or smaller, the
\Phi^{H}_{\lambda^{\prime}}
are certain character sums on maximal tori related to traces against the finite dimensional representation on
H
with infinitesimal character
\lambda^{\prime}
derived from
\lambda
,
\gamma
ranges over some fixed finite set of rational conjugacy classes of
H
depending only on
H,\Box
and
K^{\prime}
, and
C_{\gamma}
are some inexplicit constants that nevertheless depend only on
H,\Box,K^{\prime}
, and
\gamma
.

Finally, we apply the analysis of
[
82
]
to these terms—in particular
[
82
, Lem 6.10(ii)]
bounds the
\Phi^{H}_{\lambda^{\prime}}
, thereby showing that a term for
\gamma=1
on
G_{F}(\Box)
itself dominates. For this term specifically,
\Phi^{H}_{\lambda}(\gamma)=\dim_{\Box}\lambda
and
C_{\gamma}
can be made more precise.
∎

Remark 7.1.2
.

We will in fact only need that

(\dim_{\Box}\lambda)^{-1}\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{AR}_{\mathrm{disc}}(G)}\mathcal{F}^{G,K^{\prime}}_{\lambda,\Box}(\pi)=(\dim_{\Box}\lambda)^{-1}I_{\Box}^{G}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{K^{\prime}})

is bounded by a constant independent of
\lambda
.

7.2
Indicators of Balls

We now define the functions
f_{v_{0}}^{\epsilon,Z}
defining our variant of the
f
-ball family and bound (
7.2
). Our main technical tool here is Kirilov’s orbit-method character formula; see
[
75
,
86
]
for the full proof and
[
50
, Ch 5]
for a textbook summary. Our
f_{v_{0}}^{\epsilon,Z}
are close to but not exactly indicator functions, instead chosen specifically to simplify the orbit-method computations.

7.2.1
Modified Indicator Functions

First, consider the case of
H
a compact, semisimple, and simply connected Lie group. Let
\mathfrak{h}
be the real Lie algebra for
H
,
\dim\mathfrak{h}=N
, and
\rank H=r
. Define on
\mathfrak{h}
:

j(X):=\det\left(\frac{\sinh(\ad X/2)}{\ad X/2}\right),

Consider test functions

f^{\epsilon}\circ\exp:=\mathbf{1}_{B_{\epsilon}(0)}j^{1/2}

on
H
and where balls are defined using the Killing form. Note that
f^{\epsilon}
is supported on the ball
\exp(B_{\epsilon}(0))
, is analytic, and takes values close to
1
for small enough
\epsilon
.

We use the Kirilov character formula to compute traces of
f^{\epsilon}
against the finite dimensional representation
V_{\lambda}
. If
\mathfrak{t}
is a Cartan for
\mathfrak{h}
, the Killing form gives an embedding
\mathfrak{t}^{*}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{h}^{*}
so
i\lambda
can be interpreted as a point in
i\mathfrak{h}^{*}
. To define a Fourier transform, pick a measure on
\mathfrak{h}
that is Plancherel self-dual through the Killing form isomorphisms
\mathfrak{h}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathfrak{h}^{*}
and associate
x\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
to the multiplicative character
e^{2\pi ix(\cdot)}
on
\mathfrak{h}
. Then for small enough
\epsilon
:

\tr_{\pi_{\lambda}}(f^{\epsilon})=\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\lambda/(2\pi i)}}\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{B_{\epsilon}(0)}}\,d\omega,

(7.3)

where the coadjoint orbit
\mathcal{O}_{\lambda/(2\pi i)}\subseteq i\mathfrak{h}^{*}
is given its canonical measure as an integral symplectic manifold with total volume
\dim\lambda
.

By a classical result:

\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{B_{\epsilon}(0)}}(\xi/(2\pi))=\epsilon^{N}\|\epsilon\xi\|^{-N/2}J_{N/2}(\|\epsilon\xi\|)=\epsilon^{N/2}\|\xi\|^{-N/2}J_{N/2}(\epsilon\|\xi\|),

where
J_{N/2}
is the classical Bessel function of the first kind. Since the adjoint action preserves the Killing form, the integral in (
7.3
) is constant so:

Lemma 7.2.1
.

For
H
a compact, semisimple, and simply connected Lie group and in the above notation:

\tr_{V_{\lambda}}(f^{\epsilon})=(\dim\lambda)\epsilon^{N/2}\|\lambda\|^{-N/2}J_{N/2}(\epsilon\|\lambda\|).

for small enough
\epsilon
.

Remark 7.2.2
.

We can understand the factors in lemma
7.2.1
through lemma
5.3.4
,

\dim\lambda\leq\|\lambda\|^{P_{H}},

and the Bessel function asymptotics:

|J_{N/2}(x)|\leq\begin{cases}Cx^{N/2}&x\ll\sqrt{N/2+1}\\
Cx^{-1/2}&x\gg\sqrt{N/2+1}\end{cases}.

In particular, this term should be thought of as order

\tr_{V_{\lambda}}(f^{\epsilon}_{\infty})=\begin{cases}O(\epsilon^{N}\|\lambda\|^{1/2(N-r)})&\|\lambda\|\ll\epsilon^{-1}\\
O(\epsilon^{(N-1)/2}\|\lambda\|^{-1/2(r+1)})&\|\lambda\|\gg\epsilon^{-1}\end{cases}.

We can generalize this to our
G_{v_{0}}
that is compact and (topologically) connected. Then
G_{v_{0}}=G^{\mathrm{der}}_{v_{0}}\times Z_{G_{v_{0}}}/Z_{G^{\mathrm{der}}_{v_{0}}}
on points and we have a corresponding canonical factorization on Lie algebras
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}^{\mathrm{der}}\times\mathfrak{z}
.

Then for any small enough
\epsilon
and subset
Z\subseteq\mathfrak{z}
on which
\exp
to
G_{v_{0}}
is injective, define

f^{\epsilon,Z}_{v_{0}}\circ\exp:=j^{1/2}\mathbf{1}_{B_{\epsilon}(0)\times Z}.

(7.4)

The Kirillov character formula a priori computes the trace character of
V_{\lambda}
pulled back to the simply connected cover
(G^{\mathrm{der}}_{v_{0}})_{\mathrm{sc}}
. However, for small enough
\epsilon
, this is the same as its trace against
G^{\mathrm{der}}_{v_{0}}
.

We can in addition integrate over
Z_{G_{v_{0}}}
to compute traces of the pullback to
G^{\mathrm{der}}_{v_{0}}\times Z_{G_{v_{0}}}
, noting that
V_{\lambda}
has central character
\lambda|_{\mathfrak{z}}
on
\mathfrak{z}
. Since the diagonal embedding of
Z_{G^{\mathrm{der}}_{v_{0}}}
intersects
\exp(B_{\epsilon}(0)\times Z)\subseteq G^{\mathrm{der}}_{v_{0}}\times Z_{G_{v_{0}}}
trivially for small enough
\epsilon
, this is the same as computing traces on
G_{v_{0}}
.

In total:

Lemma 7.2.3
.

In our case where
G_{v_{0}}
is compact and (topologically) connected:

\tr_{V_{\lambda}}(f^{\epsilon,Z}_{v_{0}})=(\dim\lambda)\epsilon^{N^{\mathrm{der}}/2}\|\lambda\|^{-N^{\mathrm{der}}/2}J_{N^{\mathrm{der}}/2}(\epsilon\|\lambda\|)\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{Z}}(\lambda|_{\mathfrak{z}})

for small enough
\epsilon
and
Z\subseteq\mathfrak{z}
on which
\exp
to
G_{v_{0}}
is injective. Here,
\mathfrak{z}
is given the measure that corresponds to unit Haar measure on
Z_{G_{v_{0}}}
and recall that
N^{\mathrm{der}}=\dim\mathfrak{g}^{\mathrm{der}}
.

We also need to understand traces against
(f_{v_{0}}^{\epsilon,Z})^{*}\star f_{v_{0}}^{\epsilon,Z}
. By Theorem 10 on page 174 of
[
50
]
,

(f_{v_{0}}^{\epsilon,Z})^{*}\star_{G_{v_{0}}}f_{v_{0}}^{\epsilon,Z}=(\mathbf{1}_{B_{\epsilon}(0)\times(-Z)}\star_{\mathfrak{g}^{\mathrm{der}}\times\mathfrak{z}}\mathbf{1}_{B_{\epsilon}(0)\times Z})j^{1/2},

so using that abelian Fourier transform takes convolution to product, a similar computation gives:

Lemma 7.2.4
.

In our case where
G_{v_{0}}
is compact and (topologically) connected:

\tr_{V_{\lambda}}((f_{v_{0}}^{\epsilon,Z})^{*}\star f_{v_{0}}^{\epsilon,Z})=(\dim\lambda)\epsilon^{N^{\mathrm{der}}}\|\lambda\|^{-N^{\mathrm{der}}}J_{N^{\mathrm{der}}/2}(\epsilon\|\lambda\|)^{2}\left|\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{Z}}(\lambda|_{\mathfrak{z}})\right|^{2}

for small enough
\epsilon
and
Z\subseteq\mathfrak{z}
on which
\exp
to
G_{v_{0}}
. Here,
\mathfrak{z}
is given the measure that corresponds to unit Haar measure on
Z_{G_{v_{0}}}
and recall that
N=\dim\mathfrak{g}^{\mathrm{der}}
.

Finally, note that pulling back to the Lie algebra gives

\|f_{v_{0}}^{\epsilon,Z}\|^{2}_{G_{v_{0}}}=\|\mathbf{1}_{B_{\epsilon}(0)\times Z}\|^{2}_{\mathfrak{g}}=\vol(Z)\frac{\pi^{N^{\mathrm{der}}/2}}{\Gamma(N^{\mathrm{der}}/2+1)}\epsilon^{N^{\mathrm{der}}}.

(7.5)

7.2.2
Projection Bounds

With the above Kirilov formula computation, we can now input Theorem
7.1.1
and bound
\|\Proj_{L^{2}_{\Box}}f^{\epsilon,Z}_{v_{0}}\|^{2}_{2}
. We will consider two possible
Z
: either
Z_{\epsilon}:=(-\epsilon/2,\epsilon/2)
or
Z_{1}:=(-1/2,1/2)
. Here,
\mathfrak{z}
is parameterized so that Lebesgue measure matches unit Haar measure: i.e. intervals of length
1
exactly cover
Z_{G_{v_{0}}}=U_{1}
.

First, since
G_{v_{0}}
is compact, we can choose the function
\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}
to be the matrix coefficient of the finite dimensional representation
V_{\lambda}
with infinitesimal character
\lambda
. In particular, by Peter-Weyl, any
f_{v_{0}}
always has the same orbital integrals as a function of the form

\sum_{\lambda}a_{\lambda}\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}.

Since by definition
\tr_{V_{\mu}}\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}=\mathbf{1}_{\mu=\lambda}
for any two finite-dimensional reps
V_{\mu}
and
V_{\lambda}
, comparing traces solves for the coefficients and gives:

I(f_{v_{0}})=I\left(\sum_{\lambda}(\tr_{V_{\lambda}}f_{v_{0}})\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}\right)

for any invariant distribution
I
.

In addition, the Plancherel formula gives

\|\Proj_{\Box}f_{v_{0}}\|^{2}_{2}=\tr_{L^{2}_{\Box}}(f_{v_{0}}^{*}\star f_{v_{0}})=I^{G}_{\Box}((f_{v_{0}}^{*}\star f_{v_{0}})\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{G_{\infty\setminus v_{0}}}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{K^{\prime}}).

Therefore:

\displaystyle\|\Proj_{\Box}(\rho_{*}^{K^{\prime}}f^{\epsilon,Z}_{v_{0}})\|^{2}_{2}

\displaystyle\qquad=I^{G}_{\Box}(((f^{\epsilon,Z}_{v_{0}})^{*}\star f^{\epsilon,Z}_{v_{0}})\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{G_{\infty\setminus v_{0}}}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{K^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{\lambda\in\Box}\tr_{V_{\lambda}}((f^{\epsilon,Z}_{v_{0}})^{*}\star f^{\epsilon,Z}_{v_{0}})I^{G}_{\Box}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{G_{\infty\setminus v_{0}}}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{K^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\qquad\overset{\ref{lem:balltrace}}{=}\sum_{\lambda\in\Box}(\dim\lambda)\epsilon^{N^{\mathrm{der}}}\|\lambda\|^{-N^{\mathrm{der}}}J_{N^{\mathrm{der}}/2}(\epsilon\|\lambda\|)^{2}\left|\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{Z}}(\lambda|_{\mathfrak{z}})\right|^{2}I^{G}_{\Box}(\mathrm{EP}_{\lambda}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{G_{\infty\setminus v_{0}}}\bar{\mathbf{1}}_{K^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\qquad\overset{\ref{thm:shapebound}}{\leq}\epsilon^{N^{\mathrm{der}}}\sum_{\lambda\in\Box}(\dim\lambda)\|\lambda\|^{-N^{\mathrm{der}}}J_{N^{\mathrm{der}}/2}(\epsilon\|\lambda\|)^{2}\left|\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{Z}}(\lambda|_{\mathfrak{z}})\right|^{2}\dim_{\Box}(\lambda)(\Lambda+O(m(\lambda)^{-1}))

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C\epsilon^{N^{\mathrm{der}}}\sum_{\lambda\in\Box}(\dim\lambda)\|\lambda\|^{-N^{\mathrm{der}}}J_{N^{\mathrm{der}}/2}(\epsilon\|\lambda\|)^{2}\left|\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{Z}}(\lambda|_{\mathfrak{z}})\right|^{2}\dim_{\Box}(\lambda)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C\epsilon^{N_{G}^{\mathrm{der}}}\sum_{\lambda\in\Box}\|\lambda\|^{-N_{G}^{\mathrm{der}}+P_{G}+P_{G_{F}(\Box)}-e(\Box)}J_{N_{G}^{\mathrm{der}}/2}(\epsilon\|\lambda\|)^{2}\left|\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{Z}}(\lambda|_{\mathfrak{z}})\right|^{2},

(7.6)

for some constant
C
depending only on
G
,
\Box
, and
K^{\prime}
and where the last step uses lemma
5.3.4
and Corollary
5.3.6
. Recall the convention
\lambda\in\Box
to mean that
\lambda
is a possible total infinitesimal character for a parameter of shape
\Box
and also recall formula (
5.4
) defining
e(\Box)
.

Consider first the case
Z=[-\epsilon/2,\epsilon/2]
. Note that
\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{Z}}
is zero on any character that sends
\lambda(-1)=-1
, so the lattice of possible
\lambda
is of the form
L_{G}\times L_{Z}
where
L_{Z}
is a character of
U_{1}/\pm 1
and
L_{G}
are regular, integral infinitesimal characters for
(G_{v_{0}})_{\ad}
. Summing over
L_{Z}
using Poisson summation on
\mathfrak{z}
turns the
\left|\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{Z}}(\lambda|_{\mathfrak{z}})\right|^{2}
into
\mathbf{1}_{Z}\star(\mathbf{1}_{Z})^{*}(0)=\epsilon
as long as
\epsilon
is small enough. Therefore, our estimate (
7.6
) becomes:

C\epsilon^{N_{G}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\Box\\
\lambda\text{ for }(G_{v_{0}})_{\ad}\end{subarray}}\|\lambda\|^{-N_{G}^{\mathrm{der}}+P_{G}+P_{G_{F}(\Box)}-e(\Box)}J_{N_{G}^{\mathrm{der}}/2}(\epsilon\|\lambda\|)^{2}.

(7.7)

In the other case
Z=[-1/2,1/2]
, note that
\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{Z}}(\lambda|_{\mathfrak{z}})
is an indicator function testing if
\lambda|_{\mathfrak{z}}=1
, so this simply changes the
\epsilon^{N_{G}}
coefficient on the sum back into
\epsilon^{N^{\mathrm{der}}_{G}}
.

Now, we input the asymptotics for
J_{N/2}
to evaluate the sum. For
\|\lambda\|\ll 1/\epsilon
, the terms in the sum are

\ll\epsilon^{N_{G}^{\mathrm{der}}}\|\lambda\|^{P_{G}+P_{G_{F}(\Box)}-e(\Box)}.

The
\lambda\in\Box
that are integral on
(G_{v_{0}})_{\ad}
form an
(r_{G_{F}(\Box)}-1)
-dimensional sublattice of all
\lambda
(shifted by a small fixed vector depending only on
\Box
that becomes negligible as
1/\epsilon\to\infty
). Assume
r_{G_{F}(\Box)}\geq 2
. Then, summing over the
1/\epsilon
-ball in this subspace gives something of order

\ll\epsilon^{N_{G}^{\mathrm{der}}-P_{G}-P_{G_{F}(\Box)}+e(\Box)-r_{G_{F}(\Box)}+1}\\
=\epsilon^{N_{G}-P_{G}-P_{G_{F}(\Box)}-r_{G_{F}(\Box)}+e(\Box)}=\epsilon^{(N_{G}-P_{G})-(N_{G_{F}(\Box)}-P_{G_{F}(\Box)})+e(\Box)},

after approximating the sum by an integral, which we can do since
r_{G_{F}(\Box)}\geq 2
means the number of terms in the sum with
\|\lambda\|\ll 1/\epsilon
goes to infinity as
\epsilon\to 0
.

On the other side,
\|\lambda\|\ll 1/\epsilon
, the terms in the sum are

\ll\epsilon^{-1}\|\lambda\|^{-N_{G}^{\mathrm{der}}+P_{G}+P_{G_{F}(\Box)}-e(\Box)-1},

which after summing gives something of the same order

\ll\epsilon^{N_{G}^{\mathrm{der}}-P_{G}-P_{G_{F}(\Box)}-r_{G_{F}(\Box)}+e(\Box)+1}=\epsilon^{(N_{G}-P_{G})-(N_{G_{F}(\Box)}-P_{G_{F}(\Box)})+e(\Box)}.

We substitute this into the sum from (
7.7
), first in the case of
Z=(-\epsilon/2,\epsilon/2)
. Then, when
r_{G_{F}(\Box)}\geq 2
:

\|\Proj_{\Box}(\rho_{*}^{K^{\prime}}f^{\epsilon,Z}_{v_{0}})\|^{2}_{2}\ll\epsilon^{N_{G}+(N_{G}-P_{G})-(N_{G_{F}(\Box)}-P_{G_{F}(\Box)})+e(\Box)}.

When
r_{G_{F}(\Box)}=1
, there is a single
\lambda\in\Box
. We can therefore treat
\|\lambda\|
as a constant and reproduce the same formula (note that for this shape
e(\Box)=P_{G}
).

The case of of
Z_{1}
just removes a power of
\epsilon
. Unifying the two cases by noting that
\|f^{\epsilon,Z_{\epsilon}}_{v_{0}}\|^{2}_{2}\asymp\epsilon^{N_{G}}
and
\|f^{\epsilon,Z_{1}}_{v_{0}}\|^{2}_{2}\asymp\epsilon^{N_{G}-1}
, we get:

Theorem 7.2.5
.

Normalize
\mathfrak{z}
so that intervals of length
1
exactly cover
Z_{G_{v_{0}}}=U_{1}
. Then, if
Z
is either
(-\epsilon/2,\epsilon/2)
or
(-1/2,1/2)
:

\frac{\|\Proj_{\Box}(\rho_{*}^{K^{\prime}}f^{\epsilon,Z}_{v_{0}})\|^{2}_{2}}{\|f^{\epsilon,Z}_{v_{0}}\|^{2}_{2}}\ll\epsilon^{(N_{G}-P_{G})-(N_{G_{F}(\Box)}-P_{G_{F}(\Box)})+e(\Box)}.

As two special cases, we get
\epsilon^{0}
when
\Box=(n,1)
is the trivial shape and
\epsilon^{N_{G}-1}
when
\Box=(1,n)
is the shape for
1
-d representations.

7.3
Density Hypothesis Proof

Now we can put together Theorems
7.2.5
and
6.4.2
to prove the density hypothesis.

First,

\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{AR}_{\mathrm{disc}}(G)}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon,Z}(\pi)=\frac{\|f_{v_{0}}^{\epsilon,Z}\|_{2}^{2}}{\|f_{v_{0}}^{\epsilon,Z}\|_{2}^{2}}=1.

The automorphic characters restricted to
\Gamma\backslash G_{v_{0}}
span
L^{2}(\Gamma\backslash G_{v_{0}}/G^{\mathrm{der}}_{v_{0}})
, so we can calculate projections:

\Proj_{\mathcal{AC}(G)}f(x)=\int_{g\in G^{\mathrm{der}}_{v_{0}}}f(xg)\,dg.

This allows us to compute

\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{AC}(G)}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon,Z}(\pi)=\frac{\|\Proj_{\mathcal{AC}(G)}(\rho_{*}^{K^{\prime}}f^{\epsilon,Z}_{v_{0}})\|_{2}^{2}}{\|f_{v_{0}}^{\epsilon,Z}\|_{2}^{2}}\asymp\vol(Z)\epsilon^{N_{G}-1}.

Since there are finitely many possible
\Box
for each
N
, Theorem
6.4.2
gives that the density hypothesis for
\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon,Z_{1}}
at some finite, non-split place
v
would be implied by:

Theorem 7.3.1
.

\sum_{\pi\in\Box}\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon,Z_{1}}(\pi)=\frac{\|\Proj_{\Box}(\rho_{*}^{K^{\prime}}f^{\epsilon,Z}_{v_{0}})\|_{2}^{2}}{\|f_{v_{0}}^{\epsilon,Z_{1}}\|_{2}^{2}}\ll\epsilon^{(N_{G}-1)\left(1-\frac{2}{\sigma_{\Box}}\right)}.

Proof.

The inequality we want to show is that

R_{G}(\Box):=(N_{G}-P_{G})-(N_{G_{F}(\Box)}-P_{G_{F}(\Box)})+e(\Box)\geq(N_{G}-1)\left(1-\frac{2}{\sigma_{\Box}}\right)=:S_{G}(\Box).

(7.8)

Define the Arthur-
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
of a shape
\Box
to be the partition
Q
determined by the restriction of
\psi\in\Box
to the Arthur-
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
. Note that the left-hand side of the inequality only depends on the Arthur-
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
so call it
S_{G}(Q)
. Let
R_{G}(Q)
be the minimum of
R_{G}(\Box)
over
\Box
with Arthur-
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
given by
Q
—this is achieved for the unique such
\Box=((T_{i},d_{i}))
with all
d_{i}
distinct.

It therefore suffices to show that
R_{G}(Q)\geq S_{G}(Q)
for all
Q
. Let
d
be the maximum size of a part of
Q
. Then

R_{G}(Q)\geq R_{G}(d,1,\dotsc,1)=\frac{n(n+1)}{2}-1-\frac{(n-d)(n-d+1)}{2}+\frac{d(d-1)}{2}=nd-1.

Recall the definitions of
Q_{d}
and
Q^{\prime}_{d}
from
[
23
, lem 12.4.3]
, which also gives that if we also have that
Q\neq Q_{d}
, then

S_{G}(Q)\leq S_{G}(Q^{\prime}_{d})=(n^{2}-1)\frac{d-1}{n-\lfloor n/d\rfloor+1}

using
[
23
, 12.4.4]
for the equality. By a computer check, this always gives
R_{G}(Q)\geq S_{G}(Q)
.

It remains to check the case when
Q=Q_{d}
. Let
r=\lfloor n/d\rfloor
and
q=n-rd
. Then

R_{G}(Q_{d})=\frac{n(n+1)}{2}-\frac{r(r+1)}{2}-\mathbf{1}_{q\neq 0}+\frac{rd(d-1)}{2}+\frac{q(q-1)}{2}

and by
[
23
, 12.4.4]
,

S_{G}(Q_{d})=(n^{2}-1)\frac{d-1}{n-\lfloor n/d\rfloor}.

By computer check again, we always have that
R_{G}(Q_{d})\geq S_{G}(Q_{d})
.
∎

Summarizing the final result:

Corollary 7.3.2
.

Let
G=U^{E,H}_{n}
be a definite unitary group and
K^{\prime}<G^{\infty}
be open compact. Pick infinite place
v_{0}
and define
f^{\epsilon,Z_{1}}_{v_{0}}
as in (
7.4
). Then the family

\mathcal{F}^{K^{\prime}}_{\epsilon,Z_{1}}(\pi):=m_{\pi}\frac{\|\Proj_{\pi_{v_{0}}}(f_{v_{0}}^{\epsilon,Z_{1}})\|_{2}^{2}}{\|f_{v_{0}}^{\epsilon,Z_{1}}\|_{2}^{2}}\mathbf{1}_{\pi_{\infty\setminus v_{0}}\text{ triv.}}\dim\left((\pi^{\infty})^{K^{\prime}}\right)

satisfies the density hypothesis
5.4.7
at finite place
v
in the following cases:

•

n=4
,

•

K^{\prime}_{v}
is hyperspecial,

•

v
is split,

•

Conjecture
6.3.4
holds for Arthur-type representations of
G_{v}
with a
K^{\prime}_{v}
-fixed vector.

Proof.

This follows from Theorem
7.3.1
together with Theorem
6.4.2
, Remark
6.4.3
and Corollary
6.3.6
.
∎

Remark 7.3.3
.

Note that the
\epsilon
safety factor in the exponents in the density hypothesis can be removed in Corollary
7.3.2
—i.e, the
\lesssim
of Definition
5.4.7
can be improved to an
\ll
.

Furthermore, the inequality (
7.8
) is only tight when when
\Box
corresponds to tempered representations or characters. Therefore, for
\sigma\neq 2,\infty
, we can tighten the exponents on the right-hand side of
7.3.1
by some small constant depending on
N_{G}
.

Corollary 7.3.4
.

Corollary
7.3.2
holds when
n=8

Proof.

For every restriction of a parameter
\psi_{v}
to the Arthur and Deligne-
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
’s, we by computer list out all the possible exponents of
\pi_{v}
satisfying the conditions of Theorem
6.3.3
. Bounding these potential
\sigma(\pi_{v})
by
6.2.3
, the only cases of Langlands data that violate the bound in Theorem
7.3.1
with
\sigma_{\Box}
replaced by
\sigma(\pi_{v})
are:

\displaystyle\pi_{v}\subseteq[3]\cdot||^{-1}\rtimes\pi_{\mathrm{temp}}\text{ in packet }\psi_{v}=[5][1]+[1][3],

(7.9)

\displaystyle\pi_{v}\subseteq[2]\cdot||^{-1}\times[2]\cdot||^{-1}\rtimes 0\text{ in packet }\psi_{v}=[4][1]+[1][4].

(7.10)

in the natural shorthand describing exponents and Arthur/Deligne-
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
-pieces. We show that both these cannot occur.

For the case (
7.9
), the infinitesimal character of
\pi_{v}
always has a factor of the form
\rho||^{0}
or
\rho||^{1/2}
coming from the choice of
2
-dimensional
\pi_{\mathrm{temp}}
. The
\rho||^{1/2}
cannot occur since the infinitesimal character of the packet has only integral powers of
||
and the
\rho||^{0}
cannot occur because the two zero powers of
||
in the infinitesimal character of the packet
\psi_{v}
are already accounted for by the
[3]\cdot||^{-1}
.

The case (
7.10
) cannot occur since it violates
[
67
, Thm 6.3]
—it corresponds to partition
(2,2,2,2)
while the packet restricted to the Deligne-
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
corresponds to partition
(4,1,1,1,1)
.
∎

8
Optimal Covering

Here we translate the spectral analysis of previously constructed gate sets in
PU(n)
to the settings of automorphic representation theory and show that the density Theorem
7.3.2
implies the optimal covering property. Theorem
1.2.2
from the introduction will then follow from the main result
8.3.3
of this section and Theorem
1.2.3
from the main result combined with Proposition
4.5.4
. Note that while Definition
1.2.1
was stated just for Lie groups for simplicity, it also applies to Lie groups mod discrete lattices.

We make a technical assumption that
\mathcal{O}_{F}
is Euclidean for the approximation property from
4.4.1
to hold.

Theorem 8.0.1
.

Let
G=U_{n}^{E/F,H}
be a definite unitary group and choose distinguished Archimedean place
v_{0}
of
F
. Let
K^{v_{0}}=K^{\prime}G_{\infty\setminus v_{0}}
for
K^{\prime}\leq G(\hat{\mathbb{Z}})
an almost golden adelic group that is almost golden at
\mathfrak{p}
(resp. almost
\tau
-super-golden for
\tau
traversable).

Recall the definition of the gate set
S_{\mathfrak{p}}
from
4.2.1
(resp.
{}^{0}\!S_{\mathfrak{p}}
and
C_{\mathfrak{p}}
from
4.2.4
and the discussion afterwards). Then
S_{\mathfrak{p}}\cup\Gamma
is a golden gate set (resp. the finite subgroup
C_{\mathfrak{p}}
and finite-order elements
{}^{0}\!S_{\mathfrak{p}}
form a super-golden gate set) of
G(F_{v_{0}})/\mathrm{center}=PU(n)
.

For convenience, assume
F=\mathbb{Q}
on the first read so
v_{0}
is the sole infinite place and
K^{\prime}\leq G(\widehat{\mathbb{Z}})
. Also assume on a first read that
K^{\prime}
is golden instead of just almost golden so that
\Gamma=1
.

8.1
Definition of Hecke Operators

We start by interpreting as a Hecke operator the operation of averaging over translates by the set
S_{p}^{[\ell]}
approximately of words in gates with minimum representation of length
\ell
(recall all precise definitions from Proposition
3.4.2
).

First, consider
K^{\prime}
an almost golden adelic group and define
\Gamma,\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}
as in §
4.1
. Then we get the following
identifications by lemma
4.1.2
:

L^{2}(\Gamma\backslash U(n))\cong L^{2}\left(\Lambda_{p}\backslash G(F_{v_{0}})\times G(F_{\mathfrak{p}})\right)^{K^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\\
\cong L^{2}(G(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A}^{\infty})\times G(F_{v_{0}}))^{K^{\prime}G_{\infty\setminus v_{0}}}\cong L^{2}\left(G(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A})\right)^{K^{\prime}G_{\infty\setminus v_{0}}}.

(8.1)

In particular, we can decompose

V=L^{2}(\Gamma\backslash U(n))=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\mathcal{AR}_{\mathrm{disc}}(G)\\
\pi_{\infty\setminus v_{0}}\text{ trivial}\end{subarray}}\pi_{v_{0}}\boxtimes(\pi^{\infty})^{K^{\prime}},

where the right-translation action corresponds to the action on the left factor of the
\boxtimes
as representations of
G(F_{v_{0}})=U(n)
. We also get corresponding subspaces
V_{\Box}
and restricted projections
\Proj_{\Box}:V\to V_{\Box}
.

Through the right factor of the
\boxtimes
, this decomposition also respects an action of Hecke operators:

Definition 8.1.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{p}
be a finite place of
F
and
\pi_{\mathfrak{p}}
a
G_{\mathfrak{p}}
-representation. Then any finite set
S\subset G_{\mathfrak{p}}
defines a Hecke operator
\mathbbm{1}_{K^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}SK^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\in C_{c}\left(K^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}\backslash G_{\mathfrak{p}}/K^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}\right)
, which acts on
\pi_{\mathfrak{p}}^{K^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}}
:

\mathbbm{1}_{K^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}SK^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}}.u:=\int_{g\in K^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}SK^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\pi_{v}(g).u\,dg=\sum_{s\in K^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}SK^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}/K^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\pi_{v}(s).u,

where the integral is normalized by
\vol(K^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}})=1
.

Define the gate set
S_{\mathfrak{p}}
to be as in Definition
3.4.1
or a non-standard/decimated variant as in Remark
3.4.4
/Definition
3.4.5
. Recall the definition of
S_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}
from Proposition
3.4.2
: when
K^{\prime}
is golden at
\mathfrak{p}
, this is simply the set of words in
S_{\mathfrak{p}}
of length precisely
\ell
in their shortest representation. As a technicality when
G_{\mathfrak{p}}
has non-anisotropic center, recall also from Proposition
3.4.2
their lifts
\widetilde{S}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}
.

Then, define the following operator
T_{S_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}}
:

\left(T_{S_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}}f\right)\left(g\right)=\frac{1}{|S_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}|}\sum\nolimits_{s\in\widetilde{S}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}}f\left(s^{-1}g\right)\qquad\left(f\in L^{2}(U(n))\;,\;g\in U(n)\right).

This can be interpreted as a Hecke operator on
V=L^{2}(\Gamma\backslash U(n))
:

Lemma 8.1.2
.

For any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
:

(1)

the inclusion
\Gamma\backslash\widetilde{S}_{p}^{[\ell]}\hookrightarrow\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}\backslash U(n)\times K^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}\widetilde{S}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}K^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}/K^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}
is a bijection (where
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}
is embedded diagonally in
U(n)\times G_{\mathfrak{p}}
and
K^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}\widetilde{S}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}K^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}\subset G_{\mathfrak{p}}
).

(2)

The operator
T_{S_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}}
descends to
L^{2}(\Gamma\backslash U(n))
where it is equal to the normalized Hecke operator
|\widetilde{S}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}|^{-1}\mathbbm{1}_{K^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}\widetilde{S}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}K^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}}
acting on the
G_{\mathfrak{p}}
component through the isomorphisms (
8.1
).

Proof.

First, (2) follows from (1): for descent, (1) in particular gives that
\Gamma\widetilde{S}_{p}^{[\ell]}=\widetilde{S}_{p}^{[\ell]}
so
(T_{S_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}}f)\left(g\right)=(T_{S_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}}f)\left(\gamma^{-1}g\right)
for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
. For the comparison to a Hecke operator, in the identification
L^{2}(U(n))=L^{2}(\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}\backslash U(n)\times G_{\mathfrak{p}})^{K^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}}
, the
s
-action on the
G_{\mathfrak{p}}
coordinate is equivalent to an
s^{-1}
action on the left on the
U(n)
coordinate. Note that each argument
s^{-1}g\in\Gamma\backslash U(n)
appears
|\Gamma|
times in the sum defining
T
.

Next, we prove (1). By Proposition
3.4.2
as used in Section
4.2
,
\widetilde{S}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}.v_{0}
is the set of all
v\in\mathcal{B}
(or lifts
v
of depending on
v-v_{0}\in X_{+}(\widehat{A}_{H})
and the choice of
\widetilde{\Sigma}
) such that
\|v-v_{0}\|=\ell
. In the super-golden case, we instead look at elements
g\tau
for
g\in G_{\mathfrak{p}}
such that
\|gv_{0}-v_{0}\|=\ell
(again possibly lifted depending on
gv_{0}-v_{0}\in X_{1+}(\widehat{A}_{H})
).

Since
K_{\mathfrak{p}}
preserves the
v-v_{0}\in X_{1+}(\bar{A}_{H})
, we get that
K^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}\widetilde{S}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}.v_{0}=\widetilde{S}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}.v_{0}
(resp.
K^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}\widetilde{S}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}.\tau=\widetilde{S}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}.\tau
), hence there is a bijection between
K^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}\widetilde{S}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}K^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}/K^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}
and
\widetilde{S}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}
, which in turns induces a bijection between
\Gamma\backslash\widetilde{S}_{p}^{[\ell]}
and
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}\backslash U(n)\times K^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}\widetilde{S}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{[\ell]}K^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}/K^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}
. This completes the proof.
∎

8.2
Bounds on Hecke Operators

The goal of this subsection is to provide the main upper bounds for the operator norm of Hecke operators acting on unitary irreducible representations in terms of their rate of decay of matrix coefficients.

Let us fix some notations.
Throughout this subsection we denote by
G=G_{\mathfrak{p}}
our
p
-adic group, by
\mathcal{B}
be the (reduced) Bruhat-Tits building of
G
, by
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{B}
a fixed fundamental apartment, by
C\in\mathcal{A}
a fixed fundamental chamber in it, by
I:=I_{\mathfrak{p}}
the Iwahori subgroup corresponding to
C
, and by
W
the size of the finite Weyl group of
G
. Then, the Hecke algebra for
I\backslash G_{\mathfrak{p}}/I
is the Iwahori-Weyl group studied in
[
38
,
73
]
.

We start with our key input bound on the operator norm of an Iwahori operator with a translation element.

Proposition 8.2.1
.

Let
a\in X_{1+}(A_{G})
and
n_{a}\in N_{G}(A_{G})
, as in the notation of §
3.3
be an element in the Iwahori-Weyl group which acts as a translation in a corresponding apartment.
For any
\sigma\geq 2
, if
\pi
is a unitary irreducible representation of
G
whose matrix coefficients are in
L^{\sigma+\epsilon}
mod center for all
\epsilon>0
, then

\|\mathbbm{1}_{In_{a}I}\mid_{\pi^{I}}\|_{op}\ll(\log|In_{a}I/I|)^{nW}\cdot|In_{a}I/I|^{\frac{\sigma-1}{\sigma}}.

Remark 8.2.2
.

We note that a slightly weaker bound can be deduced from the works of
[
57
,
48
]
, namely,
\|\mathbbm{1}_{In_{a}I}\mid_{\pi^{I}}\|_{op}\lesssim|In_{a}I/I|^{\frac{\sigma-1}{\sigma}}
.

Proof.

Let
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}\in X_{1+}(A_{G})
be a basis for the group
X_{1}(A_{G})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{n}
of translations and write
a=\sum_{i}m_{i}t_{i}
,
m_{i}\geq 0
.
If
\ell
is the Iwahori-Weyl length function then
\ell(n_{a})=\sum_{i}m_{i}\ell(n_{t_{i}})
, and by the Iwahori-Bruhat relations we get that
\mathbbm{1}_{In_{a}I}=(\mathbbm{1}_{In_{t_{1}}I})^{m_{1}}\circ\ldots\circ(\mathbbm{1}_{In_{t_{n}}I})^{m_{n}}
and
In_{a}I=In_{t_{1}}^{m_{1}}I\cdots In_{t_{n}}^{m_{n}}I=(In_{t_{1}}I)^{m_{1}}\cdots(In_{t_{n}}I)^{m_{n}}
. Furthermore,
|In_{a}I/I|=|In_{t_{1}}^{m_{1}}I/I|\times\cdots\times|In_{t_{n}}^{m_{n}}I/I|
(see, e.g,
[
19
, Lem 1.5.1]
). Therefore, without loss of generality, it suffices to compute a bound when
a=t^{m}
for
t=t_{1}
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

Write
T=\mathbbm{1}_{In_{t}I}
and
k=|In_{t}I/I|
.
Note that
\mathbbm{1}_{In_{a}I}=T^{m}
and
|In_{a}I/I|=k^{m}
.
By Proposition 4.5 and Theorem 5.6 of
[
57
]
, the Iwahori-operator
T
is
W
-normal and (since
a
is a translation) collision-free.
Let
\lambda=\sup\{|z|\,|\,z\in\mathrm{Spec}(T\mid_{\pi^{I}})\}
.
Then by Proposition 4.1 of
[
71
]
, we get
\|T^{m}\mid_{\pi^{I}}\|_{op}\ll m^{W}\lambda^{m}
.
It suffices to prove that
\lambda\leq k^{\frac{\sigma-1}{\sigma}}
.

This bound follows from a straightforward generalization of Proposition 2.3 of
[
57
]
:
If
T
is a
k
-branching collision-free operator
f
is a
L^{\sigma+\epsilon}

T
-eigenfunction with eigenvalue
\lambda
, then
\lambda\leq k^{\frac{\sigma-1}{\sigma}}
.
The proof of Proposition 2.3 of
[
57
]
works mutatis mutandis (i.e. replacing
2+\epsilon
with
\sigma+\epsilon
and
1+\epsilon
with
\sigma-1+\epsilon
).
∎

We wish to generalize Proposition
8.2.1
to cover more general compact open subgroups.
To do this we state and prove some auxiliary claims, and introduce the following notions of flat stabilizers and stable sets.

Definition 8.2.3
.

Let
P\leq G
be a compact open subgroup and
S\subset G
a finite set.

(1)

Say that
P
is a flat stabilizer, if it is the stabilizer in
G
of a finite collection of faces contained in a single apartment
X\subset\mathcal{A}
.

(2)

Say that
S
is a
P
-stable set, if
PSP=SP
.

(3)

Say that
S
is a translation set, if
S\subset\bigcup_{a\in X_{1+}(A_{G})}In_{a}I
(equivalently, the union can be taken over
X_{1}(A_{G})
).

Remark 8.2.4
.

The above conditions are not too hard to satisfy:

(1)

When the Kottowitz kernel is trivial (see
[
38
]
), then parahoric subgroups are the same as stabilizers of faces, hence they are flat stabilizers.

(2)

Since the Cartan norms are
K
-invariant, where
K
is a special maximal compact subgroup of
G
, then the sets
S^{[\ell]}
defined in Proposition
3.4.2
are
K
-stable.

(3)

By the Cartan decomposition, any
K
-stable set, where
K
is a special maximal compact subgroup of
G
, is a translation set.

Proposition 8.2.5
.

Let
P\leq G
be a flat stabilizer and
S\subset G
a finite
P
-stable set.
Then

|P\backslash PSP/P|\ll\left(\log|S|\right)^{d}.

Furthermore, assuming
|S\cap P|\ll 1
, we get

\left(\log|S|\right)^{-d}\cdot|S|\ll\max_{s\in S}|PsP/P|\ll|S|.

Proof.

Assume first that
X=\{\sigma_{0}\}
is a single face, hence
P
is a stabilizer of a face, and assume without loss of generality that
\sigma_{0}\subset C
, i.e.
I\subset P
.
Let
\ell(s)=\mathrm{dist}(s.\sigma_{0},\sigma_{0})
,
\ell(S)=\max_{s\in S}\ell(s)
and let
s_{m}\in S
such that
\ell(S)=\ell(s_{m})
.
Using the Bruhat decomposition, for any
g\in G
, denote by
w_{g}
the unique Iwahori-Weyl element such that
g\in Iw_{g}I
.
We note that
\ell(g)
is quasi-isometric to the Coxeter length of
w_{g}
which also equals
\log_{q}|Iw_{g}I/I|
.
Combining this with Lemma
8.2.6
, we get

|PgP/P|\asymp|IgI/I|\asymp q^{\ell(g)}.

Denote by
B_{\mathcal{B}}(r)=\{\sigma\in\mathcal{B}\,|\,\mathrm{dist}(\sigma,\sigma_{0})\leq r\}
and
B_{\mathcal{A}}(r)=\{\sigma\in\mathcal{A}\,|\,\mathrm{dist}(\sigma,\sigma_{0})\leq r\}
the balls of radius
r
around
\sigma_{0}
in the building and apartment, respectively (if
\sigma
is not of the same size as
\sigma_{0}
then we decree
\mathrm{dist}(\sigma,\sigma_{0})=\infty
).
Note that
P
acts on
B_{\mathcal{A}}(r)
and that
P.B_{\mathcal{A}}(r)=B_{\mathcal{B}}(r)
.
Also note that since
\mathcal{A}
is Euclidean, the size of its balls is approximately their radius to the power of the dimension.
Hence

|P\backslash B_{\mathcal{B}}(r)|\ll|B_{\mathcal{A}}(r)|\asymp r^{d}.

On the one hand, since
PSP=SP
, we get

q^{\ell(S)}=q^{\ell(s_{m})}\asymp|Ps_{m}P/P|\leq|PSP/P|=|SP/P|\leq|S|.

On the other hand, since
S.\sigma_{0}\subset B_{\mathcal{B}}(\ell(S))
, we get from the above estimates that

|P\backslash PSP/P|=|P\backslash PS.\sigma_{0}|\leq|P\backslash B_{\mathcal{B}}(\ell(S))|\asymp\ell(S)^{d}\ll(\log_{q}|S|)^{d}.

Next we consider a general finite collection of faces
X
.
Define a distance function on finite collection of
\mathcal{B}
, by
\mathrm{Dist}(Y,Z)=0
, if
Z
and
Y
are not in the same
G
-orbit, and otherwise
\mathrm{Dist}(Y,Z)=\min\{\|g\|_{0}\,|\,g\in G,\,g.Z=Y\}
, where
\|\cdot\|_{0}
is the Cartan norm (it could also be the modified Cartan, for the asymptotic argument here it will not matter).
Denote by
B^{D}_{\mathcal{B}}(Y,r)
and
B^{D}_{\mathcal{A}}(Y,r)
the balls around
Y
of radius
r
, w.r.t.
\mathrm{Dist}
, in
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{A}
, respectively.
Let
K
be a special maximal compact subgroup of
G
and without loss of generality assume that
P\subset K
and
I\subset K
: hence
K.\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{B}
.

Let
\Omega
be a finite set of representatives
K/P
and note that
\mathcal{B}=P.\left(\bigcup_{w\in\Omega}w.\mathcal{A}\right)
.
Denote
c=\max_{w\in\Omega}\mathrm{Dist}(X,w.X)
and
\ell=\max_{s\in S}\mathrm{Dist}(X,s.X)
.
Then
S.X\subset B^{D}_{\mathcal{B}}(X,r)
, and by the triangle inequality,
B^{D}_{\mathcal{B}}(X,\ell)\subset P.\left(\bigcup_{k_{i}\in\Omega}B^{D}_{k_{i}.\mathcal{A}}(k_{i}.X,\ell+c)\right)
.
From
P
-stability we get
PSP/P=SP/P\cong S.X
, and by arguing as before we get

|P\backslash PSP/P|=|P\backslash S.X|\leq|P\backslash B^{D}_{\mathcal{B}}(X,\ell)|\leq\sum_{w\in\Omega}|B^{D}_{w.\mathcal{A}}(w.X,\ell+c)|\ll\ell^{d}\asymp(\log|S|)^{d},

which completes the proof of the first identity.

For the second identity, first note that
PSP/P=SP/P\cong S/S\cap P
, and since
|S\cap P|\ll 1
, we get that
|S|\ll|PSP/P|\leq|S|
.
Hence
|PsP/P|\leq|PSP/P|\leq|S|
, for any
s\in S
, which gives the right inequality.
The left inequality follows from

|S|\ll|SP/P|=|PSP/P|=\sum_{PsP\in P\backslash PSP/P}|PsP/P|\leq|P\backslash PSP/P|\cdot\max_{s\in S}|PsP/P|

together with the first identity
|P\backslash PSP/P|\ll(\log|S|)^{d}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 8.2.6
.

Let
Q\leq P\leq G
be two fixed open compact subgroups, and let
\Omega\subset P
be a finite transversal set such that
P=\Omega Q=Q\Omega
.
Then for any
g\in G
,

|\Omega|\cdot|QgQ/Q|\leq|PgP/P|\leq|\Omega|^{3}\cdot|QgQ/Q|,

in particular
|QgQ/Q|\asymp|PgP/P|
, and for any
P
-spherical representation
V
of
G
,

\|\mathbbm{1}_{PgP}\mid_{V^{P}}\|_{op}\leq|\Omega|^{2}\max_{w,w^{\prime}\in\Omega}\|\mathbbm{1}_{Qwgw^{\prime}Q}\mid_{V^{P}}\|_{op}.

Proof.

Let
\mu
be a Haar measure on
G
, which is bi-invariant since
G
is a reductive
p
-adic group.
Note that
|X/H|=\frac{\mu(X)}{\mu(H)}
, for any compact open set
X\subset G
and compact open subgroup
H\leq G
.
Hence for the first claim it suffices to prove
\mu(QgQ)\leq\mu(PgP)\leq|\Omega|^{2}\cdot\mu(QgQ)
, which follows from the fact that
QgQ\subset PgP=\Omega QgQ\Omega
.

For the second claim we pick a transversal set
X\subset\Omega g\Omega
for the space of double cosets
Q\backslash PgP/Q
.
In particular,
PgP=\bigsqcup_{x\in X}QxQ
, hence
\mathbbm{1}_{PgP}=\sum_{x\in X}\mathbbm{1}_{QxQ}
.
Therefore

\|\mathbbm{1}_{PgP}\mid_{V^{P}}\|_{op}\leq\sum_{x\in X}\|\mathbbm{1}_{QxQ}\mid_{V^{P}}\|_{op}\leq|X|\max_{x\in X}\|\mathbbm{1}_{QxQ}\mid_{V^{P}}\|_{op}\leq|\Omega|^{2}\max_{w,w^{\prime}\in\Omega}\|\mathbbm{1}_{Qwgw^{\prime}Q}\mid_{V^{P}}\|_{op},

which completes the proof.
∎

Definition 8.2.7
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be the fundamental apartment,
C\in\mathcal{A}
the fundamental chamber, which is the convex hull in
\mathcal{A}
of
0
and
\lambda_{\alpha}/l_{\alpha}
, where
\alpha\in\Phi^{*}
, in the notation of Section
3.3
.
For
r\in\mathbb{N}
, denote by
\Delta_{r}
the convex hull in
\mathcal{A}
of
0
and
r\cdot\lambda_{\alpha}/l_{\alpha}
, call it the fundamental
r
-level truncated sector, and denote
Q_{r}=\mathrm{stab}_{G}(\Delta_{r})\leq I
, call it the
r
-deep Iwahori subgroup.
Note that
\Delta_{1}=C
and
I_{1}=I
.

Proposition 8.2.8
.

Let
Q=Q_{r}
be the
r
-deep Iwahori subgroup for some
r
.
For any
a\in X_{1+}(A_{G})
, the (well defined) map
Qn_{a}Q/Q\to In_{a}I/I
,
qn_{a}Q\mapsto qn_{a}I
, is a bijection.

Proof.

Let
I=I_{1}^{-}I_{0}^{0}I_{0}^{+}
be the Iwahori decomposition (see
[
19
]
Section 1.4).
Observe that
Q_{r}=I^{-}_{r}I_{0}^{0}I_{0}^{+}
is the
r
-deep Iwahori subgroup (note that
I_{0}^{0}I_{0}^{+}
is the stabilizer of the full sector
\Delta=\bigcup_{r}\Delta_{r}
).
Note that for any
a\in X_{1+}(A_{G})
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, then
n_{a}^{-1}I_{k}^{0}n_{a}=I_{k}^{0}
,
n_{a}^{-1}I_{k}^{+}n_{a}\supseteq I_{k}^{+}
and
n_{a}^{-1}I_{k}^{-}n_{a}\subseteq I_{k}^{-}
.
Therefore

n_{a}^{-1}Qn_{a}\cap I\subset Q\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad Qn_{a}I=In_{a}I.

Injectivity follows from
n_{a}^{-1}Qn_{a}\cap I\subset Q
, since for any
q_{1},q_{2}\in Q
such that
q_{1}n_{a}I=q_{2}n_{a}I
, then
q=n_{a}^{-1}q_{2}^{-1}q_{1}n_{a}\in n_{a}^{-1}Qn_{a}\cap I\subset Q
, hence
q_{1}n_{a}Q=q_{2}n_{a}Q
, and surjectivity clearly follows from
Qn_{a}I=In_{a}I
.
∎

Proposition 8.2.9
.

Let
Q=I_{r}
be the
r
-deep Iwahori subgroup for some
r
.
Let
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}\in X_{1+}(A_{G})
be a basis of the group
X_{1}(A_{G})
, for any
a\in X_{1+}(A_{G})
write it as
a=\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}t_{i}
,
m_{i}\geq 0
.
Then
\mathbbm{1}_{Qn_{a}Q}
